> **The Philosophical Move:** Truth vs. Desirability & The Donkey and the Roses. Kierkegaard warns that without God, existence is a bottomless void of despair. Camus accepts the challenge: if avoiding despair requires grazing like a donkey on the roses of illusion, the lucid mind chooses despair over lies.

---

### §18 • Original PDF Page 37
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 37):**  
> I am taking the liberty at this point of calling the existential attitude philosophical suicide. But this does not imply a judgment. It is a convenient way of indicating the movement by which a thought negates itself and tends to transcend itself in its very negation. For the existentials negation is their God. To be precise, that god is maintained only through the negation of human reason. But, like suicides, gods change with men. There are many ways of leaping, the essential being to leap. Those redeeming negations, those ultimate contradictions which negate the obstacle that has not yet been leaped over, may spring just as well (this is the paradox at which this reasoning aims) from a certain religious inspiration as from the rational order. They always lay claim to the eternal, and it is solely in this that they take the leap.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
At this juncture, I take the liberty of naming the existential strategy for what it is: intellectual surrender, or philosophical suicide. Yet this designation carries no mere moralistic condemnation. It is simply an accurate description of that intellectual maneuver by which thought abdicates its own function, attempting to transcend itself through self-annihilation. For the existentialists, self-negation becomes their deity. More precisely, that god survives exclusively upon the sacrifice of human reason. Yet just as methods of physical suicide vary, so the gods men invent change with their creators. There are countless techniques for executing the leap; the defining act is the leap itself. Those redemptive negations and ultimate paradoxes that dissolve the obstacle before it has been confronted can emerge just as easily from rationalist philosophy as from religious fervor—and this constitutes the central paradox of our inquiry. Both systems lay claim to the eternal, and it is in that very claim that the leap consists.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Naming 'Philosophical Suicide' (intellectual surrender). Camus formally coins the term: thought negating itself in order to transcend itself. Crucially, Camus reveals that the leap is not exclusively religious; rationalism can commit the identical suicide by deifying abstract concepts.

---

### §19 • Original PDF Page 38
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 38):**  
> It must be repeated that the reasoning developed in this essay leaves out altogether the most widespread spiritual attitude of our enlightened century: the one, based on the principle that all is reason, which aims to explain the world. It is natural to give a clear view of the world after accepting the idea that it must be clear. That is even legitimate, but does not concern the reasoning we are following out here. In fact, our aim is to shed light upon the step taken by the mind when, starting from a philosophy of the world’s lack of meaning, it ends up by finding a meaning and depth in it. The most touching of those steps is religious in essence; it becomes obvious in the theme of the irrational. But the most paradoxical and most significant is certainly the one that attributes rational reasons to a world it originally imagined as devoid of any guiding principle. It is impossible in any case to reach the consequences that concern us without having given an idea of this new attainment of the spirit of nostalgia.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
I must reiterate that the reasoning pursued in this essay deliberately bypasses the most prevalent intellectual stance of our enlightened era: the totalizing rationalism which assumes that everything is intelligible and aims to explain the universe completely. It is easy enough to present a coherent vision of reality if one begins with the dogmatic assumption that reality must be coherent. That classical approach is intellectually coherent on its own terms, but it has no bearing on our investigation. Our objective is to track the precise mental pivot whereby thought begins with the world's absolute meaninglessness, only to conclude by discovering transcendent meaning and profound purpose within it. The most poignant of these maneuvers is religious, exemplified by the cult of the irrational. But the most paradoxical and revealing maneuver is the rationalist one: attributing rational necessity to a cosmos initially recognized as utterly devoid of any guiding purpose. We cannot evaluate the final consequences of the Absurd without examining this sophisticated triumph of intellectual escapism.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The taxonomy of intellectual evasion. Having analyzed religious evasion (deifying the irrational via Jaspers, Chestov, and Kierkegaard), Camus now targets rationalist evasion: philosophies that begin by acknowledging chaos, but sneak cosmic order back in through the side door.

---

### §20 • Original PDF Page 38
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 38):**  
> I shall examine merely the theme of “the Intention” made fashionable by Husserl and the phenomenologists. I have already alluded to it. Originally Husserl’s method negates the classic procedure of the reason. Let me repeat. Thinking is not unifying or making the appearance familiar under the guise of a great principle. Thinking is learning all over again how to see, directing one’s consciousness, making of every image a privileged place. In other words, phenomenology declines to explain the world, it wants to be merely a description of actual experience. It confirms absurd thought in its initial assertion that there is no truth, but merely truths. From the evening breeze to this hand on my shoulder, everything has its truth. Consciousness illuminates it by paying attention to it. Consciousness does not form the object of its understanding, it merely focuses, it is the act of attention, and, to borrow a Bergsonian image, it resembles the projector that suddenly focuses on an image. The difference is that there is no scenario, but a successive and incoherent illustration. In that magic lantern all the pictures are privileged. Consciousness suspends in experience the objects of its attention. Through its miracle it isolates them. Henceforth they are beyond all judgments. This is the “intention” that characterizes consciousness. But the word does not imply any idea of finality; it is taken in its sense of “direction”: its only value is topographical.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
I will focus specifically on the concept of 'intentionality,' made prominent by Edmund Husserl and the phenomenologists. I have already touched upon it. In its initial formulation, Husserl's method directly challenges the classical operations of rationalism. Let me restate the principle: thinking does not mean synthesizing or forcing appearances under some grand metaphysical umbrella. To think is to learn anew how to see, to direct one's attention, to transform every perceptual experience into a privileged moment of awareness. In other words, phenomenology refuses to explain the universe; it aims solely to describe lived experience as it actually appears. It reinforces the absurd mind in its fundamental axiom: there is no universal Truth, only localized truths. From the cool evening breeze to this hand resting upon my shoulder, every single phenomenon possesses its own distinct reality. Consciousness does not invent reality; it illuminates it simply by attending to it. The mind does not construct the object of its knowledge; it focuses like a lens, acting as pure attention—resembling, in Henri Bergson's metaphor, a magic lantern spotlighting a slide. The difference is that there is no overarching screenplay, only an unfolding sequence of disparate, disconnected images. Inside that magic lantern, every projection holds equal dignity. Consciousness suspends its chosen objects within lived experience. Through this act of focus, it isolates them from the background. Henceforth, they stand beyond all reductive metaphysical judgments. This is the 'intentionality' that defines consciousness. The term carries no implication of cosmic purpose or ultimate teleology. It designates pure mental orientation: its function is entirely descriptive and topographical.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Phenomenology's initial promise. Husserl's concept of intentionality initially appears compatible with the Absurd: it rejects grand metaphysical unifications, replaces universal 'Truth' with localized 'truths', and restricts itself to describing lived experience (the magic lantern).

---

### §21 • Original PDF Page 39
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 39):**  
> At first sight, it certainly seems that in this way nothing contradicts the absurd spirit. That apparent modesty of thought that limits itself to describing what it declines to explain, that intentional discipline whence result paradoxically a profound enrichment of experience and the rebirth of the world in its prolixity are absurd procedures. At least at first sight. For methods of thought, in this case as elsewhere, always assume two aspects, one psychological and the other metaphysical. Thereby they harbor two truths. If the theme of the intentional claims to illustrate merely a psychological attitude, by which reality is drained instead of being explained, nothing in fact separates it from the absurd spirit. It aims to enumerate what it cannot transcend. It affirms solely that without any unifying principle thought can still take delight in describing and understanding every aspect of experience. The truth involved then for each of those aspects is psychological in nature. It simply testifies to the “interest” that reality can offer. It is a way of awaking a sleeping world and of making it vivid to the mind. But if one attempts to extend and give a rational basis to that notion of truth, if one claims to discover in this way the “essence” of each object of knowledge, one restores its depth to experience. For an absurd mind that is incomprehensible. Now, it is this wavering between modesty and assurance that is noticeable in the intentional attitude, and this shimmering of phenomenological thought will illustrate the absurd reasoning better than anything else. For Husserl speaks likewise of “extra-temporal essences” brought to light by the intention, and he sounds like Plato. All things are not explained by one thing but by all things. I see no difference. To be sure, those ideas or those essences that consciousness “effectuates” at the end of every description are not yet to be considered perfect models. But it is asserted that they are directly present in each datum of perception. There is no longer a single idea explaining everything, but an infinite number of essences giving a meaning to an infinite number of objects. The world comes to a stop, but also lights up. Platonic realism becomes intuitive, but it is still realism. Kierkegaard was swallowed up in his God; Parmenides plunged thought into the One. But here thought hurls itself into an abstract polytheism. But this is not all: hallucinations and fictions likewise belong to “extra-temporal essences.” In the new world of ideas, the species of centaurs collaborates with the more modest species of metropolitan man.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
At first glance, nothing in this methodology seems to contradict the spirit of the Absurd. That apparent intellectual humility—content to describe what it refuses to explain—and that disciplined focus which paradoxically restores the rich, kaleidoscopic variety of the physical world are authentic absurd strategies. At least, so it seems on the surface. For philosophical methods always possess two dimensions: an empirical psychological side and an underlying metaphysical side. Consequently, they harbor two competing truths. If intentionality merely represents an empirical attitude—exhausting the richness of reality instead of explaining it away—nothing separates it from the absurd stance. It simply catalogues what it cannot transcend. It demonstrates that even without a grand unifying principle, the human mind can still take joy in observing and clarifying every facet of experience. The truth belonging to each phenomenon remains strictly psychological. It simply bears witness to the fascinating texture that reality presents to our senses. It awakens a somnolent world, making it burst into vivid life before the mind. But the moment one attempts to universalize that insight—claiming to extract an eternal 'essence' from every perceived object—one smuggles metaphysical depth back into existence. To the lucid absurd mind, that move is completely unjustifiable. This perpetual oscillation between empirical modesty and dogmatic certainty defines phenomenology, and this intellectual double-dealing provides the clearest possible case study in philosophical suicide. For when Husserl begins speaking of 'extra-temporal essences' uncovered by intentionality, he sounds indistinguishable from Plato. Whether reality is explained by a single supreme Form or by an infinity of Forms makes zero practical difference. The metaphysical trap is identical. Admittedly, the essences that consciousness isolates through description are not presented as transcendent heavenly archetypes. Yet Husserl claims they are eternally present within every single perceptual datum. Instead of one monistic Idea explaining reality, we are given an infinite pantheon of eternal essences conferring meaning upon an infinite catalog of objects. The physical world is arrested in freeze-frame, bathed in artificial metaphysical light. Platonic realism has been made intuitive, but it remains Platonic realism. Kierkegaard drowned consciousness in his God; Parmenides dissolved it into the cosmic One. Husserl hurls thought into an abstract polytheism. Worse still, hallucinations, dreams, and imaginary creatures are granted their own 'extra-temporal essences.' In this bizarre intellectual heaven, the mythical species of centaurs enjoys equal metaphysical standing with the ordinary office worker.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Polytheism of Essences: Husserl as Inverted Plato. Phenomenology begins modestly by describing appearances, but then commits a rationalist leap by declaring every appearance an 'extra-temporal essence.' Husserl creates an abstract pantheon where centaurs and office workers share eternal Platonic reality.

---

### §22 • Original PDF Page 40
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 40):**  
> For the absurd man, there was a truth as well as a bitterness in that purely psychological opinion that all aspects of the world are privileged. To say that everything is privileged is tantamount to saying that everything is equivalent. But the metaphysical aspect of that truth is so far-reaching that through an elementary reaction he feels closer perhaps to Plato. He is taught, in fact, that every image presupposes an equally privileged essence. In this ideal world without hierarchy, the formal army is composed solely of generals. To be sure, transcendency had been eliminated. But a sudden shift in thought brings back into the world a sort of fragmentary immanence which restores to the universe its depth.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
For the absurd mind, there was both profound truth and bitter irony in the psychological insight that all phenomena are equally privileged. To affirm that every experience is privileged is simply another way of saying that all experiences are metaphysically equivalent. Yet Husserl's metaphysical inflation of that insight is so extravagant that it involuntarily drives the thinker back into the arms of Plato. We are informed that behind every fleeting image lies an equally privileged, eternal essence. In this ideal realm stripped of all hierarchy, the army consists exclusively of four-star generals. Classical theological transcendence had supposedly been expelled from the house. Yet by a sudden metaphysical sleight of hand, Husserl reintroduces a fragmented immanence that injects illusory cosmic depth back into the universe.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Army of Generals. If every mundane perception contains an eternal essence, all hierarchy vanishes: the army consists entirely of generals. Husserl claims to eliminate transcendence, but merely fragments it into millions of miniature Platonic idols.

---

### §23 • Original PDF Page 40
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 40):**  
> Am I to fear having carried too far a theme handled with greater circumspection by its creators? I read merely these assertions of Husserl, apparently paradoxical yet rigorously logical if what precedes is accepted: “That which is true is true absolutely, in itself; truth is one, identical with itself, however different the creatures who perceive it, men, monsters, angels or gods.” Reason triumphs and trumpets forth with that voice, I cannot deny. What can its assertions mean in the absurd world? The perception of an angel or a god has no meaning for me. That geometrical spot where divine reason ratifies mine will always be incomprehensible to me. There, too, I discern a leap, and though performed in the abstract, it nonetheless means for me forgetting just what I do not want to forget. When farther on Husserl exclaims: “If all masses subject to attraction were to disappear, the law of attraction would not be destroyed but would simply remain without any possible application,” I know that I am faced with a metaphysic of consolation. And if I want to discover the point where thought leaves the path of evidence, I have only to reread the parallel reasoning that Husserl voices regarding the mind: “If we could contemplate clearly the exact laws of psychic processes, they would be seen to be likewise eternal and invariable, like the basic laws of theoretical natural science. Hence they would be valid even if there were no psychic process.” Even if the mind were not, its laws would be! I see then that of a psychological truth Husserl aims to make a rational rule: after having denied the integrating power of human reason, he leaps by this expedient to eternal Reason.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Must I worry that I have pushed a philosophical theme further than its authors intended? I simply read Husserl's own words—paradoxical at first glance, yet strictly logical if one accepts his initial premises: 'Whatever is true is true absolutely, in itself; truth is unified and identical with itself, regardless of whether those who perceive it are men, monsters, angels, or gods.' Through that pronouncement, I readily acknowledge, human reason triumphs and proclaims its dominion. Yet what significance can such sweeping claims hold within the world of the Absurd? The hypothetical perceptions of an angel or a deity are meaningless to a mortal mind. That metaphysical coordinate where divine intellect allegedly ratifies human reason will remain forever beyond my comprehension. Here, too, I detect a leap—and though executed in the realm of pure abstraction, it still demands that I forget the very reality I refuse to abandon. When Husserl goes on to declare: 'If all physical matter subject to gravitation were to vanish, the law of gravity would not be destroyed, but would simply persist without physical application,' I know I am confronting a philosophy of emotional consolation. And if I seek the exact juncture where thought abandons empirical evidence, I need only examine Husserl's identical claim regarding human consciousness: 'If we could clearly perceive the exact laws governing psychic processes, we would recognize them as eternal and invariable, like the fundamental laws of theoretical physics. They would therefore remain fully valid even if no conscious mind existed.' Even if consciousness did not exist, the laws of consciousness would still endure! In this way, Husserl converts a psychological observation into a cosmic metaphysical law: having rejected the unifying power of human reason, he uses this conceptual trick to leap directly into the lap of eternal Reason.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Husserl's Platonic Leap into 'Eternal Reason'. Husserl claims that even if all matter and conscious minds vanished, the abstract laws of gravity and psychology would remain eternally true. Camus unmasks this as rationalist metaphysics of consolation: an abstract leap that deifies human concepts.

---

### §24 • Original PDF Page 41
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 41):**  
> Husserl’s theme of the “concrete universe” cannot then surprise me. If I am told that all essences are not formal but that some are material, that the first are the object of logic and the second of science, this is merely a question of definition. The abstract, I am told, indicates but a part, without consistency in itself, of a concrete universal. But the wavering already noted allows me to throw light on the confusion of these terms. For that may mean that the concrete object of my attention, this sky, the reflection of that water on this coat, alone preserve the prestige of the real that my interest isolates in the world. And I shall not deny it. But that may mean also that this coat itself is universal, has its particular and sufficient essence, belongs to the world of forms. I then realize that merely the order of the procession has been changed. This world has ceased to have its reflection in a higher universe, but the heaven of forms is figured in the host of images of this earth. This changes nothing for me. Rather than encountering here a taste for the concrete, the meaning of the human condition, I find an intellectualism sufficiently unbridled to generalize the concrete itself.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Husserl's doctrine of the 'concrete universe' comes as no surprise. When we are told that some essences are abstract while others are material—the former belonging to formal logic, the latter to empirical science—we are dealing merely with academic taxonomy. The abstract, we are assured, represents merely an incomplete fragment of a larger concrete universal. Yet the conceptual ambiguity we noted earlier allows us to untangle the confusion beneath these terms. On one hand, this could mean that the concrete object before me—this patch of sky, the shimmering reflection of water upon this wool coat—retains the sole genuine reality that my attention carves out from the world. And to that claim, I have no objection. But on the other hand, it could mean that this coat is itself an eternal universal, possessing an autonomous essence that participates in the Platonic realm of Forms. I then realize that Husserl has merely reversed the direction of the metaphysical procession. The physical earth no longer reflects a higher transcendent heaven; instead, the heaven of Platonic Forms has been crammed into the mundane objects of this world. For the absurd mind, this changes absolutely nothing. Instead of finding a genuine reverence for the concrete texture of human existence, I encounter an unchecked intellectualism that universalizes and abstracts the concrete itself.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Critique of the 'Concrete Universal': Inverted Platonism. Husserl claims to celebrate the concrete world, but he does so by elevating everyday physical objects (a coat, a patch of sky) into eternal Platonic essences. Concrete reality is not lived; it is intellectualized and abstracted.

---

### §25 • Original PDF Page 41
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 41):**  
> It is futile to be amazed by the apparent paradox that leads thought to its own negation by the opposite paths of humiliated reason and triumphal reason. From the abstract god of Husserl to the dazzling god of Kierkegaard the distance is not so great. Reason and the irrational lead to the same preaching. In truth the way matters but little; the will to arrive suffices. The abstract philosopher and the religious philosopher start out from the same disorder and support each other in the same anxiety. But the essential is to explain. Nostalgia is stronger here than knowledge. It is significant that the thought of the epoch is at once one of the most deeply imbued with a philosophy of the non-significance of the world and one of the most divided in its conclusions. It is constantly oscillating between extreme rationalization of reality which tends to break up that thought into standard reasons and its extreme irrationalization which tends to deify it. But this divorce is only apparent. It is a matter of reconciliation, and, in both cases, the leap suffices. It is always wrongly thought that the notion of reason is a one-way notion. To tell the truth, however rigorous it may be in its ambition, this concept is nonetheless just as unstable as others. Reason bears a quite human aspect, but it also is able to turn toward the divine. Since Plotinus, who was the first to reconcile it with the eternal climate, it has learned to turn away from the most cherished of its principles, which is contradiction, in order to integrate into it the strangest, the quite magic one of participation. It is an instrument of thought and not thought itself. Above all, a man’s thought is his nostalgia.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
There is no reason to be astonished by the apparent paradox that drives thought to its own destruction through two opposite avenues: humiliated reason and triumphant reason. From Husserl's abstract deity to Kierkegaard's blinding God, the distance is remarkably short. Dogmatic rationalism and religious irrationalism arrive at the exact same sermon. In truth, the path chosen hardly matters; the desperate urge to arrive at a comforting conclusion is all that counts. The abstract metaphysician and the religious existentialist set out from the identical existential rupture, sustained by the same underlying panic. For both, the absolute imperative is to find an ultimate explanation. In both camps, existential nostalgia proves far stronger than lucid intellect. It is deeply revealing that modern philosophy is simultaneously saturated with the meaninglessness of the cosmos and bitterly divided in its ultimate conclusions. It swings continuously between the radical rationalization of reality (which atomizes the world into eternal concepts) and its radical irrationalization (which turns cosmic silence into a god). Yet that philosophical divide is purely cosmetic. Both sides seek reconciliation at any cost—and in both cases, the leap of faith accomplishes the trick. We mistakenly assume that reason functions as a rigid, one-way mechanism. In truth, no matter how rigorous its pretensions, human reason is just as malleable and volatile as any other mental faculty. Reason possesses an earthy, human dimension, yet it can just as easily be bent toward the divine. Ever since Plotinus first married reason to the eternal, intellect has learned to abandon its most essential anchor—the law of non-contradiction—in order to embrace the mystical, quasi-magical doctrine of participation in the One. Reason is merely an instrument of consciousness, not consciousness itself. And above all else, human thought is driven by its homesickness for unity.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Convergence of Extremes: Plotinus, Husserl, and Kierkegaard. Triumphant reason (Husserl's eternal essences) and humiliated reason (Kierkegaard's divine irrationality) are twin escape routes born of the identical existential panic. Both sacrifice intellectual honesty to satisfy humanity's ancient nostalgia for unity.

---

### §26 • Original PDF Page 42
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 42):**  
> Just as reason was able to soothe the melancholy of Plotinus, it provides modern anguish the means of calming itself in the familiar setting of the eternal. The absurd mind has less luck. For it the world is neither so rational nor so irrational. It is unreasonable and only that. With Husserl the reason eventually has no limits at all. The absurd, on the contrary, establishes its limits since it is powerless to calm its anguish. Kierkegaard independently asserts that a single limit is enough to negate that anguish. But the absurd does not go so far. For it that limit is directed solely at the reason’s ambitions. The theme of the irrational, as it is conceived by the existentials, is reason becoming confused and escaping by negating itself. The absurd is lucid reason noting its limits.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Just as metaphysical reason consoled the melancholy of Plotinus, it offers contemporary anxiety a familiar refuge within the comfort of the eternal. The absurd mind enjoys no such luxury. For the lucid thinker, the world is neither completely rational nor wholly irrational. It is simply unreasonable—and nothing more. In Husserl's hands, reason ends up acknowledging no boundaries whatever. The absurd, by contrast, establishes definite boundaries precisely because it cannot soothe human anguish. Kierkegaard claims on his own terms that a single encounter with the boundary is enough to abolish that anguish through faith. But the absurd refuses to make that leap. For the absurd mind, the boundary applies exclusively to the overreaching ambitions of human intellect. The concept of the irrational, as brandished by the existentialists, is merely reason panicking, losing its nerve, and escaping through self-annihilation. The Absurd, in stark contrast, is lucid reason dispassionately mapping its own limits.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Definitive Distinction: Panic vs. Radical Clarity. Existential irrationalism is reason becoming confused and killing itself in terror. The Absurd, by contrast, is lucid reason calmly establishing its own boundaries without fleeing into faith or despair.

---

### §27 • Original PDF Page 42
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 42):**  
> Only at the end of this difficult path does the absurd man recognize his true motives. Upon comparing his inner exigence and what is then offered him, he suddenly feels he is going to turn away. In the universe of Husserl the world becomes clear and that longing for familiarity that man’s heart harbors becomes useless. In Kierkegaard’s apocalypse that desire for clarity must be given up if it wants to be satisfied. Sin is not so much knowing (if it were, everybody would be innocent) as wanting to know. Indeed, it is the only sin of which the absurd man can feel that it constitutes both his guilt and his innocence. He is offered a solution in which all the past contradictions have become merely polemical games. But this is not the way he experienced them. Their truth must be preserved, which consists in not being satisfied. He does not want preaching.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Only at the conclusion of this demanding path does the absurd man recognize his genuine motives. Comparing his unyielding demand for clarity with what these philosophies offer him, he instinctively prepares to turn away. In Husserl's philosophical universe, the world is rendered transparent, rendering human longing for intimacy entirely superfluous. In Kierkegaard's religious vision, the hunger for clarity must be surrendered if it hopes to find eternal satisfaction. In this theology, sin consists not in knowing (for if that were true, all humanity would be innocent), but in the very desire to know. Indeed, that relentless desire to know is the only 'sin' in which the absurd man recognizes both his tragic guilt and his ultimate innocence. He is offered an intellectual settlement in which all his lived agonies are reduced to clever rhetorical parlor games. But that academic compromise bears no resemblance to how he actually experienced them. The authenticity of those contradictions must be preserved—and that authenticity consists precisely in refusing to be satisfied. He has no use for sermons.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Refusal of Preaching. Comparing the absurd demand with the offers of Husserl and Kierkegaard, Camus rejects both. The absurd man's sole 'sin' is wanting to know—a hunger he refuses to trade for theological consolations or phenomenological word games.

---

### §28 • Original PDF Page 43
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 43):**  
> My reasoning wants to be faithful to the evidence that aroused it. That evidence is the absurd. It is that divorce between the mind that desires and the world that disappoints, my nostalgia for unity, this fragmented universe and the contradiction that binds them together. Kierkegaard suppresses my nostalgia and Husserl gathers together that universe. That is not what I was expecting. It was a matter of living and thinking with those dislocations, of knowing whether one had to accept or refuse. There can be no question of masking the evidence, of suppressing the absurd by denying one of the terms of its equation. It is essential to know whether one can live with it or whether, on the other hand, logic commands one to die of it. I am not interested in philosophical suicide, but rather in plain suicide. I merely wish to purge it of its emotional content and know its logic and its integrity. Any other position implies for the absurd mind deceit and the mind’s retreat before what the mind itself has brought to light. Husserl claims to obey the desire to escape “the inveterate habit of living and thinking in certain well-known and convenient conditions of existence,” but the final leap restores in him the eternal and its comfort. The leap does not represent an extreme danger as Kierkegaard would like it to do. The danger, on the contrary, lies in the subtle instant that precedes the leap. Being able to remain on that dizzying crest—that is integrity and the rest is subterfuge. I know also that never has helplessness inspired such striking harmonies as those of Kierkegaard. But if helplessness has its place in the indifferent landscapes of history, it has none in a reasoning whose exigence is now known.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
My reasoning insists on remaining radically faithful to the initial evidence that provoked it. That evidence is the Absurd. It is that irremediable divorce between a mind that hungers for meaning and a universe that disappoints it—the collision between my longing for unity, this shattered world, and the unyielding contradiction binding them together. Kierkegaard suffocates my longing, while Husserl artificially reassembles the universe. That is not what I was seeking. The task was to learn how to live and think within these profound fractures—to determine whether one must accept the condition or reject it. There can be no question of obscuring the evidence, or of erasing the absurd by eliminating one of the terms in its equation. The vital question is whether one can live with this tension, or whether, on the contrary, rigorous logic commands one to die of it. I have zero interest in philosophical suicide; my sole concern is with literal suicide. I simply want to strip the act of its emotional sentimentality, discovering its underlying logic and philosophical integrity. Any other posture represents intellectual cowardice—a shameful retreat by the mind before the very realities it has uncovered. Husserl claims to break free from 'the ingrained habit of living and thinking within conventional, comfortable conditions of existence'—yet his final leap restores the tranquil comfort of eternity. Nor does the leap constitute an act of heroic daring, as Kierkegaard so desperately wanted to believe. The true danger, on the contrary, resides in that razor-thin instant that immediately precedes the leap. Possessing the strength to maintain one's balance upon that dizzying ridge—that alone is intellectual integrity; everything else is evasion. I know, too, that human helplessness has never inspired such haunting, magnificent prose as that of Kierkegaard. Yet while tragic helplessness may claim its rightful place across the indifferent expanse of human history, it has no place in an argument whose rigorous demands are now clearly understood.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Dizzying Ridge (Camus's Manifesto of Integrity). Refusing both Kierkegaard's leap and Husserl's Platonism, Camus stands on the narrow ledge before the leap. True courage is not leaping into faith, but holding one's ground on the dizzying crest of the Absurd without hope and without surrender.

---

## 4. Absurd Freedom (pp. 44–54)
*Part I: An Absurd Reasoning*

### §1 • Original PDF Page 44
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 44):**  
> Now the main thing is done, I hold certain facts from which I cannot separate. What I know, what is certain, what I cannot deny, what I cannot reject—this is what counts. I can negate everything of that part of me that lives on vague nostalgias, except this desire for unity, this longing to solve, this need for clarity and cohesion. I can refute everything in this world surrounding me that offends or enraptures me, except this chaos, this sovereign chance and this divine equivalence which springs from anarchy. I don’t know whether this world has a meaning that transcends it. But I know that I do not know that meaning and that it is impossible for me just now to know it. What can a meaning outside my condition mean to me? I can understand only in human terms. What I touch, what resists me—that is what I understand. And these two certainties—my appetite for the absolute and for unity and the impossibility of reducing this world to a rational and reasonable principle—I also know that I cannot reconcile them. What other truth can I admit without lying, without bringing in a hope I lack and which means nothing within the limits of my condition?

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The primary work is finished; I now possess several undeniable facts from which I cannot separate myself. What I know, what is certain, what I can neither deny nor discard—this alone matters. I can dismiss every part of myself that feeds on vague nostalgia, except for this persistent hunger for unity, this drive to resolve, this demand for clarity and coherence. I can reject everything in this surrounding world that offends or delights me, except for this prevailing chaos, this sovereign randomness, and this cosmic indifference born of pure anarchy. Whether this world possesses a transcendent meaning beyond itself, I do not know. I only know that I am ignorant of such meaning, and that within my current existence it is impossible for me to grasp it. What could a meaning that lies entirely outside my human condition possibly signify to me? I can comprehend reality only in human terms. What my hands touch, what pushes back against me—that is what I understand. And between these two foundational certainties—my appetite for absolute unity and the impossibility of reducing this world to a rational principle—I know there can be no reconciliation. What other truth could I accept without lying, without introducing an unfounded hope that means nothing within the boundaries of my mortal condition?

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Camus consolidates his epistemological baseline: the twin irreducible poles of the Absurd. Human consciousness demands absolute unity and rational coherence, while the empirical universe presents only silence, randomness, and resistance. Rejecting any metaphysical leap to bridge this gap, he insists on honesty over consoling fictions.

---

### §2 • Original PDF Page 44
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 44):**  
> If I were a tree among trees, a cat among animals, this life would have a meaning, or rather this problem would not arise, for I should belong to this world. I should be this world to which I am now opposed by my whole consciousness and my whole insistence upon familiarity. This ridiculous reason is what sets me in opposition to all creation. I cannot cross it out with a stroke of the pen. What I believe to be true I must therefore preserve. What seems to me so obvious, even against me, I must support. And what constitutes the basis of that conflict, of that break between the world and my mind, but the awareness of it? If therefore I want to preserve it, I can through a constant awareness, ever revived, ever alert. This is what, for the moment, I must remember. At this moment the absurd, so obvious and yet so hard to win, returns to a man’s life and finds its home there. At this moment, too, the mind can leave the arid, dried-up path of lucid effort. That path now emerges in daily life. It encounters the world of the anonymous impersonal pronoun “one,” but henceforth man enters in with his revolt and his lucidity. He has forgotten how to hope. This hell of the present is his Kingdom at last. All problems recover their sharp edge. Abstract evidence retreats before the poetry of forms and colors. Spiritual conflicts become embodied and return to the abject and magnificent shelter of man’s heart. None of them is settled. But all are transfigured. Is one going to die, escape by the leap, rebuild a mansion of ideas and forms to one’s own scale? Is one, on the contrary, going to take up the heart-rending and marvelous wager of the absurd? Let’s make a final effort in this regard and draw all our conclusions. The body, affection, creation, action, human nobility will then resume their places in this mad world. At last man will again find there the wine of the absurd and the bread of indifference on which he feeds his greatness.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Were I a tree among trees or an animal among beasts, this life would possess inherent meaning—or rather, the problem would never arise, because I would belong entirely to this world. I would be an indivisible part of the very universe to which my conscious mind and my hunger for familiarity now violently oppose me. It is this absurd faculty of reason that pits me against the whole of creation. I cannot erase it with the stroke of a pen. What I perceive to be true, I must therefore preserve. What appears undeniable, even when it works against me, I must uphold. And what sustains this conflict, this fracture between the world and my intellect, except my active awareness of it? If I wish to preserve this tension, I can do so only through an unyielding, wakeful consciousness that is constantly renewed. This is the principle I must keep firmly in mind. At this juncture, the absurd—at once glaringly evident and yet so difficult to attain—reenters human life and makes its home there. Here, too, the intellect can depart from the dry, abstract desert of mere logical exercise. That path now opens out into the concrete reality of everyday life. It encounters the anonymous world of the crowd, yet from now on a man enters that world armed with his conscious defiance and his radical clarity. He has forgotten how to indulge in hope. This purgatory of the immediate present has become his kingdom. Every dilemma regains its razor-sharp intensity. Bloodless abstractions give way to the vibrant poetry of physical shapes and colors. Metaphysical struggles take on flesh, returning to that wretched yet magnificent sanctuary: the human heart. Not a single conflict is resolved. Yet every one of them is transfigured. Shall we choose death, escape through an intellectual leap of faith, or construct an artificial palace of theories tailored to our own comfort? Or shall we, on the contrary, take up the agonizing and magnificent wager of the absurd? Let us make one final effort to follow this reasoning through and draw all its consequences. The physical body, human affection, artistic creation, decisive action, and human dignity will then reclaim their rightful places in this irrational world. At last, man will rediscover the wine of the absurd and the bread of indifference upon which he nourishes his greatness.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Tree and Cat thought experiment illustrates human exile from nature. Consciousness separates humanity from unthinking immersion in the cosmos. Rather than remaining an abstract exercise, the absurd descends into daily life, transforming the immediate present into man's kingdom, where no tension is dissolved but all experience is illuminated.

---

### §3 • Original PDF Page 45
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 45):**  
> Let us insist again on the method: it is a matter of persisting. At a certain point on his path the absurd man is tempted. History is not lacking in either religions or prophets, even without gods. He is asked to leap. All he can reply is that he doesn’t fully understand, that it is not obvious. Indeed, he does not want to do anything but what he fully understands. He is assured that this is the sin of pride, but he does not understand the notion of sin; that perhaps hell is in store, but he has not enough imagination to visualize that strange future; that he is losing immortal life, but that seems to him an idle consideration. An attempt is made to get him to admit his guilt. He feels innocent. To tell the truth, that is all he feels—his irreparable innocence. This is what allows him everything. Hence, what he demands of himself is to live solely with what he knows, to accommodate himself to what is, and to bring in nothing that is not certain. He is told that nothing is. But this at least is a certainty. And it is with this that he is concerned: he wants to find out if it is possible to live without appeal.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Let us emphasize the method once more: everything depends upon persistence. At a certain stage of his journey, the absurd man faces severe temptation. History offers an abundance of religions and self-styled prophets, even secular ones without gods. They all urge him to take the leap. His only reply is that he does not fully grasp what they offer, that it lacks self-evident truth. Indeed, he refuses to commit to anything he does not clearly understand. They warn him that this refusal is the sin of intellectual pride, yet the concept of sin is meaningless to him; they threaten that damnation awaits, but he lacks the credulity to picture such a bizarre future; they caution that he forfeits eternal life, but that strikes him as an idle distraction. They demand that he confess his guilt. Instead, he feels entirely innocent. In truth, that is all he experiences: his irremediable, radical innocence. And it is this innocence that grants him complete freedom. Consequently, what he requires of himself is to live exclusively by what he knows, to adapt himself to what actually exists, and to introduce nothing that lacks certainty. He is told that nothing is certain. Yet that absence of certainty is itself a definitive fact. This is his sole preoccupation: he wants to discover whether it is possible to live without appeal to any higher authority.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Living 'without appeal' (vivre sans appel). Confronted with religious and secular calls for a leap of faith, the absurd man claims 'irreparable innocence.' Denying the dogmatic categories of original sin and transcendent guilt, he refuses any palliative that cannot be verified by immediate human experience.

---

### §4 • Original PDF Page 46
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 46):**  
> Now I can broach the notion of suicide. It has already been felt what solution might be given. At this point the problem is reversed. It was previously a question of finding out whether or not life had to have a meaning to be lived. It now becomes clear, on the contrary, that it will be lived all the better if it has no meaning. Living an experience, a particular fate, is accepting it fully. Now, no one will live this fate, knowing it to be absurd, unless he does everything to keep before him that absurd brought to light by consciousness. Negating one of the terms of the opposition on which he lives amounts to escaping it. To abolish conscious revolt is to elude the problem. The theme of permanent revolution is thus carried into individual experience. Living is keeping the absurd alive. Keeping it alive is, above all, contemplating it. Unlike Eurydice, the absurd dies only when we turn away from it. One of the only coherent philosophical positions is thus revolt. It is a constant confrontation between man and his own obscurity. It is an insistence upon an impossible transparency. It challenges the world anew every second. Just as danger provided man the unique opportunity of seizing awareness, so metaphysical revolt extends awareness to the whole of experience. It is that constant presence of man in his own eyes. It is not aspiration, for it is devoid of hope. That revolt is the certainty of a crushing fate, without the resignation that ought to accompany it.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
I can now address the question of suicide directly. The direction of the answer has already become apparent. At this critical juncture, the terms of the problem are reversed. Earlier, the question was whether life required an overarching purpose in order to be worth living. Now it becomes evident, on the contrary, that life will be lived far more fully precisely because it has no ultimate meaning. To undergo an experience, to inhabit a particular destiny, means accepting it without reservation. Yet no one can truly inhabit this fate, knowing it to be absurd, unless he makes every effort to keep that absurdity constantly visible before his conscious mind. To destroy either term of the tension that sustains him is simply an attempt to run away. To extinguish conscious defiance is to evade the fundamental problem. The ideal of permanent revolution is thereby translated into individual existence. To live is to keep the absurd alive. And to keep the absurd alive is, above all else, to look it directly in the face. Unlike Eurydice, the absurd perishes only when we avert our eyes. Thus, one of the few genuinely coherent philosophical stances is defiant revolt. It is an unending confrontation between man and his own inner darkness. It is a persistent demand for an impossible transparency. It interrogates the universe afresh at every passing second. Just as imminent physical peril offers a person a rare flash of heightened consciousness, so metaphysical defiance extends that lucidity across the whole breadth of experience. It is humanity standing continually face to face with itself. It is not an upward aspiration, because it is stripped of all hope. That defiance is the absolute certainty of a crushing fate, maintained entirely without the resignation that usually accompanies it.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Dialectical Inversion. Camus reverses the initial premise: life is lived better without meaning. To live an absurd fate requires sustaining the tension between consciousness and cosmic silence. Suicide abolishes one term and destroys the encounter. Defiant revolt (the first consequence) is keeping the absurd alive like a permanent inner revolution.

---

### §5 • Original PDF Page 46
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 46):**  
> This is where it is seen to what a degree absurd experience is remote from suicide. It may be thought that suicide follows revolt— but wrongly. For it does not represent the logical outcome of revolt. It is just the contrary by the consent it presupposes. Suicide, like the leap, is acceptance at its extreme. Everything is over and man returns to his essential history. His future, his unique and dreadful future—he sees and rushes toward it. In its way, suicide settles the absurd. It engulfs the absurd in the same death. But I know that in order to keep alive, the absurd cannot be settled. It escapes suicide to the extent that it is simultaneously awareness and rejection of death. It is, at the extreme limit of the condemned man’s last thought, that shoelace that despite everything he sees a few yards away, on the very brink of his dizzying fall. The contrary of suicide, in fact, is the man condemned to death.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Here one sees just how completely the experience of the absurd diverges from suicide. One might suppose that suicide is the natural continuation of revolt, but that is an error. Suicide does not represent the logical conclusion of defiance. It is, in truth, the exact opposite, because of the implicit consent it entails. Suicide, exactly like the religious leap of faith, is surrender carried to its absolute extreme. Everything ceases, and man folds back into nonexistence. He glimpses his end—his singular, terrifying destiny—and rushes blindly toward it. In its own manner, suicide resolves the absurd. It drags the absurd down into the grave alongside the physical self. Yet I know that if the absurd is to stay alive, it must never be resolved. It eludes suicide precisely because it demands both a radical consciousness of death and an unyielding refusal to welcome it. It is found at the outermost limit of the condemned man's final perception—in that discarded shoelace he notices on the ground just paces from the scaffold, on the very edge of the abyss. The true antithesis of suicide is the man sentenced to death.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Condemned Man vs. The Suicide. Suicide is an act of total capitulation: it terminates the confrontation by eliminating the subject. The absurd requires sustaining consciousness to the edge of the scaffold. The condemned man noticing the shoelace embodies radical presence and refusal to consent to annihilation.

---

### §6 • Original PDF Page 47
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 47):**  
> That revolt gives life its value. Spread out over the whole length of a life, it restores its majesty to that life. To a man devoid of blinders, there is no finer sight than that of the intelligence at grips with a reality that transcends it. The sight of human pride is unequaled. No disparagement is of any use. That discipline that the mind imposes on itself, that will conjured up out of nothing, that face-to-face struggle have something exceptional about them. To impoverish that reality whose inhumanity constitutes man’s majesty is tantamount to impoverishing him himself. I understand then why the doctrines that explain everything to me also debilitate me at the same time. They relieve me of the weight of my own life, and yet I must carry it alone. At this juncture, I cannot conceive that a skeptical metaphysics can be joined to an ethics of renunciation. Consciousness and revolt, these rejections are the contrary of renunciation. Everything that is indomitable and passionate in a human heart quickens them, on the contrary, with its own life. It is essential to die unreconciled and not of one’s own free will. Suicide is a repudiation. The absurd man can only drain everything to the bitter end, and deplete himself. The absurd is his extreme tension, which he maintains constantly by solitary effort, for he knows that in that consciousness and in that day-to-day revolt he gives proof of his only truth, which is defiance. This is a first consequence.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
It is this defiance that gives existence its genuine value. Sustained across the entire arc of an existence, it restores a lost majesty to human life. To anyone who refuses the comfort of blinders, there is no more magnificent spectacle than human intelligence locked in combat with a reality that surpasses it. The display of lucid human pride remains unmatched. No cynic's disparagement can diminish it. That mental discipline the intellect imposes on itself, that resolute will forged out of nothing, that direct, unblinking struggle possess an extraordinary nobility. To water down that cosmic indifference whose very inhumanity calls forth human greatness is to diminish humanity itself. I understand now why philosophies that claim to explain everything also sap my vital strength. They lift the burden of my own existence from my shoulders, when I ought to carry it alone. At this crossroads, I cannot see how a skeptical metaphysics could ever justify an ethics of ascetic surrender. Lucid awareness and conscious defiance—these refusals are the exact opposite of resignation. Every indomitable and passionate impulse within the human spirit animates them with its own vitality. The essential task is to die unreconciled, rather than taking one's own life. Suicide is a capitulation. The absurd man must drain every drop of experience to the very dregs, exhausting himself completely. The absurd is his perpetual state of tension, maintained through solitary, unremitting effort, because he knows that in continuous awareness and day-to-day rebellion he bears witness to his sole truth: unyielding defiance. This constitutes the first consequence.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Derivation of Consequence 1: Defiant Revolt. Overcoming asceticism and nihilism. Grand metaphysical systems that explain away life's tragedy disempower the individual. True dignity lies in dying 'unreconciled'—never resigning, never taking one's own life, exhausting life's potential through sheer stubborn defiance.

---

### §7 • Original PDF Page 47
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 47):**  
> If I remain in that prearranged position which consists in drawing all the conclusions (and nothing else) involved in a newly discovered notion, I am faced with a second paradox. In order to remain faithful to that method, I have nothing to do with the problem of metaphysical liberty. Knowing whether or not man is free doesn’t interest me. I can experience only my own freedom. As to it, I can have no general notions, but merely a few clear insights. The problem of “freedom as such” has no meaning. For it is linked in quite a different way with the problem of God. Knowing whether or not man is free involves knowing whether he can have a master. The absurdity peculiar to this problem comes from the fact that the very notion that makes the problem of freedom possible also takes away all its meaning. For in the presence of God there is less a problem of freedom than a problem of evil. You know the alternative: either we are not free and God the all-powerful is responsible for evil. Or we are free and responsible but God is not all-powerful. All the scholastic subtleties have neither added anything to nor subtracted anything from the acuteness of this paradox.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
If I hold firmly to my methodical stance—which requires extracting every consequence, and nothing more, from our initial premise—I encounter a second paradox. To preserve that rigorous method, I must set aside the traditional metaphysical problem of free will. Debating whether human beings possess abstract metaphysical freedom does not interest me. I can experience only the concrete reality of my own autonomy. Regarding that lived freedom, I have no grand universal doctrines, but only a handful of distinct insights. The question of 'freedom in itself' is meaningless. That problem is bound up in a fundamentally different way with the concept of God. To ask whether man is free is ultimately to ask whether he must submit to a cosmic master. The peculiar absurdity of this theological dilemma lies in the fact that the very postulate that makes divine freedom imaginable simultaneously strips human freedom of all coherence. In the presence of God, the question ceases to be about human freedom and becomes the problem of evil. The classic dilemma is well known: either we are unfree, and an omnipotent God is the author of evil; or we are free and morally responsible, in which case God is not all-powerful. Centuries of scholastic hairsplitting have neither sharpened nor softened the severity of that contradiction.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Dismissal of Metaphysical Liberty. Camus bypasses the theological deadlock of free will and the problem of evil (the Epicurean trilemma). Abstract metaphysical freedom requires an external sovereign. The absurd inquirer rejects universal essences in favor of immediate, concrete human agency.

---

### §8 • Original PDF Page 48
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 48):**  
> This is why I cannot get lost in the glorification or the mere definition of a notion which eludes me and loses its meaning as soon as it goes beyond the frame of reference of my individual experience. I cannot understand what kind of freedom would be given me by a higher being. I have lost the sense of hierarchy. The only conception of freedom I can have is that of the prisoner or the individual in the midst of the State. The only one I know is freedom of thought and action. Now if the absurd cancels all my chances of eternal freedom, it restores and magnifies, on the other hand, my freedom of action. That privation of hope and future means an increase in man’s availability.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
This is why I refuse to lose myself in celebrating or defining an abstract concept that slips from my grasp the moment it exceeds the boundaries of my personal experience. I cannot fathom what kind of liberty a supreme being could possibly bestow upon me. I have lost all reverence for cosmic hierarchy. The only freedom I can genuinely grasp is that of the prisoner inside his cell or the citizen facing the authority of the State. The only liberty I know intimately is the freedom to think and to act. If the reality of the absurd extinguishes any prospect of eternal liberty, it restores and magnifies, by contrast, my immediate freedom of action. The loss of hope and a guaranteed future produces an immense expansion in human availability.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Freedom Grounded in Finitude. Real freedom is not cosmic permission; it is the freedom of the prisoner within the prison walls or the citizen within the state. Stripped of eternal expectations, the individual gains complete availability (disponibilité) for immediate action.

---

### §9 • Original PDF Page 48
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 48):**  
> Before encountering the absurd, the everyday man lives with aims, a concern for the future or for justification (with regard to whom or what is not the question). He weighs his chances, he counts on “someday,” his retirement or the labor of his sons. He still thinks that something in his life can be directed. In truth, he acts as if he were free, even if all the facts make a point of contradicting that liberty. But after the absurd, everything is upset. That idea that “I am,” my way of acting as if everything has a meaning (even if, on occasion, I said that nothing has)—all that is given the lie in vertiginous fashion by the absurdity of a possible death. Thinking of the future, establishing aims for oneself, having preferences—all this presupposes a belief in freedom, even if one occasionally ascertains that one doesn’t feel it. But at that moment I am well aware that that higher liberty, that freedom to be, which alone can serve as basis for a truth, does not exist. Death is there as the only reality. After death the chips are down. I am not even free, either, to perpetuate myself, but a slave, and, above all, a slave without hope of an eternal revolution, without recourse to contempt. And who without revolution and without contempt can remain a slave? What freedom can exist in the fullest sense without assurance of eternity?

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Before encountering the absurd, the ordinary person lives guided by long-term goals, preoccupied with the future or with justifying his life (before whom or what, he never asks). He calculates his prospects, relying on 'someday,' on his eventual retirement, or on the achievements of his children. He maintains the conviction that some portion of his existence remains under his control. In truth, he behaves as if he were free, even while empirical reality relentlessly refutes that presumption. Yet once the absurd strikes, this entire structure collapses. The belief that 'I exist' in some stable metaphysical sense, my habit of living as though events possessed inherent purpose (even when I casually claimed they had none)—all of this is violently shattered by the arbitrary certainty of death. Plotting one's future, setting goals, cultivating preferences—all this assumes a foundational belief in freedom, even if one occasionally admits to feeling constrained. At this threshold, however, I realize that such higher liberty—that metaphysical 'freedom of being' which alone could anchor an absolute truth—simply does not exist. Death stands before us as the sole undeniable reality. Once death arrives, the game is over and the wagers are settled. I do not even have the freedom to prolong my own existence; I am a captive, and worse, a captive without any prospect of an eternal rebellion, stripped even of the consolation of contempt. And who, deprived of both revolt and disdain, can remain a willing slave? What absolute freedom could possibly exist without the guarantee of eternity?

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Collapse of the Social Autopilot. Conventional life rests on the illusion of self-direction ('tomorrow,' career advancement, retirement). The brutal reality of mortality exposes the farce: death is the absolute boundary that invalidates any grand 'freedom of being.'

---

### §10 • Original PDF Page 49
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 49):**  
> But at the same time the absurd man realizes that hitherto he was bound to that postulate of freedom on the illusion of which he was living. In a certain sense, that hampered him. To the extent to which he imagined a purpose to his life, he adapted himself to the demands of a purpose to be achieved and became the slave of his liberty. Thus I could not act otherwise than as the father (or the engineer or the leader of a nation, or the post-office sub-clerk) that I am preparing to be. I think I can choose to be that rather than something else. I think so unconsciously, to be sure. But at the same time I strengthen my postulate with the beliefs of those around me, with the presumptions of my human environment (others are so sure of being free, and that cheerful mood is so contagious!). However far one may remain from any presumption, moral or social, one is partly influenced by them and even, for the best among them (there are good and bad presumptions), one adapts one’s life to them. Thus the absurd man realizes that he was not really free. To speak clearly, to the extent to which I hope, to which I worry about a truth that might be individual to me, about a way of being or creating, to the extent to which I arrange my life and prove thereby that I accept its having a meaning, I create for myself barriers between which I confine my life. I do like so many bureaucrats of the mind and heart who only fill me with disgust and whose only vice, I now see clearly, is to take man’s freedom seriously.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Simultaneously, the absurd man discovers that he was previously shackled to the very illusion of freedom upon which his life was based. In a very real sense, that illusion constricted him. Whenever he imagined an overarching purpose for his life, he conformed to the requirements of that goal, turning himself into a slave of his own supposed freedom. I felt compelled to behave strictly as the father, the engineer, the statesman, or the junior post-office clerk I was preparing to become. I imagined I could freely choose one path over another. I assumed this unthinkingly, of course. Yet I reinforced that assumption with the shared convictions of those around me, bolstered by the prejudices of my social world (where everyone is so convinced of their own autonomy, and such self-assurance is immensely contagious!). No matter how detached one tries to remain from social and moral dogmas, one is inevitably influenced by them, and one shapes one's daily life around even the most admirable of them. The absurd man thus recognizes that he was never genuinely free. To put it plainly: to the extent that I invest in hope, obsess over discovering some personal destiny, or attempt to curate my existence to prove it possesses meaning, I construct the very prison walls that enclose me. I become like those bureaucrats of the intellect and the heart who inspire in me only revulsion—whose fatal error, I now see clearly, was to take human metaphysical freedom seriously.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Teleological Slavery vs. Absurd Autonomy. Paradoxically, believing in a life purpose enslaves the individual to social roles (the father, engineer, post-office clerk). The pursuit of personal justification turns men into 'bureaucrats of the intellect,' trading lived freedom for an imaginary future.

---

### §11 • Original PDF Page 49
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 49):**  
> The absurd enlightens me on this point: there is no future. Henceforth this is the reason for my inner freedom. I shall use two comparisons here. Mystics, to begin with, find freedom in giving themselves. By losing themselves in their god, by accepting his rules, they become secretly free. In spontaneously accepted slavery they recover a deeper independence. But what does that freedom mean? It may be said, above all, that they feel free with regard to themselves, and not so much free as liberated. Likewise, completely turned toward death (taken here as the most obvious absurdity), the absurd man feels released from everything outside that passionate attention crystallizing in him. He enjoys a freedom with regard to common rules. It can be seen at this point that the initial themes of existential philosophy keep their entire value. The return to consciousness, the escape from everyday sleep represent the first steps of absurd freedom. But it is existential preaching that is alluded to, and with it that spiritual leap which basically escapes consciousness. In the same way (this is my second comparison) the slaves of antiquity did not belong to themselves. But they knew that freedom which consists in not feeling responsible. Death, too, has patrician hands which, while crushing, also liberate.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The absurd illuminates this decisive truth: there is no tomorrow. From this point forward, this realization becomes the bedrock of my profound inner freedom. Consider two illustrative analogies. The mystics, first of all, discover freedom through total surrender. By dissolving into their deity and submitting to divine law, they attain an intimate, hidden freedom. Through an unreserved submission, they experience a deeper kind of independence. Yet what does such freedom actually signify? Above all, they feel unburdened of their own ego—they feel liberated rather than autonomously free. In the same manner, by confronting the certainty of death (the most glaring of all absurdities), the absurd man feels emancipated from everything other than the intense focus burning within him. He experiences a sudden freedom from conventional social constraints. Here one recognizes that the foundational insights of existential thought retain their full significance. The awakening of consciousness and the break from the stupor of daily habit mark the initial steps of absurd freedom. Yet this is where existentialist dogma begins its proselytizing, demanding a spiritual leap that ultimately abandons lucid awareness. Similarly—to take my second analogy—the slaves of classical antiquity did not own themselves. Yet they experienced that peculiar liberty of bearing no moral responsibility for their condition. Death, too, possesses aristocratic hands: while it crushes us, it simultaneously sets us free.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** No Tomorrow as Liberation. The destruction of the future liberates the absurd man from social debt. Drawing on the analogies of the mystic and the ancient slave, Camus demonstrates how release from ultimate accountability and the crushing finality of death paradoxically liberate the present.

---

### §12 • Original PDF Page 50
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 50):**  
> Losing oneself in that bottomless certainty, feeling henceforth sufficiently remote from one’s own life to increase it and take a broad view of it—this involves the principle of a liberation. Such new independence has a definite time limit, like any freedom of action. It does not write a check on eternity. But it takes the place of the illusions of freedom, which all stopped with death. The divine availability of the condemned man before whom the prison doors open in a certain early dawn, that unbelievable disinterestedness with regard to everything except for the pure flame of life—it is clear that death and the absurd are here the principles of the only reasonable freedom: that which a human heart can experience and live. This is a second consequence. The absurd man thus catches sight of a burning and frigid, transparent and limited universe in which nothing is possible but everything is given, and beyond which all is collapse and nothingness. He can then decide to accept such a universe and draw from it his strength, his refusal to hope, and the unyielding evidence of a life without consolation.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
To immerse oneself in that bottomless certainty—to feel sufficiently detached from one's own existence to see it clearly and expand its scope—is the foundation of genuine liberation. This newly won independence carries a strict expiration date, like any practical freedom of action. It draws no credit on eternity. Instead, it displaces the old illusions of freedom, all of which broke down at the threshold of death. Consider the sublime availability of the condemned man when the prison gates swing open at dawn, that profound detachment from everything except the naked flame of life itself; it becomes evident that death and the absurd are the true foundations of the only reasonable freedom: the freedom a human heart can actually inhabit and feel. This marks the second consequence. The absurd man thereby perceives a universe at once burning and frigid, transparent yet bounded, where nothing is divinely ordained but everything is immediately given, and beyond which lies only collapse and the void. He can then choose to embrace such a world, drawing from it his endurance, his defiant refusal of hope, and the lucid testimony of a life lived without consolation.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Derivation of Consequence 2: Absurd Freedom. The Condemned Man at Dawn. The absurd man achieves 'sublime availability' (divine disponibilité) without relying on eternity. In an indifferent universe where nothing is promised but everything is physically given, he finds the strength to live without consolation.

---

### §13 • Original PDF Page 51
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 51):**  
> But what does life mean in such a universe? Nothing else for the moment but indifference to the future and a desire to use up everything that is given. Belief in the meaning of life always implies a scale of values, a choice, our preferences. Belief in the absurd, according to our definitions, teaches the contrary. But this is worth examining.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Yet what does it mean to live inside such a universe? For now, it implies nothing less than an indifference to the future and a hunger to exhaust everything that is given. Belief in a transcendent purpose for existence always assumes a hierarchy of values, a selective choice, a ranking of preferences. Living in awareness of the absurd, by our definitions, teaches the exact opposite. This claim, however, requires careful examination.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Transition to the Third Consequence. Purging Value Hierarchies. Traditional morality requires a transcendent scale of values to rank experiences. The absurd levels these hierarchies, demanding that we replace qualitative selection with the total exhaustion of the given.

---

### §14 • Original PDF Page 51
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 51):**  
> Knowing whether or not one can live without appeal is all that interests me. I do not want to get out of my depth. This aspect of life being given me, can I adapt myself to it? Now, faced with this particular concern, belief in the absurd is tantamount to substituting the quantity of experiences for the quality. If I convince myself that this life has no other aspect than that of the absurd, if I feel that its whole equilibrium depends on that perpetual opposition between my conscious revolt and the darkness in which it struggles, if I admit that my freedom has no meaning except in relation to its limited fate, then I must say that what counts is not the best living but the most living. It is not up to me to wonder if this is vulgar or revolting, elegant or deplorable. Once and for all, value judgments are discarded here in favor of factual judgments. I have merely to draw the conclusions from what I can see and to risk nothing that is hypothetical. Supposing that living in this way were not honorable, then true propriety would command me to be dishonorable.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
My sole inquiry is to determine whether it is possible to live without appeal to any higher authority. I have no desire to wander into speculative abstractions. Given this concrete condition of existence, can I truly adapt myself to it? Confronted with this question, living by the absurd is equivalent to replacing the quality of experiences with sheer quantity. If I am convinced that life possesses no dimension other than the absurd; if I recognize that its balance rests entirely on the unbroken tension between conscious defiance and the darkness surrounding it; and if I acknowledge that my freedom exists only within this bounded fate, then I must conclude that what matters is not the best life, but the most life. It is not my task to ask whether this ethic is crude or repugnant, refined or regrettable. Moral value judgments are permanently discarded here in favor of empirical judgments of fact. My only responsibility is to infer conclusions from what I observe, staking nothing on mere hypothesis. Even if living in this fashion were deemed dishonorable, true intellectual integrity would demand that I accept the dishonor.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Quantity Over Quality ('Not the best living, but the most living'). Camus repudiates qualitative moral hierarchies. When transcendent standards are abandoned, one cannot judge one experience as 'higher' than another. The imperative shifts from moral perfection to experiential quantity and intensity.

---

### §15 • Original PDF Page 51
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 51):**  
> The most living; in the broadest sense, that rule means nothing. It calls for definition. It seems to begin with the fact that the notion of quantity has not been sufficiently explored. For it can account for a large share of human experience. A man’s rule of conduct and his scale of values have no meaning except through the quantity and variety of experiences he has been in a position to accumulate. Now, the conditions of modern life impose on the majority of men the same quantity of experiences and consequently the same profound experience. To be sure, there must also be taken into consideration the individual’s spontaneous contribution, the “given” element in him. But I cannot judge of that, and let me repeat that my rule here is to get along with the immediate evidence.
