I see, then, that the individual character of a common code of ethics lies not so much in the ideal importance of its basic principles as in the norm of an experience that it is possible to measure. To stretch a point somewhat, the Greeks had the code of their leisure just as we have the code of our eight-hour day. But already many men among the most tragic cause us to foresee that a longer experience changes this table of values. They make us imagine that adventurer of the everyday who through mere quantity of experiences would break all records (I am purposely using this sports expression) and would thus win his own code of ethics. Yet let’s avoid romanticism and just ask ourselves what such an attitude may mean to a man with his mind made up to take up his bet and to observe strictly what he takes to be the rules of the game.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
'Living to the maximum' remains an empty formula in the abstract. It demands rigorous definition. The inquiry must begin with the fact that the concept of quantity has never been adequately examined. Yet quantity explains a vast portion of human experience. A person's moral conduct and hierarchy of values derive their practical meaning solely from the quantity and variety of experiences they have managed to accumulate. Today, the conditions of modern life impose upon the vast majority of people the identical volume of experiences, and consequently the very same underlying reality. To be sure, one must also account for an individual's innate temperament, the raw 'given' of their nature. Yet I have no means to evaluate that hidden factor, and I reiterate that my method relies strictly on observable evidence. I observe, then, that the distinct character of an ethical code resides less in the theoretical nobility of its principles than in the measurable baseline of lived experience it reflects. To draw an analogy, the ancient Greeks possessed an ethic shaped by aristocratic leisure, just as our modern culture possesses an ethic defined by the eight-hour workday. Yet even now, certain figures of tragic stature show us that an expanded range of experience completely alters this table of values. They prompt us to envision an adventurer of the everyday who, through the sheer volume of his encounters, would break every record (I use this athletic metaphor intentionally) and thereby forge his own moral code. Let us strip away romantic melodrama, however, and ask what such a stance actually demands of someone resolved to honor his wager and follow the rules of the game to the letter.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Everyday Adventurer Breaking All Records. Ethics are conditioned by the structure of time (Greek leisure vs. the modern eight-hour workday). The absurd hero acts as an 'adventurer of the everyday,' maximizing empirical encounters to forge an autonomous ethic without romantic delusion.

---

### §16 • Original PDF Page 52
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 52):**  
> Breaking all the records is first and foremost being faced with the world as often as possible. How can that be done without contradictions and without playing on words? For on the one hand the absurd teaches that all experiences are unimportant, and on the other it urges toward the greatest quantity of experiences. How, then, can one fail to do as so many of those men I was speaking of earlier—choose the form of life that brings us the most possible of that human matter, thereby introducing a scale of values that on the other hand one claims to reject?

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Breaking every record means, above all, confronting the physical world as frequently as possible. Yet how can one achieve this without falling into contradiction and verbal sleight of hand? On one hand, the absurd teaches that all experiences are fundamentally equivalent in their lack of cosmic significance; on the other, it urges us toward the greatest possible quantity of those experiences. How can one avoid doing what so many others have done—selecting a lifestyle designed to yield the greatest heap of raw human sensation, and thereby smuggling back the very hierarchy of values we claim to have abolished?

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Dialectical Dilemma of Quantity. The apparent contradiction: if all experiences are equal in an absurd world, how does one justify striving for the greatest number without secretly reintroducing a qualitative hierarchy of values?

---

### §17 • Original PDF Page 52
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 52):**  
> But again it is the absurd and its contradictory life that teaches us. For the mistake is thinking that that quantity of experiences depends on the circumstances of our life when it depends solely on us. Here we have to be over-simple. To two men living the same number of years, the world always provides the same sum of experiences. It is up to us to be conscious of them. Being aware of one’s life, one’s revolt, one’s freedom, and to the maximum, is living, and to the maximum. Where lucidity dominates, the scale of values becomes useless. Let’s be even more simple. Let us say that the sole obstacle, the sole deficiency to be made good, is constituted by premature death. Thus it is that no depth, no emotion, no passion, and no sacrifice could render equal in the eyes of the absurd man (even if he wished it so) a conscious life of forty years and a lucidity spread over sixty years. Madness and death are his irreparables. Man does not choose. The absurd and the extra life it involves therefore do not depend on man’s will, but on its contrary, which is death. Weighing words carefully, it is altogether a question of luck. One just has to be able to consent to this. There will never be any substitute for twenty years of life and experience. By what is an odd inconsistency in such an alert race, the Greeks claimed that those who died young were beloved of the gods. And that is true only if you are willing to believe that entering the ridiculous world of the gods is forever losing the purest of joys, which is feeling, and feeling on this earth. The present and the succession of presents before a constantly conscious soul is the ideal of the absurd man. But the word “ideal” rings false in this connection. It is not even his vocation, but merely the third consequence of his reasoning. Having started from an anguished awareness of the inhuman, the meditation on the absurd returns at the end of its itinerary to the very heart of the passionate flames of human revolt.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Once again, the paradox of the absurd provides the solution. The fundamental error lies in assuming that the volume of experience depends upon external circumstances, when in reality it depends entirely upon ourselves. Here we must reduce the matter to its barest essentials. To two individuals living the identical number of years, the external world offers the exact same sum of raw sensations. The difference lies in our conscious awareness of them. To remain lucidly aware of one's existence, one's defiance, and one's autonomy to the highest degree—that is what it means to live to the absolute maximum. Wherever radical clarity reigns, external hierarchies of value become entirely superfluous. Let us make the point simpler still. The only genuine obstacle, the sole irreversible deprivation, is premature death. Thus, no psychological depth, no emotional intensity, no passionate devotion, and no heroic sacrifice could ever make forty years of conscious existence equal, in the eyes of the absurd man, to sixty years of awake, lucid life. Madness and physical death are the only two irreparable catastrophes. Man does not choose the hour of his departure. The absurd and the surplus of existence it demands therefore do not depend upon human will, but upon its exact opposite: mortality itself. Weighing our terms with precision, longevity is entirely a matter of biological fortune. One must have the honesty to accept this condition. Nothing can ever compensate for twenty lost years of lived, conscious experience. Through a strange contradiction in so sharp-minded a civilization, the ancient Greeks maintained that those who die young are beloved by the gods. Yet that myth holds true only if one believes that entering the illusory realm of the gods compensates for the permanent loss of the purest joy imaginable: sensory feeling, and feeling here on this earth. To inhabit the present and a relentless succession of presents before a constantly wakeful consciousness is the aim of the absurd man. Yet the word 'aim' rings false in this context. It is not even a chosen calling, but simply the third unavoidable consequence of our reasoning. Having originated in the agonizing awareness of a hostile, inhuman cosmos, this meditation on the absurd arrives at the conclusion of its journey directly in the passionate fire of human revolt.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Derivation of Consequence 3: Passion. Consciousness as Multiplier. Experiential quantity is not external tourism; it is the intensity of conscious presence across time. Sixty lucid years are unequivocally superior to forty, because death is the sole irreparable loss. The Greek myth of early death is exposed as a theological fraud.

---

### §18 • Original PDF Page 53
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 53):**  
> Thus I draw from the absurd three consequences, which are my revolt, my freedom, and my passion. By the mere activity of consciousness I transform into a rule of life what was an invitation to death—and I refuse suicide. I know, to be sure, the dull resonance that vibrates throughout these days. Yet I have but a word to say: that it is necessary. When Nietzsche writes: “It clearly seems that the chief thing in heaven and on earth is to obey at length and in a single direction: in the long run there results something for which it is worth the trouble of living on this earth as, for example, virtue, art, music, the dance, reason, the mind— something that transfigures, something delicate, mad, or divine,” he elucidates the rule of a really distinguished code of ethics. But he also points the way of the absurd man. Obeying the flame is both the easiest and the hardest thing to do. However, it is good for man to judge himself occasionally. He is alone in being able to do so. “Prayer,” says Alain, “is when night descends over thought.” “But the mind must meet the night,” reply the mystics and the existentials. Yes, indeed, but not that night that is born under closed eyelids and through the mere will of man—dark, impenetrable night that the mind calls up in order to plunge into it. If it must encounter a night, let it be rather that of despair, which remains lucid—polar night, vigil of the mind, whence will arise perhaps that white and virginal brightness which outlines every object in the light of the intelligence. At that degree, equivalence encounters passionate understanding. Then it is no longer even a question of judging the existential leap. It resumes its place amid the age-old fresco of human attitudes. For the spectator, if he is conscious, that leap is still absurd. In so far as it thinks it solves the paradox, it reinstates it intact. On this score, it is stirring. On this score, everything resumes its place and the absurd world is reborn in all its splendor and diversity.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Thus, from the reality of the absurd, I extract three inevitable consequences: my defiance, my freedom, and my passion. Through the solitary activity of consciousness, I transform what seemed a summons to death into an imperative to live—and I repudiate suicide. I am acutely aware of the hollow ache that reverberates through these days of confrontation. Yet I have only one answer to offer: it is necessary. When Nietzsche writes that 'the essential thing in heaven and earth is to obey relentlessly in a single direction over time, yielding something that makes life on this earth worth living—such as virtue, art, music, dance, reason, or intellect: something transfiguring, delicate, wild, or divine,' he articulates the rule of a genuinely aristocratic ethic. Yet he also illuminates the trajectory of the absurd individual. To obey this inner flame is at once the easiest and the most demanding task of all. Nevertheless, it is vital that a man sit in judgment of himself from time to time. He alone is qualified to deliver the verdict. 'Prayer,' observed the philosopher Alain, 'is when night settles over thought.' 'Yet the intellect must confront the night,' answer the mystics and the existentialists. Undoubtedly—yet not that artificial darkness conjured behind deliberately shut eyelids by a willful act of self-delusion, an impenetrable gloom that the mind invents solely to drown itself in comforting ignorance. If the intellect must face the darkness, let it be the night of lucid despair: a polar night, a vigilant watch of the mind, from which may rise that stark, immaculate dawn throwing every contour into the sharp relief of intelligence. At this altitude, cosmic indifference meets passionate comprehension. At that point, it is no longer even necessary to pass judgment upon the existential leap of faith. It simply reassumes its place among the ancient tapestry of human compromises. To the lucid observer, that leap remains thoroughly absurd. In believing that it solves the paradox, it leaves the contradiction entirely intact. In that very futility, it retains a tragic grandeur. And on these terms, every element reclaims its rightful place, and the absurd universe is reborn in all its richness and diversity.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Grand Synthesis: Defiance, Freedom, Passion. Through pure lucidity, the absurd inquiry turns an invitation to suicide into a rule of heroic existence. Contrasting the voluntary blindness of the leap ('night born under closed eyelids') with the 'polar night' of lucid despair, Camus affirms life without transcendent consolation.

---

### §19 • Original PDF Page 54
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 54):**  
> But it is bad to stop, hard to be satisfied with a single way of seeing, to go without contradiction, perhaps the most subtle of all spiritual forces. The preceding merely defines a way of thinking. But the point is to live.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Yet it is a mistake to stand still; it is difficult to rest content with a single perspective or to dispense with contradiction, which is perhaps the most subtle of all spiritual engines. The preceding analysis outlines only an intellectual framework. The true task is to live it.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Coda: From Epistemology to Praxis. The transition from Part I ('An Absurd Reasoning') to Part II ('The Absurd Man'). Dialectical reasoning is merely the scaffolding; philosophy must culminate in lived physical existence and active creation.

---

## 5. Don Juanism (pp. 58–63)
*Part II: The Absurd Man*

### §1 • Original PDF Page 58
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 58):**  
> As a modern author writes, “it is not essential to be a dupe.” The attitudes of which I shall treat can assume their whole meaning only through consideration of their contraries. A sub-clerk in the post office is the equal of a conqueror if consciousness is common to them. All experiences are indifferent in this regard. There are some that do either a service or a disservice to man. They do him a service if he is conscious. Otherwise, that has no importance: a man’s failures imply judgment, not of circumstances, but of himself. I am choosing solely men who aim only to expend themselves or whom I see to be expending themselves. That has no further implications. For the moment I want to speak only of a world in which thoughts, like lives, are devoid of future. Everything that makes man work and get excited utilizes hope. The sole thought that is not mendacious is therefore a sterile thought. In the absurd world the value of a notion or of a life is measured by its sterility.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
As a modern writer observed, "One is not obliged to be a fool." The ways of living I am about to examine derive their full significance only when contrasted with their opposites. A minor clerk in a post office is the exact equal of a conqueror, provided both possess the same lucid awareness. From this perspective, all experiences are fundamentally equal. Certain experiences either serve a person or do them a disservice. They serve a person only if they remain fully conscious of them. Otherwise, the circumstance matters little: a person’s failure reflects not on external conditions, but on themselves. I select here only individuals whose sole aim is to burn through their vitality, or who visibly expend their lives without reservation. That choice implies nothing beyond itself. For now, I wish to speak only of a world where thoughts, like human lives, possess no future. Everything that drives human labor and ambition depends on the illusion of hope. The only thought that does not lie, therefore, is a sterile thought. Within an absurd world, the true value of an idea—or of a life—is measured precisely by its refusal to bear fruit for an imagined future.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Post-Office Clerk and the Conqueror. Camus establishes the premise of Part II: social status and worldly grandeur are irrelevant illusions. Dignity depends entirely on lucid awareness. Authentic existential living demands thought and action devoid of hope—measuring life by conscious expenditure rather than future dividends.

---

### §2 • Original PDF Page 58
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 58):**  
> If it were sufficient to love, things would be too easy. The more one loves, the stronger the absurd grows. It is not through lack of love that Don Juan goes from woman to woman. It is ridiculous to represent him as a mystic in quest of total love. But it is indeed because he loves them with the same passion and each time with his whole self that he must repeat his gift and his profound quest. Whence each woman hopes to give him what no one has ever given him. Each time they are utterly wrong and merely manage to make him feel the need of that repetition. “At last,” exclaims one of them, “I have given you love.” Can we be surprised that Don Juan laughs at this? “At last? No,” he says, “but once more.” Why should it be essential to love rarely in order to love much?

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
If loving were enough, existence would be simple. The more intensely one loves, the sharper the friction of the absurd becomes. It is not from an inability to love that Don Juan moves from woman to woman. It is absurd to depict him as a frustrated mystic searching for an absolute, transcendent love. On the contrary: it is precisely because he loves each woman with identical intensity, offering his entire being each time, that he must continually repeat this total gift and quest. This is why each woman dreams she will finally give him what no woman before has ever provided. Each time she is completely mistaken, merely reinforcing in him the necessity of repetition. "At last," one of them proclaims, "I have given you true love!" Can anyone be surprised that Don Juan laughs? "At last? No," he answers, "only once more." Why should one have to love rarely in order to love deeply?

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Ethic of Quantity over Quality in Love. Romantic idealism insists that 'true love' must be singular, exclusive, and eternal. Camus demolishes this mystical trope: Don Juan is not a tragic seeker of an impossible ideal, but an absurd hero who refuses the illusion of eternity, giving himself fully to each immediate encounter.

---

### §3 • Original PDF Page 58
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 58):**  
> Is Don Juan melancholy? This is not likely. I shall barely have recourse to the legend. That laugh, the conquering insolence, that playfulness and love of the theater are all clear and joyous. Every healthy creature tends to multiply himself. So it is with Don Juan. But, furthermore, melancholy people have two reasons for being so: they don’t know or they hope. Don Juan knows and does not hope. He reminds one of those artists who know their limits, never go beyond them, and in that precarious interval in which they take their spiritual stand enjoy all the wonderful ease of masters. And that is indeed genius: the intelligence that knows its frontiers. Up to the frontier of physical death Don Juan is ignorant of melancholy. The moment he knows, his laugh bursts forth and makes one forgive everything. He was melancholy at the time when he hoped. Today, on the mouth of that woman he recognizes the bitter and comforting taste of the only knowledge. Bitter? Barely: that necessary imperfection that makes happiness perceptible!

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Is Don Juan a melancholic figure? That is highly unlikely. We hardly need to consult romantic legends to see this. His laughter, his triumphant audacity, his sheer delight in performance and theatricality are entirely radiant and joyful. Every healthy, vital organism naturally seeks to multiply itself; so it is with Don Juan. Moreover, people fall into melancholy for only two reasons: either they are ignorant of reality, or they cling to hope. Don Juan knows reality completely, and he harbors no hope. He resembles those accomplished artists who recognize their exact boundaries, never exceed them, and within that narrow, precarious space where they take their stand, command all the graceful mastery of genius. And that is true genius: an intellect fully conscious of its frontiers. Right up to the boundary of physical death, Don Juan remains untouched by melancholy. The moment clarity dawns on him, his laughter rings out, sweeping away all reproach. He was melancholy only back when he still clung to hope. Today, upon the lips of the woman before him, he tastes the astringent, bracing flavor of the only knowledge that exists. Bitter? Hardly—merely that essential imperfection that renders human happiness tangible.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Melancholy Belongs to Hope, Not Lucidity. Melancholy is born from two sources: ignorance of mortal limits or agonizing longing for an eternity. Don Juan eliminates both. Because he accepts his finite horizon, his joy is unpoisoned by regret. Like a master artist working strictly within the constraints of his medium, Don Juan masters living within mortality.

---

### §4 • Original PDF Page 59
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 59):**  
> It is quite false to try to see in Don Juan a man brought up on Ecclesiastes. For nothing is vanity to him except the hope of another life. He proves this because he gambles that other life against heaven itself. Longing for desire killed by satisfaction, that commonplace of the impotent man, does not belong to him. That is all right for Faust, who believed in God enough to sell himself to the devil. For Don Juan the thing is simpler. Molina’s Burlador ever replies to the threats of hell: “What a long respite you give me!” What comes after death is futile, and what a long succession of days for whoever knows how to be alive! Faust craved worldly goods; the poor man had only to stretch out his hand. It already amounted to selling his soul when he was unable to gladden it. As for satiety, Don Juan insists upon it, on the contrary. If he leaves a woman it is not absolutely because he has ceased to desire her. A beautiful woman is always desirable. But he desires another, and no, this is not the same thing.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
It is entirely mistaken to view Don Juan as a disciple of Ecclesiastes, murmuring that all is vanity. For him, nothing is vanity except the promise of another life. He demonstrates this by wagering any supposed afterlife against the immediate pleasures of the earth. The weary disillusionment that follows satisfaction—that cliché of the emotionally exhausted—is alien to him. That weary posture suits Faust, whose belief in God was still strong enough to warrant bargaining with the Devil. For Don Juan, the matter is far more direct. In Tirso de Molina's play, the Trickster answers every threat of damnation with the same refrain: "What a generous respite you grant me!" Whatever lies beyond death is irrelevant; what matters is the boundless succession of days granted to anyone who truly knows how to live. Faust hungered for worldly mastery, yet the poor wretch had only to reach out his hand to seize it. He had already bartered his soul away the moment he proved incapable of bringing it joy. Don Juan, by contrast, embraces satiety fully. When he departs from a woman, it is never simply because he has ceased to desire her. A beautiful woman remains permanently desirable. But he desires another woman, and those two desires are entirely distinct.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Don Juan vs. Faust (The Refusal of Transcendent Bargains). Faust is an intellectual who secretly believes in the absolute and trades his soul out of spiritual impotence. Don Juan has no truck with the supernatural; hell is an empty threat because eternity is meaningless. Molina's line ('What a long respite you grant me!') anchors his focus on the concrete span of life.

---

### §5 • Original PDF Page 59
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 59):**  
> This life gratifies his every wish, and nothing is worse than losing it. This madman is a great wise man. But men who live on hope do not thrive in this universe where kindness yields to generosity, affection to virile silence, and communion to solitary courage. And all hasten to say: “He was a weakling, an idealist or a saint.” One has to disparage the greatness that insults.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
This mortal life satisfies his every desire, and no catastrophe exceeds the tragedy of losing it. What ordinary society brands a madman is, in truth, an accomplished sage. Yet those who survive on hope cannot breathe in a world where polite sentiment yields to lavish generosity, easy affection to resolute silence, and herd solidarity to solitary courage. Thus moralists rush to dismiss him: "He was merely an unstable weakling, an unfulfilled idealist, or a latent saint." Mediocrity always feels compelled to demean a greatness that exposes its own cowardice.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Sage Branded as Madman. Conventional morality denounces Don Juan because his radical focus on mortal enjoyment insults their self-righteous asceticism. Camus contrasts conventional herd virtues (politeness, sentimental warmth, reliance on community) with absurd virtues (magnanimity, resolute silence, solitary courage).

---

### §6 • Original PDF Page 60
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 60):**  
> People are sufficiently annoyed (or that smile of complicity that debases what it admires) by Don Juan’s speeches and by that same remark that he uses on all women. But to anyone who seeks quantity in his joys, the only thing that matters is efficacy. What is the use of complicating the passwords that have stood the test? No one, neither the woman nor the man, listens to them, but rather to the voice that pronounces them. They are the rule, the convention, and the courtesy. After they are spoken the most important still remains to be done. Don Juan is already getting ready for it. Why should he give himself a problem in morality? He is not like Milosz’s Mañara, who damns himself through a desire to be a saint. Hell for him is a thing to be provoked. He has but one reply to divine wrath, and that is human honor: “I have honor,” he says to the Commander, “and I am keeping my promise because I am a knight.” But it would be just as great an error to make an immoralist of him. In this regard, he is “like everyone else”: he has the moral code of his likes and dislikes. Don Juan can be properly understood only by constant reference to what he commonly symbolizes: the ordinary seducer and the sexual athlete. He is an ordinary seducer. Except for the difference that he is conscious, and that is why he is absurd. A seducer who has become lucid will not change for all that. Seducing is his condition in life. Only in novels does one change condition or become better. Yet it can be said that at the same time nothing is changed and everything is transformed. What Don Juan realizes in action is an ethic of quantity, whereas the saint, on the contrary, tends toward quality. Not to believe in the profound meaning of things belongs to the absurd man. As for those cordial or wonder-struck faces, he eyes them, stores them up, and does not pause over them. Time keeps up with him. The absurd man is he who is not apart from time. Don Juan does not think of “collecting” women. He exhausts their number and with them his chances of life. “Collecting” amounts to being capable of living off one’s past. But he rejects regret, that other form of hope. He is incapable of looking at portraits.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Critics are perpetually irritated—or wear that sly, condescending smirk that cheapens whatever it secretly envies—by Don Juan's standard declarations and the repeated lines he addresses to every woman. Yet for anyone pursuing an ethic of quantitative joy, the only criterion is practical efficacy. Why complicate passwords that have already proven their power? Neither the woman nor the man truly analyzes the literal words; what moves them is the living voice that delivers them. They serve as the opening ritual, the established convention, and the required courtesy. Once spoken, the essential work still lies ahead, and Don Juan is already bracing for it. Why should he invent artificial moral dilemmas for himself? He bears no resemblance to Milosz's Miguel Mañara, who courts damnation out of an inverted hunger for sainthood. For Don Juan, the threat of hell is something to be openly defied. To divine wrath he offers only a single defense: human honor. "I possess honor," he informs the Stone Commander, "and I keep my word because I am a gentleman." Yet it would be an equal mistake to classify him as a dogmatic immoralist. In this respect he is like everyone else: his morality consists entirely of his genuine sympathies and antipathies. Don Juan can only be understood by returning to what he fundamentally represents: the ordinary seducer and the relentless sensualist. He is, in essence, an ordinary seducer. With the sole distinction that he is fully conscious, and for that exact reason, he is absurd. A seducer who attains radical clarity will not suddenly renounce his nature. Seduction is his vital condition. It is only in didactic novels that human beings magically change their nature or achieve moral improvement. Yet one can say that while nothing on the surface changes, everything is fundamentally transformed. What Don Juan enacts through his behavior is an ethic of quantity, directly opposing the saint's pursuit of qualitative perfection. Refusing to believe in hidden metaphysical meanings is the hallmark of the absurd person. As for those welcoming or spellbound faces, he takes them in, absorbs them, and never lingers. Time marches in step with him. The absurd man is one who refuses to detach himself from time. Don Juan does not "collect" women like souvenirs. He exhausts their number, and in doing so, exhausts his mortal possibilities. "Collecting" implies an ability to feed on memories of the past. But Don Juan rejects nostalgia, which is merely hope turned backward. He cannot bear to gaze at portraits.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Conscious Sensualism vs. Moral Conversion. Becoming lucid about life's absurdity does not convert someone into an ascetic; it sharpens their commitment to their authentic vocation. Camus also distinguishes the absurd man from the collector: collecting clings to the past (nostalgia as inverted hope), whereas Don Juan lives purely in the present instant without accumulating relics.

---

### §7 • Original PDF Page 61
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 61):**  
> Is he selfish for all that? In his way, probably. But here, too, it is essential to understand one another. There are those who are made for living and those who are made for loving. At least Don Juan would be inclined to say so. But he would do so in a very few words such as he is capable of choosing. For the love we are speaking of here is clothed in illusions of the eternal. As all the specialists in passion teach us, there is no eternal love but what is thwarted. There is scarcely any passion without struggle. Such a love culminates only in the ultimate contradiction of death. One must be Werther or nothing. There, too, there are several ways of committing suicide, one of which is the total gift and forgetfulness of self. Don Juan, as well as anyone else, knows that this can be stirring. But he is one of the very few who know that this is not the important thing. He knows just as well that those who turn away from all personal life through a great love enrich themselves perhaps but certainly impoverish those their love has chosen. A mother or a passionate wife necessarily has a closed heart, for it is turned away from the world. A single emotion, a single creature, a single face, but all is devoured. Quite a different love disturbs Don Juan, and this one is liberating. It brings with it all the faces in the world, and its tremor comes from the fact that it knows itself to be mortal. Don Juan has chosen to be nothing.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Does this make him fundamentally selfish? In his own way, perhaps. Yet here, too, we must define our terms carefully. There are those born for living, and those born for loving—or so Don Juan would argue, using the spare, precise vocabulary natural to him. At least Don Juan would be inclined to frame it in those terms. And he would express it in the few sharp words he chooses with such precision. For romantic love, as traditionally conceived, cloaks itself in fantasies of eternity. As every chronicler of tragic romance demonstrates, the only love that feels eternal is a thwarted love. Passion rarely thrives without obstacles and agony. Such romantic adoration inevitably culminates in the ultimate contradiction: death. One must either become Werther or remain nothing. In romance, too, there are numerous forms of suicide, not least of which is the total surrender and dissolution of self in another. Don Juan understands as well as anyone that such self-immolation can be emotionally moving. Yet he is among the rare few who realize that it is fundamentally beside the point. He recognizes that those who abandon their own independent life for the sake of a grand passion may enrich their own emotional drama, but they inevitably drain and suffocate the person they claim to adore. A possessive mother or an obsessively devoted wife inevitably possesses a constricted heart, because it has closed itself off from the wider world. A single emotion, a single person, a single face—and everything else is consumed. Don Juan is stirred by a completely different love: an expansive, liberating passion. It embraces every face under the sun, and its exquisite tremor arises from the lucid awareness that it is finite and mortal. Don Juan has consciously chosen to surrender any claim to permanent identity.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Romantic Monogamy as Covert Suicide vs. Generous Ephemeral Love. Camus exposes the romantic cult of 'grand passion' (Werther) as a death drive: romantic obsession demands impossible eternity and ends in mutual suffocation or literal suicide. Don Juan's love is expansive and non-possessive; acknowledging its mortality, it leaves the beloved free and rejoices in the living world.

---

### §8 • Original PDF Page 61
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 61):**  
> For him it is a matter of seeing clearly. We call love what binds us to certain creatures only by reference to a collective way of seeing for which books and legends are responsible. But of love I know only that mixture of desire, affection, and intelligence that binds me to this or that creature. That compound is not the same for another person. I do not have the right to cover all these experiences with the same name. This exempts one from conducting them with the same gestures. The absurd man multiplies here again what he cannot unify. Thus he discovers a new way of being which liberates him at least as much as it liberates those who approach him. There is no noble love but that which recognizes itself to be both short-lived and exceptional. All those deaths and all those rebirths gathered together as in a sheaf make up for Don Juan the flowering of his life. It is his way of giving and of vivifying. I let it be decided whether or not one can speak of selfishness.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
For him, everything reduces to seeing clearly. We assign the word "love" to the bond tying us to particular individuals only by relying on cultural myths manufactured by novels and folklore. In reality, all I directly experience of love is that unique fusion of desire, tenderness, and intellectual affinity binding me to a specific human being. That exact combination cannot be duplicated with anyone else. I have no right to paste the same generic label over radically diverse encounters. Refusing to use a single label frees one from approaching each person with identical, scripted gestures. Here again, the absurd man multiplies what he is powerless to unify. In doing so, he discovers a mode of existence that liberates him every bit as much as it frees those who enter his life. There is no truly noble love other than that which understands itself to be simultaneously transient and rare. All those successive endings and rebirths, bound together like a rich harvest, constitute for Don Juan the full flowering of his existence. This is his unique way of giving of himself and breathing vitality into others. I leave it to the reader to decide whether this can legitimately be called selfishness.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Demystifying Romantic Vocabulary. The universal concept of 'Love' is an abstraction borrowed from literature. Real emotional experience is always particular, transient, and unrepeatable. The absurd strategy is to multiply concrete encounters rather than attempting to force them into a single, unified metaphysical category.

---

### §9 • Original PDF Page 62
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 62):**  
> I think at this point of all those who absolutely insist that Don Juan be punished. Not only in another life, but even in this one. I think of all those tales, legends, and laughs about the aged Don Juan. But Don Juan is already ready. To a conscious man old age and what it portends are not a surprise. Indeed, he is conscious only in so far as he does not conceal its horror from himself. There was in Athens a temple dedicated to old age. Children were taken there. As for Don Juan, the more people laugh at him, the more his figure stands out. Thereby he rejects the one the romantics lent him. No one wants to laugh at that tormented, pitiful Don Juan. He is pitied; heaven itself will redeem him? But that’s not it. In the universe of which Don Juan has a glimpse, ridicule too is included. He would consider it normal to be chastised. That is the rule of the game. And, indeed, it is typical of his nobility to have accepted all the rules of the game. Yet he knows he is right and that there can be no question of punishment. A fate is not a punishment.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
At this point, I think of all those moralists who demand that Don Juan be punished—not merely in the next world, but within this one. They insist he must suffer retribution not only in eternity, but in his earthly life. I think of the endless cautionary tales, folklore, and derisive mockery directed at the aging Don Juan. But Don Juan is already prepared. To a lucid human being, physical aging and everything it entails come as no surprise. Indeed, one is truly conscious only to the extent that one refuses to hide from the grim reality of decay. In ancient Athens, there stood a temple dedicated to Old Age. Children were taken there to behold their future. As for Don Juan, the louder society laughs at his physical decline, the more majestic his stature becomes. By accepting this ridicule, he shatters the romantic caricature of himself. No one wants to laugh at the brooding, pitiful Don Juan invented by the Romantics. Instead, people pity him and imagine that heaven will eventually redeem him. But that is a sentimental distortion. Within the absurd cosmos Don Juan perceives, mockery is part of the landscape. He regards social condemnation as perfectly natural. That is simply part of the rules of the game. And it is the hallmark of his aristocratic nobility to have accepted every rule of the game without complaint. Yet in his core, he knows he is justified, and that talk of moral retribution is meaningless. A mortal destiny is not a divine punishment.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Destiny vs. Punishment (Aging and Ridicule). Society demands that the aging seducer suffer humiliation as divine retribution for his sensuality. Camus counters that biological decay is not moral punishment, but the universal human fate. The absurd man accepts physical degradation with open eyes, without seeking sentimental pity or religious redemption.

---

### §10 • Original PDF Page 62
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 62):**  
> That is his crime, and how easy it is to understand why the men of God call down punishment on his head. He achieves a knowledge without illusions which negates everything they profess. Loving and possessing, conquering and consuming—that is his way of knowing. (There is significance in that favorite Scriptural word that calls the carnal act “knowing.”) He is their worst enemy to the extent that he is ignorant of them. A chronicler relates that the true Burlador died assassinated by Franciscans who wanted “to put an end to the excesses and blasphemies of Don Juan, whose birth assured him impunity.” Then they proclaimed that heaven had struck him down. No one has proved that strange end. Nor has anyone proved the contrary. But without wondering if it is probable, I can say that it is logical. I want merely to single out at this point the word “birth” and to play on words: it was the fact of living that assured his innocence. It was from death alone that he derived a guilt now become legendary.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
That is his true transgression, and it is easy to see why religious authorities demand vengeance upon his head. He achieves an uncompromising clarity that invalidates everything they preach. Loving and possessing, conquering and expending—this is his method of knowing the world. (It is deeply revealing that the biblical idiom refers to the carnal act itself as "knowing.") He is their most dangerous adversary precisely because he pays them no mind. An ancient chronicler records that the historical Trickster of Seville was assassinated in secret by Franciscan monks who wished "to terminate the outrages and blasphemies of a Don Juan whose noble birth guaranteed him impunity." The friars then publicly announced that divine wrath had struck him dead. No historian has verified that bizarre account. Nor has anyone managed to refute it. Yet without debating its historical probability, one can affirm its poetic logic. I wish only to focus here on the word "birth" and make a deliberate play upon it: it was the raw fact of being alive that established his innocence. It was solely from death that moralists manufactured the legendary guilt attached to his name.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Carnal Knowledge as Epistemological Heresy. By grounding knowledge purely in physical experience ('knowing' in the Biblical sense), Don Juan invalidates religious claims to transcendent authority. The church had to invent divine retribution (or murder him and blame God) because an unpunished, joyful mortal exposes religious guilt as an artificial construct.

---

### §11 • Original PDF Page 63
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 63):**  
> What else does that stone Commander signify, that cold statue set in motion to punish the blood and courage that dared to think? All the powers of eternal Reason, of order, of universal morality, all the foreign grandeur of a God open to wrath are summed up in him. That gigantic and soulless stone merely symbolizes the forces that Don Juan negated forever. But the Commander’s mission stops there. The thunder and lightning can return to the imitation heaven whence they were called forth. The real tragedy takes place quite apart from them. No, it was not under a stone hand that Don Juan met his death. I am inclined to believe in the legendary bravado, in that mad laughter of the healthy man provoking a non-existent God. But, above all, I believe that on that evening when Don Juan was waiting at Anna’s the Commander didn’t come, and that after midnight the blasphemer must have felt the dreadful bitterness of those who have been right. I accept even more readily the account of his life that has him eventually burying himself in a monastery. Not that the edifying aspect of the story can be considered probable. What refuge can he go ask of God? But this symbolizes rather the logical outcome of a life completely imbued with the absurd, the grim ending of an existence turned toward short-lived joys. At this point sensual pleasure winds up in asceticism. It is essential to realize that they may be, as it were, the two aspects of the same destitution. What more ghastly image can be called up than that of a man betrayed by his body who, simply because he did not die in time, lives out the comedy while awaiting the end, face to face with that God he does not adore, serving him as he served life, kneeling before a void and arms outstretched toward a heaven without eloquence that he knows to be also without depth?

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
What else does the Stone Commander embody—that frozen statue brought to life to crush the living pulse of a man who dared to think for himself? In that statue are concentrated all the dictates of cosmic Reason, institutional order, universal morality, and the alien majesty of an angry God. That colossal, lifeless monument of stone merely personifies the external dogmas Don Juan rejected his entire life. Yet the Commander's dramatic role ends right there. The theatrical thunder and lightning can return to the artificial stage ceiling from which they were summoned. The genuine tragedy unfolds entirely elsewhere. No, Don Juan did not meet his end crushed beneath a stone handshake. I am certainly ready to believe in the legendary defiance—in the wild, defiant laughter of a vital man challenging a non-existent deity. But above all, I believe that on the evening Don Juan waited for his final reckoning, the Stone Commander never showed up at all—and that past midnight, the blasphemer experienced the crushing, bitter desolation of those who have been entirely correct all along. I find it even easier to accept the historical tradition in which Don Juan ends his days secluded inside a monastery. Not that the pious moral of the story holds any water: what possible sanctuary could he ask from a God he does not believe in? What refuge could he conceivably seek from a deity he has always denied? Rather, this cloister represents the ultimate logical denouement of an existence steeped in the absurd—the austere finale of a life devoted exclusively to fleeting pleasures. At this threshold, extreme sensuality folds into extreme asceticism. One must understand that these two extremes are merely opposite faces of the identical existential poverty. What more haunting spectacle could one imagine than a man betrayed by his aging flesh, who—simply because he failed to die in the prime of life—plays out the theatrical farce to the very end: confronting a God he does not worship, performing religious rituals with the same mechanical detachment with which he once pursued seduction, kneeling before an absolute void, with arms outstretched toward a silent heaven he knows to be utterly devoid of depth?

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Stone Commander Never Came: The Desolation of Being Right. The greatest existential insight in the essay: the terrifying realization is not that God punishes the rebel, but that God never appears. Seduction and monastic asceticism are two sides of the same coin: living fully within physical reality without transcendent illusions. Don Juan kneeling in an empty chapel before a silent void is the ultimate absurd hero.

---

### §12 • Original PDF Page 63
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 63):**  
> I see Don Juan in a cell of one of those Spanish monasteries lost on a hilltop. And if he contemplates anything at all, it is not the ghosts of past loves, but perhaps, through a narrow slit in the sun-baked wall, some silent Spanish plain, a noble, soulless land in which he recognizes himself. Yes, it is on this melancholy and radiant image that the curtain must be rung down. The ultimate end, awaited but never desired, the ultimate end is negligible.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
I envision Don Juan in the cell of an isolated Spanish monastery perched high upon an arid hilltop. And if he gazes out at anything at all, it is certainly not the phantom memories of forgotten lovers, but rather—framed through a narrow slit in the sun-baked stone wall—the silent, sweeping Spanish plateau: an austere, soulless expanse in which he recognizes his own nature. Yes, it is upon this melancholic yet luminous tableau that the theatrical curtain must descend. That final death—inevitable, never sought, yet completely anticipated—remains fundamentally negligible.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Arid Landscape of Radical Lucidity. In the final scene, Don Juan achieves perfect harmony with the absurd: looking out over the barren, sun-drenched Spanish plateau—a landscape as indifferent, austere, and soulless as his own lucid mind. Death is neither feared nor courted; it is merely an indifferent terminus to a fully expended life.

---

## 6. Drama (pp. 64–69)
*Part II: The Absurd Man*

### §1 • Original PDF Page 64
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 64):**  
> “The play’s the thing,” says Hamlet, “wherein I’ll catch the conscience of the king.” “Catch” is indeed the word. For conscience moves swiftly or withdraws within itself. It has to be caught on the wing, at that barely perceptible moment when it glances fleetingly at itself. The everyday man does not enjoy tarrying. Everything, on the contrary, hurries him onward. But at the same time nothing interests him more than himself, especially his potentialities. Whence his interest in the theater, in the show, where so many fates are offered him, where he can accept the poetry without feeling the sorrow. There at least can be recognized the thoughtless man, and he continues to hasten toward some hope or other. The absurd man begins where that one leaves off, where, ceasing to admire the play, the mind wants to enter in. Entering into all these lives, experiencing them in their diversity, amounts to acting them out. I am not saying that actors in general obey that impulse, that they are absurd men, but that their fate is an absurd fate which might charm and attract a lucid heart. It is necessary to establish this in order to grasp without misunderstanding what will follow.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"The play's the thing," declares Hamlet, "wherein I'll catch the conscience of the King." "Catch" is precisely the right word. For consciousness moves with extreme speed, or retreats instantly back into itself. It must be captured mid-flight, at that fleeting, almost imperceptible instant when it casts a backward glance upon its own reflection. The ordinary person has no patience for lingering self-examination. On the contrary, the momentum of daily life relentlessly drives them forward. Yet at the same time, nothing fascinates a person more than themselves—especially their own unrealized possibilities. Hence their enduring attraction to the theater, to the stage, where countless alternative destinies are laid out before them, allowing them to savor the dramatic beauty without bearing the actual weight of the agony. In that passive spectator one recognizes the unreflective person, perpetually rushing toward one comforting hope after another. The absurd man begins precisely where that spectator stops: where, refusing to remain a mere detached observer, the mind demands to enter the arena directly. To inhabit all these varied human lives, experiencing them in their full multiplicity, is to perform them on the stage. I am not claiming that actors as a class consciously obey this philosophical imperative, or that they are personally absurd heroes; rather, their professional destiny is inherently an absurd destiny—one capable of fascinating and attracting a lucid mind. This distinction must be established clearly from the outset to avoid any confusion in what follows.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Spectator vs. The Actor (Passive Illusion vs. Active Embodiment). Everyday humans use theater as escapism: an effortless peek into alternate lives without personal risk or suffering. The absurd person, however, refuses passive spectatorship. By physically embodying multiple characters, the actor transforms the quantitative appetite of the absurd into physical reality, living multiple lifetimes in an afternoon.

---

### §2 • Original PDF Page 64
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 64):**  
> The actor’s realm is that of the fleeting. Of all kinds of fame, it is known, his is the most ephemeral. At least, this is said in conversation. But all kinds of fame are ephemeral. From the point of view of Sirius, Goethe’s works in ten thousand years will be dust and his name forgotten. Perhaps a handful of archæologists will look for “evidence” as to our era. That idea has always contained a lesson. Seriously meditated upon, it reduces our perturbations to the profound nobility that is found in indifference. Above all, it directs our concerns toward what is most certain—that is, toward the immediate. Of all kinds of fame, the least deceptive is the one that is lived.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The actor's domain is the kingdom of the fleeting. Of all forms of celebrity, theatrical fame is acknowledged to be the most ephemeral. At least, that is the common platitude. Yet in truth, every form of human glory is ephemeral. Viewed from the cosmic perspective of Sirius, even Goethe's masterpieces will be reduced to dust in ten thousand years, and his name will be utterly erased. At most, a few future archaeologists may sift the earth for artifacts of our forgotten civilization. That realization has always offered a profound lesson. Contemplated with seriousness, it dissolves our petty anxieties into the dignified nobility of existential indifference. Above all, it turns our attention toward the only certainty we possess: the immediate present. Of all varieties of fame, the least deceptive is the fame that is directly experienced in the flesh.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Cosmic Indifference (The Perspective of Sirius). Intellectuals boast of posthumous immortality through books and monuments. Camus invokes the cosmic scale (the star Sirius) to show that all art and human memory will perish. The actor's fame is the most honest because it does not pretend to outlive its performance; it is expended and enjoyed entirely in real time.

---

### §3 • Original PDF Page 65
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 65):**  
> Hence the actor has chosen multiple fame, the fame that is hallowed and tested. From the fact that everything is to die someday he draws the best conclusion. An actor succeeds or does not succeed. A writer has some hope even if he is not appreciated. He assumes that his works will bear witness to what he was. At best the actor will leave us a photograph, and nothing of what he was himself, his gestures and his silences, his gasping or his panting with love, will come down to us. For him, not to be known is not to act, and not acting is dying a hundred times with all the creatures he would have brought to life or resuscitated.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The actor has therefore chosen an immediate, collective glory—a fame affirmed and experienced night after night before a living audience. From the undeniable truth that everything must eventually perish, he derives the most rigorous conclusion. For the actor, success or failure is instantaneous: he either triumphs in the room or he fails completely. A writer can always nurse the secret hope of future recognition even when neglected in his lifetime, assuming his written pages will testify to posterity on his behalf. At best, the actor leaves behind a lifeless photograph; nothing of his living essence—his sudden physical gestures, his pregnant pauses, his breath catching in grief or panting with desire—can ever be preserved. For the actor, not to be recognized in the moment is not to perform at all; and not performing means dying a hundred deaths alongside every character he was poised to ignite into life.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Binary of the Stage vs. The Delusion of Posterity. The writer can hide behind the illusion of posthumous vindication; the actor cannot. The actor's existence is binary: immediate triumph or immediate oblivion. To be unperformed is to surrender the creation of life.

---

### §4 • Original PDF Page 65
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 65):**  
> Why should we be surprised to find a fleeting fame built upon the most ephemeral of creations? The actor has three hours to be Iago or Alceste, Phèdre or Gloucester. In that short space of time he makes them come to life and die on fifty square yards of boards. Never has the absurd been so well illustrated or at such length. What more revelatory epitome can be imagined than those marvelous lives, those exceptional and total destinies unfolding for a few hours within a stage set? Off the stage, Sigismundo ceases to count. Two hours later he is seen dining out. Then it is, perhaps, that life is a dream. But after Sigismundo comes another. The hero suffering from uncertainty takes the place of the man roaring for his revenge. By thus sweeping over centuries and minds, by miming man as he can be and as he is, the actor has much in common with that other absurd individual, the traveler. Like him, he drains something and is constantly on the move. He is a traveler in time and, for the best, the hunted traveler, pursued by souls. If ever the ethics of quantity could find sustenance, it is indeed on that strange stage.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Why should we find it strange that a transient reputation rests upon the most ephemeral of human creations? The actor is granted exactly three hours to inhabit Iago or Alceste, Phèdre or Gloucester. Within that brief window, he brings them screaming into existence and escorts them to their deaths across fifty square yards of wooden floorboards. Never has the condition of the absurd been illustrated with such visceral clarity. What more vivid microcosm could one conceive than these extraordinary destinies, unfolding from birth to annihilation within the artificial frame of a theatrical set? Once he steps into the wings, Prince Sigismundo ceases to exist; two hours later, the man who played him is spotted eating supper at a local tavern. It is at that precise moment, perhaps, that Calderón's maxim rings true: life is indeed a dream. Yet directly behind Sigismundo follows another persona: the indecisive, agonizing intellectual abruptly replaces the warlord crying out for bloody vengeance. By traversing centuries and sensibilities, by physically enacting human nature both as it is and as it might be, the actor shares a deep kinship with that other absurd wanderer: the traveler. Like the traveler, he exhausts his surroundings and remains perpetually in motion. He is a traveler across historical time—and, at his peak, a hunted traveler pursued by the ghost of a hundred souls. If the ethic of quantitative experience ever found an ideal laboratory, it is surely upon these strange, illuminated boards.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Actor as Time Traveler (Calderón's 'Life is a Dream'). On fifty square yards of wooden boards, a complete human destiny is ignited, exhausted, and extinguished within three hours. The actor demonstrates the ultimate quantitative triumph over linear time: by inhabiting conflicting souls in rapid succession, he becomes a traveler through centuries of human consciousness.

---

### §5 • Original PDF Page 65
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 65):**  
> To what degree the actor benefits from the characters is hard to say. But that is not the important thing. It is merely a matter of knowing how far he identifies himself with those irreplaceable lives. It often happens that he carries them with him, that they somewhat overflow the time and place in which they were born. They accompany the actor, who cannot very readily separate himself from what he has been. Occasionally when reaching for his glass he resumes Hamlet’s gesture of raising his cup. No, the distance separating him from the creatures into whom he infuses life is not so great. He abundantly illustrates every month or every day that so suggestive truth that there is no frontier between what a man wants to be and what he is. Always concerned with better representing, he demonstrates to what a degree appearing creates being. For that is his art—to simulate absolutely, to project himself as deeply as possible into lives that are not his own. At the end of his effort his vocation becomes clear: to apply himself wholeheartedly to being nothing or to being several. The narrower the limits allotted him for creating his character, the more necessary his talent. He will die in three hours under the mask he has assumed today. Within three hours he must experience and express a whole exceptional life. That is called losing oneself to find oneself. In those three hours he travels the whole course of the dead-end path that the man in the audience takes a lifetime to cover.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Exactly how much the actor absorbs internally from the roles he portrays is difficult to measure. But that psychological question is beside the point. The real question is how completely he merges his identity with those unrepeatable lives. Frequently, he carries them with him off the stage, their emotional resonance spilling over the boundaries of the performance. They linger in his physical presence; he cannot easily sever his own body from the ghosts he has animated. At dinner, reaching casually for his wine glass, he inadvertently replicates Hamlet's motion of raising the poisoned chalice. No, the gulf separating the actor from the creatures he breathes life into is remarkably thin. Day after day, month after month, he provides compelling proof of an unsettling truth: there is no clear boundary between what a human being pretends to be and what they actually are. Constantly focused on externalizing character through action, he demonstrates how powerfully appearance generates being. For that is his discipline: to simulate so completely that he projects himself into destinies foreign to his own. When his labor is finished, his existential calling stands revealed: to devote himself entirely either to being nobody, or to being a multitude. The tighter the time limit imposed for constructing his character, the more formidable his craft must be. Within three short hours, he must expire beneath the mask he put on tonight. In three hours, he must experience, articulate, and exhaust an entire lifetime. This is the true meaning of losing oneself in order to find oneself. Across that single evening, he travels the entire trajectory of that fatal, dead-end road which takes the spectator in the seats an entire lifetime to navigate.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Appearance Creates Being (The Dissolution of Fixed Identity). Camus challenges the metaphysical assumption of an essential 'true self.' The actor demonstrates that pretending to be someone physically actually produces that being ('appearing creates being'). The actor compresses into 180 minutes the identical mortal cul-de-sac that the passive spectator takes eighty years to endure.

---

### §6 • Original PDF Page 66
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 66):**  
> A mime of the ephemeral, the actor trains and perfects himself only in appearances. The theatrical convention is that the heart expresses itself and communicates itself only through gestures and in the body—or through the voice, which is as much of the soul as of the body. The rule of that art insists that everything be magnified and translated into flesh. If it were essential on the stage to love as people really love, to employ that irreplaceable voice of the heart, to look as people contemplate in life, our speech would be in code. But here silences must make themselves heard. Love speaks up louder, and immobility itself becomes spectacular. The body is king. Not everyone can be “theatrical,” and this unjustly maligned word covers a whole æsthetic and a whole ethic. Half a man’s life is spent in implying, in turning away, and in keeping silent. Here the actor is the intruder. He breaks the spell chaining that soul, and at last the passions can rush onto their stage. They speak in every gesture; they live only through shouts and cries. Thus the actor creates his characters for display. He outlines or sculptures them and slips into their imaginary form, transfusing his blood into their phantoms. I am of course speaking of great drama, the kind that gives the actor an opportunity to fulfill his wholly physical fate. Take Shakespeare, for instance. In that impulsive drama the physical passions lead the dance. They explain everything. Without them all would collapse. Never would King Lear keep the appointment set by madness without the brutal gesture that exiles Cordelia and condemns Edgar. It is just that the unfolding of that tragedy should thenceforth be dominated by madness. Souls are given over to the demons and their saraband. No fewer than four madmen: one by trade, another by intention, and the last two through suffering—four disordered bodies, four unutterable aspects of a single condition.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
A pantomimist of the temporary, the actor refines his craft exclusively within the realm of surfaces and appearances. The fundamental rule of the stage is that the human heart can communicate its internal truth only through physical gesture and the living body—or through the human voice, which belongs equally to the flesh and to the spirit. The law of the theater dictates that every psychological nuance must be magnified and translated into physical reality. If an actor were to portray love on stage with the whispered murmurs of everyday life, using the mute glances of private domesticity, his performance would remain an indecipherable secret to the room. On stage, even silence must ring with audible force. Passion must speak with heightened volume, and complete immobility must become a gripping spectacle. The physical body reigns supreme. Not everyone possesses the talent to be "theatrical"—a word unfairly degraded, which in truth encompasses an entire aesthetic and moral philosophy. Ordinary human life is spent in hesitation, evasive glances, and stifled silences. The actor arrives on stage as a radical intruder. He shatters the paralytic spell binding the human soul, finally letting dormant passions storm the platform. Here, passions speak through muscular movement; they announce their reality through cries, physical confrontations, and declarations. In this way, the actor builds figures meant for public exposure. He chisels their silhouettes, slips into their ghostly contours, and transfuses his own warm blood into their fictional veins. I am speaking, of course, of classical tragedy—the kind of drama that permits an actor to realize his purely physical destiny. Consider Shakespeare. In his visceral theater, raw physical passions orchestrate the entire drama. They provide the sole engine of the narrative; without them, the architecture falls to pieces. King Lear would never arrive at his fatal rendezvous with madness on the storm-swept heath were it not for the impulsive, violent gestures with which he disowns Cordelia and banishes Edgar. It is entirely fitting that this catastrophic collapse should be ruled by lunacy. Souls are surrendered to chaotic furies and their frenzied dance. Shakespeare assembles no fewer than four distinct madmen: one a professional fool, one feigning insanity by design, and two driven mad through unbearable anguish—four shattered physical vessels embodying four unspeakable dimensions of a single human condition.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Primacy of the Body in Drama (Shakespeare's Four Madmen). In normal life, humans hide behind polite restraint and repression. Great theater translates internal suffering into visceral, physical motion. Using King Lear, Camus demonstrates how tragedy operates through bodily violence and physical madness: the abstract mind must yield to the reality of the flesh.

---

### §7 • Original PDF Page 67
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 67):**  
> The very scale of the human body is inadequate. The mask and the buskin, the make-up that reduces and accentuates the face in its essential elements, the costume that exaggerates and simplifies—that universe sacrifices everything to appearance and is made solely for the eye. Through an absurd miracle, it is the body that also brings knowledge. I should never really understand Iago unless I played his part. It is not enough to hear him, for I grasp him only at the moment when I see him. Of the absurd character the actor consequently has the monotony, that single, oppressive silhouette, simultaneously strange and familiar, that he carries about from hero to hero. There, too, the great dramatic work contributes to this unity of tone. This is where the actor contradicts himself: the same and yet so various, so many souls summed up in a single body. Yet it is the absurd contradiction itself, that individual who wants to achieve everything and live everything, that useless attempt, that ineffectual persistence. What always contradicts itself nevertheless joins in him. He is at that point where body and mind converge, where the mind, tired of its defeats, turns toward its most faithful ally. “And blest are those,” says Hamlet, “whose blood and judgment are so well commingled that they are not a pipe for fortune’s finger to sound what stop she please.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The natural proportions of the naked human frame are almost insufficient for the stage. The ancient tragic mask and high-soled buskin, the exaggerated makeup that reduces the face to stark archetypal planes, the heavy costumes that dramatize and distill character—this entire world sacrifices inward subtlety to external form, engineered purely for the spectator's eye. Yet through a strange and marvelous paradox, it is this physical apparatus that delivers genuine comprehension. I could never truly understand Iago simply by analyzing his speeches; I understand him only when I physically embody him. It is not enough to read his words; I grasp his malice only when I watch his posture and mimic his gait. From his absurd existence, the actor inherits a certain underlying monotony: that persistent, singular physical silhouette, at once foreign and intimate, which he drags with him from one legendary role to the next. In this respect, great classical drama supports this unity of tone. Here lies the central contradiction of the actor's craft: he remains the identical flesh-and-blood person, yet manifests an astonishing multiplicity of souls gathered inside a solitary physical frame. This tension mirrors the foundational paradox of the absurd itself: a mortal being longing to experience everything and conquer every fate, locked in a magnificent, futile struggle marked by relentless perseverance. Contradictory impulses achieve physical reconciliation within him. He stands at the exact crossroads where intellect and biology fuse—where the mind, exhausted by abstract philosophical defeats, turns back toward its most dependable comrade: the body. "And blessed are those," Hamlet proclaims, "whose blood and judgment are so well commingled that they are not a pipe for fortune's finger to sound what stop she please."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Embodied Knowledge: Flesh as Epistemology. Theoretical analysis of evil (like Iago's malice) remains an academic abstraction until it is physically performed through breath, posture, and facial musculature. The actor resolves the Cartesian split between mind and matter: the mind, exhausted by unsolvable metaphysical riddles, grounds itself in the living physiology of the body.

---

### §8 • Original PDF Page 68
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 68):**  
> How could the Church have failed to condemn such a practice on the part of the actor? She repudiated in that art the heretical multiplication of souls, the emotional debauch, the scandalous presumption of a mind that objects to living but one life and hurls itself into all forms of excess. She proscribed in them that preference for the present and that triumph of Proteus which are the negation of everything she teaches. Eternity is not a game. A mind foolish enough to prefer a comedy to eternity has lost its salvation. Between “everywhere” and “forever” there is no compromise. Whence that much maligned profession can give rise to a tremendous spiritual conflict. “What matters,” said Nietzsche, “is not eternal life but eternal vivacity.” All drama is, in fact, in this choice.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
How could the Christian Church have failed to condemn the actor's vocation? The Church recoiled from an art that heretically multiplied the singular human soul—denouncing its emotional license and the brazen arrogance of a mind that refuses to content itself with a solitary earthly life, plunging instead into every imaginable excess. The Church outlawed the actor's adoration of the immediate present and his protean shape-shifting, recognizing in them the absolute negation of everything theology proclaims. Eternity cannot be treated as a theatrical diversion. A mind frivolous enough to prefer a three-hour comedy to eternal salvation has forfeited its place in heaven. Between the horizontal spread of being "everywhere" in time and the vertical absolute of existing "forever," no reconciliation is possible. Hence this historically reviled profession has constantly generated intense spiritual warfare. "What matters," Nietzsche declared, "is not eternal life, but eternal vivacity." The essence of theatrical tragedy resides entirely within that fateful choice.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Church's Condemnation: 'Everywhere' vs. 'Forever'. The historical excommunication of actors was not an accident of puritanism; it was an ontological clash. The Church demands that man invest everything in vertical eternity ('forever'); the actor invests everything in horizontal richness across mortal time ('everywhere'). The actor embodies Nietzsche's dictum: eternal vivacity over eternal salvation.

---

### §9 • Original PDF Page 68
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 68):**  
> Adrienne Lecouvreur on her deathbed was willing to confess and receive communion, but refused to abjure her profession. She thereby lost the benefit of the confession. Did this not amount, in effect, to choosing her absorbing passion in preference to God? And that woman in the death throes refusing in tears to repudiate what she called her art gave evidence of a greatness that she never achieved behind the footlights. This was her finest role and the hardest one to play. Choosing between heaven and a ridiculous fidelity, preferring oneself to eternity or losing oneself in God is the age-old tragedy in which each must play his part.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
On her deathbed, the celebrated actress Adrienne Lecouvreur was willing to make her confession and receive Holy Communion, but steadfastly refused to renounce her acting profession. By that refusal, she forfeited the Church's absolution. Did this act not amount, in reality, to choosing her consuming mortal art over the claims of God? And that dying woman, weeping in her death throes while refusing to disown what she revered as her art, exhibited a tragic heroism she never matched behind the footlights. That final refusal was her most magnificent performance, and the most agonizing role she ever undertook. Forced to choose between the promise of paradise and a stubborn, unyielding fidelity to her mortal craft—weighing loyalty to one's own identity against the submission of the soul to God—she enacted the timeless tragedy in which every conscious human being must take a stand.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Defiance of Adrienne Lecouvreur. By refusing on her deathbed to renounce her stagecraft in exchange for the last rites, Lecouvreur enacted the ultimate absurd revolt. She chose her ephemeral mortal passion over eternal salvation, proving that authentic human dignity lies in unyielding loyalty to one's earthly devotion.

---

### §10 • Original PDF Page 68
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 68):**  
> The actors of the era knew they were excommunicated. Entering the profession amounted to choosing Hell. And the Church discerned in them her worst enemies. A few men of letters protest: “What! Refuse the last rites to Molière!” But that was just, and especially in one who died onstage and finished under the actor’s make-up a life entirely devoted to dispersion. In his case genius is invoked, which excuses everything. But genius excuses nothing, just because it refuses to do so.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Actors of that era knew beyond doubt that they stood officially excommunicated. Entering the theatrical profession was tantamount to choosing damnation. And the Church correctly identified them as its most dangerous philosophical adversaries. Later literary critics protested with moral indignation: "How barbaric to deny Christian burial to Molière!" Yet the Church's judgment was entirely consistent—especially toward a playwright who collapsed upon the stage itself, concluding beneath greasepaint and costume a life dedicated entirely to the dispersion of the self into temporary roles. Admirers appeal to Molière's genius as an all-forgiving defense. But true genius seeks no special dispensations, precisely because it accepts the full consequences of its defiance.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Molière Dying in Greasepaint. Denying Christian burial to Molière was logically just: Molière died onstage in the middle of performing The Hypochondriac, literally dying under actor's greasepaint. He gave his entire life to the ephemeral dispersion of the self. True artistic genius demands no theological pardons; it stands by its choice of the mortal over the divine.

---

### §11 • Original PDF Page 68
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 68):**
