> The actor knew at that time what punishment was in store for him. But what significance could such vague threats have compared to the final punishment that life itself was reserving for him? This was the one that he felt in advance and accepted wholly. To the actor as to the absurd man, a premature death is irreparable. Nothing can make up for the sum of faces and centuries he would otherwise have traversed. But in any case, one has to die. For the actor is doubtless everywhere, but time sweeps him along, too, and makes its impression with him.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
In those days, the actor was fully aware of the ecclesiastical penalties awaiting him. Yet what terrifying weight could such supernatural warnings carry when weighed against the final, inescapable penalty life itself had prepared for him? It was this inevitable mortal finish that he anticipated from the beginning and embraced without reservation. To the actor, as to every absurd individual, an untimely death represents an irreparable disaster. Nothing can compensate for the lost sum of human faces, roles, and centuries he would otherwise have brought to life. Yet regardless of when it strikes, death remains inescapable. For while the actor seemingly travels everywhere across the imaginative expanse, linear time sweeps him along just as inexorably, leaving its relentless scars upon his flesh.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Untimely Death as Irreparable Loss. The absurd man fears neither hell nor moral judgment; his only real adversary is premature physical death. In an ethic of quantity, every year or decade lost is an absolute subtraction from the sum of potential experiences, characters, and eras that could have been lived.

---

### §12 • Original PDF Page 69
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 69):**  
> It requires but a little imagination to feel what an actor’s fate means. It is in time that he makes up and enumerates his characters. It is in time likewise that he learns to dominate them. The greater number of different lives he has lived, the more aloof he can be from them. The time comes when he must die to the stage and for the world. What he has lived faces him. He sees clearly. He feels the harrowing and irreplaceable quality of that adventure. He knows and can now die. There are homes for aged actors.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
It requires only a small leap of imagination to grasp the ultimate meaning of an actor's destiny. It is within the crucible of time that he invents and tallies his diverse roles. It is within time, equally, that he learns to master and inhabit them. The greater the multitude of foreign lives he has embodied, the more detached and serene he can remain toward them all. Eventually, the hour arrives when he must retire from the stage and vanish from public view. The entire sum of what he has lived rises up before his eyes. He sees the totality of his existence with radical clarity. He senses the poignant, irreplaceable splendor of that ephemeral journey. He has looked reality in the face, and now he is ready to die. There are retirement homes for aged actors.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Retirement Home for Aged Actors. The closing image of Chapter 6 strips away all theatrical glamour: the retired actor sitting quietly in a rest home. Having lived a hundred intense, contradictory lifetimes, he looks back without illusions. He has exhausted his mortal possibilities and achieved radical clarity; now, having fully expended his life, he can die without regret.

---

## 7. Conquest (pp. 69–75)
*Part II: The Absurd Man*

### §1 • Original PDF Page 69
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 69):**  
> “No,” says the conqueror, “don’t assume that because I love action I have had to forget how to think. On the contrary I can thoroughly define what I believe. For I believe it firmly and I see it surely and clearly. Beware of those who say: ‘I know this too well to be able to express it.’ For if they cannot do so, this is because they don’t know it or because out of laziness they stopped at the outer crust.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"No," the conqueror declares, "do not imagine that because I have dedicated myself to action, I have forgotten how to think." "On the contrary, I can articulate my convictions with absolute precision." "For what I believe, I believe unflinchingly, seeing it with unclouded lucidity." "Distrust anyone who insists: 'I understand this truth too deeply to express it in mere words.'" "If they cannot formulate their thought, it is either because they do not truly comprehend it, or because intellectual sloth led them to stop at the surface."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Conqueror as Lucid Thinker (Action is Not Mindlessness). The third absurd archetype is the Conqueror—the person of historical and political engagement. Camus rejects the prejudice that action is anti-philosophical. Genuine action requires rigorous, lucid thought; mystics who hide behind 'ineffable' feelings are merely intellectually lazy.

---

### §2 • Original PDF Page 69
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 69):**  
> “I have not many opinions. At the end of a life man notices that he has spent years becoming sure of a single truth. But a single truth, if it is obvious, is enough to guide an existence. As for me, I decidedly have something to say about the individual. One must speak of him bluntly and, if need be, with the appropriate contempt.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"I do not carry a vast baggage of philosophical opinions." "At the close of an existence, a person discovers that they have spent decades arriving at certainty regarding a solitary truth." "Yet a single truth, provided it is self-evident, is more than sufficient to orient an entire life." "As for my own compass, I have something decisive to say regarding the individual." "One must speak of human nature without sentimentality, and, where honesty demands it, with a measured degree of contempt."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Economy of a Single Lucid Truth. Intellectuals multiply complex systems, but existential living demands only one or two self-evident truths tested by lived experience. The conqueror strips away sentimental humanism to assess the real, unvarnished capabilities of mortal human beings.

---

### §3 • Original PDF Page 69
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 69):**  
> “A man is more a man through the things he keeps to himself than through those he says. There are many that I shall keep to myself. But I firmly believe that all those who have judged the individual have done so with much less experience than we on which to base their judgment. The intelligence, the stirring intelligence perhaps foresaw what it was essential to note. But the era, its ruins, and its blood overwhelm us with facts. It was possible for ancient nations, and even for more recent ones down to our machine age, to weigh one against the other the virtues of society and of the individual, to try to find out which was to serve the other. To begin with, that was possible by virtue of that stubborn aberration in man’s heart according to which human beings were created to serve or be served. In the second place, it was possible because neither society nor the individual had yet revealed all their ability.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"A human being is defined far more by what they keep silent than by what they say aloud." "There are vast realities I choose to keep to myself." "Yet I am convinced that earlier thinkers who sat in judgment on human nature did so with far less direct historical experience than our generation possesses." "Abstract intellect, in all its brilliance, may have anticipated what was worth noticing." "Yet our catastrophic historical era, choked with ruins and drenched in blood, confronts us with brutal, undeniable facts." "In antiquity, and even through recent centuries leading up to the industrial machine age, philosophers could debate the competing claims of the individual versus the collective, attempting to calculate which entity ought to serve the other." "That academic debate was possible, first, because of that obstinate delusion rooted in the human heart that humanity was cosmically designed either to master or to be enslaved." "And second, it was possible because neither modern totalitarian societies nor isolated individuals had yet unleashed the full extent of their destructive capacities."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Historical Catastrophe as Philosophical Laboratory. 20th-century history—wars, ruins, and mechanized slaughter—has permanently destroyed academic debates about whether man was made to serve the state or vice versa. The conqueror speaks from direct historical catastrophe, which stripped away all metaphysical illusions about human nature.

---

### §4 • Original PDF Page 70
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 70):**  
> “I have seen bright minds express astonishment at the masterpieces of Dutch painters born at the height of the bloody wars in Flanders, be amazed by the prayers of Silesian mystics brought up during the frightful Thirty Years’ War. Eternal values survive secular turmoils before their astonished eyes. But there has been progress since. The painters of today are deprived of such serenity. Even if they have basically the heart the creator needs—I mean the closed heart—it is of no use; for everyone, including the saint himself, is mobilized. This is perhaps what I have felt most deeply. At every form that miscarries in the trenches, at every outline, metaphor, or prayer crushed under steel, the eternal loses a round. Conscious that I cannot stand aloof from my time, I have decided to be an integral part of it. This is why I esteem the individual only because he strikes me as ridiculous and humiliated. Knowing that there are no victorious causes, I have a liking for lost causes: they require an uncontaminated soul, equal to its defeat as to its temporary victories. For anyone who feels bound up with this world’s fate, the clash of civilizations has something agonizing about it. I have made that anguish mine at the same time that I wanted to join in. Between history and the eternal I have chosen history because I like certainties. Of it, at least, I am certain, and how can I deny this force crushing me?

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"I have watched refined intellectuals marvel that Dutch painting produced masterpieces during the blood-soaked campaigns of Flanders, or wonder at the mystical poetry composed in Silesia during the horrors of the Thirty Years' War." "Before their admiring gaze, 'eternal spiritual values' supposedly rose untouched above temporal butchery." "Yet history has marched forward since then." "Contemporary artists can no longer afford such detached serenity." "Even if an artist possesses the hard, sheltered heart required for creation, it offers no protection; in our time, every single human being—even the contemplative saint—is drafted into the conflict." "This is perhaps the intuition I have experienced most profoundly." "With every work of art stillborn in the frontline trenches, with every poetic metaphor or devotional prayer crushed beneath industrial steel, the illusion of eternity suffers another crushing defeat." "Recognizing that I cannot retreat into ivory-tower isolation from my era, I have resolved to embed myself entirely within it." "This is why I value the individual: precisely because humanity appears to me both absurdly fragile and degraded by modern machinery." "Knowing that no historical cause is ever permanently victorious, I hold a deep allegiance to lost causes: they demand an uncompromised spirit, as steadfast in inevitable defeat as in transient triumphs." "To anyone who feels their fate bound to the destiny of the world, the violent collision of empires is agonizing." "I have claimed that historical anguish as my own, choosing to step directly into the fray." "Between the mirage of eternity and the harsh reality of historical time, I choose history, because I insist on tangible certainties." "Of historical reality, at least, I am undeniably certain—for how could I possibly deny the very juggernaut that threatens to crush me?"

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Total Mobilization: The Death of the Ivory Tower. In previous centuries, a Vermeer or a Silesian mystic could ignore the war outside their window. In the modern age of total war, every human being is mobilized; steel crushes poetry. The conqueror chooses history over eternity because history is concrete, immediate, and inescapable. He champions 'lost causes' because knowing victory is never eternal demands supreme integrity.

---

### §5 • Original PDF Page 70
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 70):**  
> “There always comes a time when one must choose between contemplation and action. This is called becoming a man. Such wrenches are dreadful. But for a proud heart there can be no compromise. There is God or time, that cross or this sword. This world has a higher meaning that transcends its worries, or nothing is true but those worries. One must live with time and die with it, or else elude it for a greater life. I know that one can compromise and live in the world while believing in the eternal. That is called accepting. But I loathe this term and want all or nothing. If I choose action, don’t think that contemplation is like an unknown country to me. But it cannot give me everything, and, deprived of the eternal, I want to ally myself with time. I do not want to put down to my account either nostalgia or bitterness, and I merely want to see clearly. I tell you, tomorrow you will be mobilized. For you and for me that is a liberation. The individual can do nothing and yet he can do everything. In that wonderful unattached state you understand why I exalt and crush him at one and the same time. It is the world that pulverizes him and I who liberate him. I provide him with all his rights.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"A moment inevitably arrives when a person must choose between detached contemplation and direct action." "This crisis is what marks the transition into full adulthood." "Such ruptures tear at the soul." "Yet for a proud spirit, there can be no comfortable compromise." "There is either God or historical time—the religious cross or the revolutionary sword." "Either this universe possesses a transcendent meaning that redeems human suffering, or nothing is real except that suffering itself." "One must either live and perish within the stream of time, or evade it in pursuit of an imaginary afterlife." "I know well that many choose a lukewarm compromise: living in the physical world while quietly consoling themselves with hopes of eternity." "They call that pragmatic acceptance." "I despise the very word; I demand all or nothing." "If I throw myself into action, do not imagine that intellectual contemplation is foreign territory to me." "Yet contemplation cannot grant me the whole of reality; denied access to eternity, I choose to make common cause with time." "I refuse to burden my account with either backward-looking nostalgia or impotent bitterness; my sole ambition is to see reality without blinders." "Mark my words: tomorrow, you too will be mobilized into the historical struggle." "For both of us, that conscription is a profound liberation." "Stripped of cosmic purpose, the individual is utterly helpless, yet simultaneously capable of everything." "In that state of absolute detachment, you can see why I glorify the individual even while acknowledging his insignificance." "It is the blind universe that grinds him to dust; it is I who grant him existential freedom." "I restore to him the full inventory of his earthly rights."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Cross or the Sword: The Refusal of Compromise. Camus rejects the intellectual cowardice of straddling both worlds—enjoying earthly privileges while banking on heavenly salvation. The absurd demands an absolute choice: either the universe has transcendent meaning, or mortal history is all we have. By choosing the sword of historical engagement, the conqueror liberates man from cosmic servitude.

---

### §6 • Original PDF Page 71
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 71):**  
> “Conquerors know that action is in itself useless. There is but one useful action, that of remaking man and the earth. I shall never remake men. But one must do ‘as if.’ For the path of struggle leads me to the flesh. Even humiliated, the flesh is my only certainty. I can live only on it. The creature is my native land. This is why I have chosen this absurd and ineffectual effort. This is why I am on the side of the struggle. The epoch lends itself to this, as I have said. Hitherto the greatness of a conqueror was geographical. It was measured by the extent of the conquered territories. There is a reason why the word has changed in meaning and has ceased to signify the victorious general. The greatness has changed camp. It lies in protest and the blind-alley sacrifice. There, too, it is not through a preference for defeat. Victory would be desirable. But there is but one victory, and it is eternal. That is the one I shall never have. That is where I stumble and cling. A revolution is always accomplished against the gods, beginning with the revolution of Prometheus, the first of modern conquerors. It is man’s demands made against his fate; the demands of the poor are but a pretext. Yet I can seize that spirit only in its historical act, and that is where I make contact with it. Don’t assume, however, that I take pleasure in it: opposite the essential contradiction, I maintain my human contradiction. I establish my lucidity in the midst of what negates it. I exalt man before what crushes him, and my freedom, my revolt, and my passion come together then in that tension, that lucidity, and that vast repetition.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"Genuine conquerors know that political action is, in the cosmic sense, utterly futile." "There is only one action that would truly matter: to fundamentally remake human nature and the earth." "I will never succeed in remaking humanity." "Yet one must act 'as if' such transformation were possible." "For the path of active struggle binds me directly to physical existence." "Even when degraded and broken, mortal flesh remains my only undeniable certainty." "I can draw sustenance from nothing else; living humanity is my only homeland." "This is why I have committed myself to this absurd, inconclusive struggle." "This is why I stand unconditionally on the side of human resistance." "Our historical era demands this stance, as I have already shown." "In the past, a conqueror's glory was purely geographical, measured by the acreage of annexed soil." "There is a profound reason why that title has shifted meaning and no longer designates a victorious general." "True conquest has switched sides." "Today, greatness resides in active resistance and the lucid sacrifice made inside a historical cul-de-sac." "Here again, this is not born from a morbid infatuation with failure." "Definitive victory would be magnificent." "Yet there is only one ultimate victory, and that victory is eternal—the very triumph I can never possess." "That is the boundary against which I stumble, and to which I stubbornly cling." "Every authentic revolution is fundamentally waged against the gods, beginning with the revolt of Prometheus, the earliest modern conqueror." "It represents humanity's defiance hurled against cosmic destiny; economic demands are merely the immediate pretext." "Yet I can apprehend that spirit of rebellion only when it crystallizes in historical action; that is where I engage it directly." "Do not imagine, however, that I indulge in cheap romanticism: confronting the cosmic contradiction of existence, I hold fast to my human integrity." "I establish my clarity directly in the face of what seeks to destroy it." "I elevate human dignity before the forces that crush it—and my freedom, my revolt, and my passion coalesce in that unyielding tension, that radical clarity, and that endless, defiant repetition."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Promethean Revolution (Conquest Redefined). The conqueror is no longer an imperial general conquering geography; he is the Promethean rebel defying cosmic fate. Political revolution is ostensibly about economic grievances, but at its deepest metaphysical level, it is humanity's protest against mortality and cosmic silence. Knowing that ultimate victory is impossible, the conqueror fights anyway out of loyalty to mortal flesh.

---

### §7 • Original PDF Page 72
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 72):**  
> “Yes, man is his own end. And he is his only end. If he aims to be something, it is in this life. Now I know it only too well. Conquerors sometimes talk of vanquishing and overcoming. But it is always ‘overcoming oneself’ that they mean. You are well aware of what that means. Every man has felt himself to be the equal of a god at certain moments. At least, this is the way it is expressed. But this comes from the fact that in a flash he felt the amazing grandeur of the human mind. The conquerors are merely those among men who are conscious enough of their strength to be sure of living constantly on those heights and fully aware of that grandeur. It is a question of arithmetic, of more or less. The conquerors are capable of the more. But they are capable of no more than man himself when he wants. This is why they never leave the human crucible, plunging into the seething soul of revolutions.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"Yes, human beings are their own end and purpose." "Indeed, they are their only purpose." "If a person is to achieve significance, it must be realized strictly within this mortal life." "I understand this now with absolute clarity." "Conquerors occasionally use the language of vanquishing and overcoming." "Yet what they truly mean is overcoming themselves." "You understand perfectly what that signifies." "Every human being has experienced moments where they felt equal to a god." "At least, that is how the intuition is traditionally phrased." "In reality, that sensation arises because in a sudden flash of insight, one perceives the staggering majesty of human consciousness." "Conquerors are simply those rare individuals whose awareness of their own capacity is strong enough to dwell permanently upon those heights, fully conscious of that majesty." "It is purely a matter of arithmetic—of degree, of more or less." "Conquerors are capable of sustaining that intensity longer." "Yet they are capable of nothing beyond what any human being can achieve whenever they genuinely resolve to do so." "This is why they never desert the human battleground, choosing instead to plunge directly into the incandescent core of revolution."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Man as His Own Sole End. Overcoming external enemies is a trivial distraction; the conqueror's true victory is overcoming his own fear, apathy, and hunger for transcendent illusions. Feeling 'like a god' is simply experiencing the full, uncompromised power of human consciousness.

---

### §8 • Original PDF Page 72
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 72):**  
> “There they find the creature mutilated, but they also encounter there the only values they like and admire, man and his silence. This is both their destitution and their wealth. There is but one luxury for them—that of human relations. How can one fail to realize that in this vulnerable universe everything that is human and solely human assumes a more vivid meaning? Taut faces, threatened fraternity, such strong and chaste friendship among men—these are the true riches because they are transitory. In their midst the mind is most aware of its powers and limitations. That is to say, its efficacy. Some have spoken of genius. But genius is easy to say; I prefer the intelligence. It must be said that it can be magnificent then. It lights up this desert and dominates it. It knows its obligations and illustrates them. It will die at the same time as this body. But knowing this constitutes its freedom.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"In the heart of that struggle, conquerors witness humanity battered and mutilated, yet they also encounter the only realities they respect and revere: mortal human beings and their stoic silence." "In this silence resides both their profound destitution and their sovereign wealth." "They permit themselves only a single luxury: the warmth of authentic human solidarity." "How could anyone fail to see that within this defenseless universe, whatever is human—and exclusively human—acquires an intensely vivid significance?" "Weather-beaten faces, solidarity forged under common peril, the fierce and uncorrupted friendship between comrades—these are life's genuine treasures, precious precisely because they are perishable." "In the crucible of shared struggle, human consciousness becomes acutely aware of its genuine strengths and its inescapable limitations." "That is to say, it discovers its practical power." "Commentators love to invoke the word 'genius.'" "Yet 'genius' is a cheap label; I prefer clear-eyed intelligence." "And that intelligence can indeed achieve true magnificence." "It illuminates the barren desert of existence and asserts mastery over it." "It recognizes its mortal duties and gives them living form." "It will perish at the exact instant this body draws its final breath." "Yet the lucid recognition of that mortality is the very foundation of its freedom."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Fragile Fraternity in the Desert (The Solidarity of Comrades). Because the universe is silent and cold, human connection becomes infinitely precious. Comradeship under mortal threat—the 'taut faces' of fighters—is valuable precisely because it will not last forever. Intelligence recognizes its own finitude, and that lucidity constitutes its sovereign freedom.

---

### §9 • Original PDF Page 73
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 73):**  
> “We are not ignorant of the fact that all churches are against us. A heart so keyed up eludes the eternal, and all churches, divine or political, lay claim to the eternal. Happiness and courage, retribution or justice are secondary ends for them. It is a doctrine they bring, and one must subscribe to it. But I have no concern with ideas or with the eternal. The truths that come within my scope can be touched with the hand. I cannot separate from them. This is why you cannot base anything on me: nothing of the conqueror lasts, not even his doctrines.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"We are fully aware that every established church stands aligned against us." "A heart keyed to such fierce temporal intensity escapes the grip of eternity—and every orthodox church, whether theological or political, stakes its power on the eternal." "For them, earthly happiness and personal courage, moral vindication and social justice are merely secondary instruments." "What they bring is an inflexible dogma, and they demand that everyone submit to it." "Yet I have no use for abstract ideological systems or promises of eternity." "The only truths that hold value for me are those I can grasp with my own two hands." "I cannot sever my allegiance from concrete reality." "This is why you cannot erect an enduring monument upon my shoulders: nothing the conqueror achieves is permanent, not even his own ideas."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** All Churches—Divine or Secular-Totalitarian—Demand Surrender. The conqueror rejects both the theological Church and totalitarian political parties (the secular churches of communism/fascism). Both demand that the living individual sacrifice their immediate mortal happiness for an abstract dogma promising future salvation. The absurd rebel trusts only what can be touched with the hand.

---

### §10 • Original PDF Page 73
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 73):**  
> “At the end of all that, despite everything, is death. We know also that it ends everything. This is why those cemeteries all over Europe, which obsess some among us, are hideous. People beautify only what they love, and death repels us and tires our patience. It, too, is to be conquered. The last Carrara, a prisoner in Padua emptied by the plague and besieged by the Venetians, ran screaming through the halls of his deserted palace: he was calling on the devil and asking him for death. This was a way of overcoming it. And it is likewise a mark of courage characteristic of the Occident to have made so ugly the places where death thinks itself honored. In the rebel’s universe, death exalts injustice. It is the supreme abuse.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"At the conclusion of every endeavor, waiting despite everything, is death." "And we know beyond doubt that death terminates all things." "This is why the sprawling cemeteries across Europe, which haunt the minds of so many, are so hideous." "Human beings adorn and beautify only what they love; death disgusts us and exhausts our patience." "Death, too, must be met with defiance." "The last Prince of Carrara, trapped as a prisoner inside a Padua devastated by plague and besieged by the Venetian army, sprinted shrieking through the echoing corridors of his deserted palace, summoning the Devil and demanding to be struck down." "That mad fury was his way of defying death." "And it is equally a testament to Western courage to have rendered deliberately ugly the burial grounds where death presumes to be revered." "Within the universe of the rebel, death is not a sacred mystery, but the ultimate vindication of injustice." "It is the supreme outrage committed against human existence."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Death as the Supreme Outrage (The Prince of Carrara). Unlike Christian theology which sanctifies death as a portal to eternal life, the absurd rebel views death as the ultimate physical outrage and cosmic injustice. The ugly, unadorned Western cemetery reflects honest horror; the Prince of Carrara running screaming through his plague-emptied palace exemplifies defiant fury rather than pious resignation.

---

### §11 • Original PDF Page 73
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 73):**  
> “Others, without compromising either, have chosen the eternal and denounced the illusion of this world. Their cemeteries smile amid numerous flowers and birds. That suits the conqueror and gives him a clear image of what he has rejected. He has chosen, on the contrary, the black iron fence or the potter’s field. The best among the men of God occasionally are seized with fright mingled with consideration and pity for minds that can live with such an image of their death. Yet those minds derive their strength and justification from this. Our fate stands before us and we provoke him. Less out of pride than out of awareness of our ineffectual condition. We, too, sometimes feel pity for ourselves. It is the only compassion that seems acceptable to us: a feeling that perhaps you hardly understand and that seems to you scarcely virile. Yet the most daring among us are the ones who feel it. But we call the lucid ones virile and we do not want a strength that is apart from lucidity.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
"Other thinkers, equally uncompromising, have chosen the path of eternity and denounced this earthly world as a hollow illusion." "Their landscaped cemeteries seem to smile with lush flowers and the singing of birds." "That pastoral decor suits the conqueror well enough, providing him with a stark portrait of the comforting illusions he has firmly rejected." "He chooses instead the stark black iron railing and the pauper's burial plot." "Even the most perceptive men of faith are occasionally gripped by dread, mixed with reluctant respect and sorrow, when contemplating minds capable of living with such an unvarnished expectation of annihilation." "Yet it is from this very dread that such minds draw their resolve and moral vindication." "Our mortal destiny looms directly before us, and we look it in the eye and provoke it." "We do so not out of theatrical arrogance, but out of absolute lucidity regarding our helpless condition." "We, too, occasionally feel a deep compassion for our own fragile state." "It is the only pity we find acceptable: an emotion you may struggle to understand, and which might strike you as insufficiently resolute." "Yet it is the most courageous among us who experience it most keenly." "For we reserve the word 'resolute' for those who live in radical clarity, and we reject any form of strength divorced from truth."

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Pauper's Grave and Lucid Compassion. Religious cemeteries are made pretty with flowers and birds to mask death; the absurd rebel prefers the raw, unadorned iron fence and the common pauper's grave. Facing total annihilation without flinching requires authentic courage. Self-compassion is not weakness; it is the tender, lucid recognition of humanity's precarious, doomed beauty.

---

### §12 • Original PDF Page 74
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 74):**  
> Let me repeat that these images do not propose moral codes and involve no judgments: they are sketches. They merely represent a style of life. The lover, the actor, or the adventurer plays the absurd. But equally well, if he wishes, the chaste man, the civil servant, or the president of the Republic. It is enough to know and to mask nothing. In Italian museums are sometimes found little painted screens that the priest used to hold in front of the face of condemned men to hide the scaffold from them. The leap in all its forms, rushing into the divine or the eternal, surrendering to the illusions of the everyday or of the idea—all these screens hide the absurd. But there are civil servants without screens, and they are the ones of whom I mean to speak.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Let me reiterate that these portraits do not constitute a moral system, nor do they pronounce ethical judgments: they are illustrative sketches. They depict a distinct style of existence. The persistent lover, the consummate actor, and the historical adventurer embody the absurd. Yet the chaste ascetic, the mundane government bureaucrat, or the president of a republic can inhabit the absurd just as authentically. The sole requirement is to perceive reality clearly and conceal nothing from oneself. In Italian museums, one occasionally encounters small painted handheld screens that priests once held before the eyes of condemned prisoners on their march to execution, shielding them from the sight of the scaffold. Every form of the existential leap—whether fleeing into theological dogmas, retreating into secular utopias, or drowning oneself in daily bureaucratic routine—serves as one of these painted screens to hide the sight of the absurd. Yet there are clerks and bureaucrats who refuse these screens, and it is of them that I ultimately speak.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Priest's Painted Screen (The Archetype of Evasion). In Renaissance Italy, priests held painted panels before the eyes of condemned men so they wouldn't see the guillotine or gallows. Camus turns this into an unforgettable concrete anchor: all metaphysics, religious dogmas, and political utopias are painted screens held up to shield dying humans from the scaffold of existence. The absurd man pushes the screen aside to look directly at the scaffold.

---

### §13 • Original PDF Page 74
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 74):**  
> I have chosen the most extreme ones. At this level the absurd gives them a royal power. It is true that those princes are without a kingdom. But they have this advantage over others: they know that all royalties are illusory. They know that is their whole nobility, and it is useless to speak in relation to them of hidden misfortune or the ashes of disillusion. Being deprived of hope is not despairing. The flames of earth are surely worth celestial perfumes. Neither I nor anyone can judge them here. They are not striving to be better; they are attempting to be consistent. If the term “wise man” can be applied to the man who lives on what he has without speculating on what he has not, then they are wise men. One of them, a conqueror but in the realm of mind, a Don Juan but of knowledge, an actor but of the intelligence, knows this better than anyone: “You nowise deserve a privilege on earth and in heaven for having brought to perfection your dear little meek sheep; you nonetheless continue to be at best a ridiculous dear little sheep with horns and nothing more—even supposing that you do not burst with vanity and do not create a scandal by posing as a judge.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
I have focused here upon the most extreme examples. At this heightened pitch, the absurd confers upon them a sovereign authority. It is true that these princes possess no physical realm. Yet they hold this decisive advantage over the rest of humanity: they know that all worldly and heavenly kingdoms are mere illusions. They recognize that this lucidity constitutes their entire nobility, and it is pointless to pity them or talk of secret despair and the bitter ashes of disillusion. To live without hope is not at all the same as living in despair. The tangible fires of this earth are unquestionably worth more than all the hypothetical perfumes of paradise. Neither I nor anyone else has the standing to sit in judgment upon them. They are not striving toward conventional moral improvement; they are laboring to achieve internal consistency. If the title of 'sage' can properly be applied to the person who lives entirely upon what they possess without gambling on what they do not have, then these individuals are true sages. One of them—a conqueror within the territory of thought, a Don Juan of knowledge, an actor of the intellect—expressed this truth better than anyone: 'You deserve no special privilege on earth or in heaven simply because you have fattened and perfected your meek little sheep; you remain, at best, an absurd little horned sheep and nothing more—even assuming you do not swell with self-righteous vanity and cause a scandal by setting yourself up as a moral judge.'

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Princes Without a Kingdom: Nietzsche's Sheep. Camus crowns the absurd heroes (Don Juan, the Actor, the Conqueror) as 'princes without a kingdom.' Living without hope is not despair; earthly flames eclipse heavenly vapors. Citing Nietzsche (Thus Spoke Zarathustra), Camus mocks conventional moralists who mistake their domesticated, herd virtues ('meek little sheep') for cosmic righteousness.

---

### §14 • Original PDF Page 75
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 75):**  
> In any case, it was essential to restore to the absurd reasoning more cordial examples. The imagination can add many others, inseparable from time and exile, who likewise know how to live in harmony with a universe without future and without weakness. This absurd, godless world is, then, peopled with men who think clearly and have ceased to hope. And I have not yet spoken of the most absurd character, who is the creator.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
In any case, it was necessary to ground the abstract logic of the absurd in these warmer, more tangible human archetypes. The imagination could easily supply many others, bound equally to the constraints of time and earthly exile, who likewise understand how to live in harmony with a universe that offers no future and tolerates no weakness. This absurd, godless cosmos is populated, then, by human beings who think with radical clarity and have entirely ceased to live on hope. And I have not yet addressed the most absurd figure of all: the creative artist.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Bridge to Part III: The Creator. Camus closes Part II by summarizing the common thread of the three archetypes: they are lucid individuals who have abandoned hope to inhabit time and exile fully. Having examined the seducer, the actor, and the conqueror, Camus sets the stage for the supreme absurd hero of Part III: the Creator.

---

## 8. Philosophy and Fiction (pp. 76–82)
*Part III: Absurd Creation*

### §1 • Original PDF Page 76
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 76):**  
> All those lives maintained in the rarefied air of the absurd could not persevere without some profound and constant thought to infuse its strength into them. Right here, it can be only a strange feeling of fidelity. Conscious men have been seen to fulfill their task amid the most stupid of wars without considering themselves in contradiction. This is because it was essential to elude nothing. There is thus a metaphysical honor in enduring the world’s absurdity. Conquest or play-acting, multiple loves, absurd revolt are tributes that man pays to his dignity in a campaign in which he is defeated in advance.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
All lives sustained within the rarefied atmosphere of the absurd would quickly collapse without some deep, steadfast conviction to lend them endurance. Here, that conviction can only be a peculiar sense of fidelity. Lucid men have carried out their duties in the midst of the most senseless wars without sensing any contradiction in their conduct. This is because their primary obligation was to evade nothing. There is an undeniable metaphysical honor in enduring the absurdity of the world. Conquest, theatrical performance, numerous loves, and absurd defiance are the tributes human beings pay to their own dignity in a struggle where defeat is guaranteed from the outset.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Camus defines 'metaphysical honor': conscious persistence in an unwinnable struggle. The absurd archetypes (the conqueror, the actor, Don Juan) do not persist expecting salvation; their dignity lies precisely in maintaining lucid defiance in a campaign already lost.

---

### §2 • Original PDF Page 76
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 76):**  
> It is merely a matter of being faithful to the rule of the battle. That thought may suffice to sustain a mind; it has supported and still supports whole civilizations. War cannot be negated. One must live it or die of it. So it is with the absurd: it is a question of breathing with it, of recognizing its lessons and recovering their flesh. In this regard the absurd joy par excellence is creation. “Art and nothing but art,” said Nietzsche; “we have art in order not to die of the truth.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
It is simply a matter of honoring the rules of engagement. That single principle can sustain a mind; indeed, it has anchored entire civilizations. War cannot be wished away. One must either endure it or perish from it. So it is with the absurd: one must learn to breathe within it, heed its instruction, and give tangible flesh to its insights. In this respect, the supreme absurd joy is artistic creation. As Nietzsche famously declared: 'Art and nothing but art; we have art so that we do not perish from the truth.'

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Artistic creation as the supreme absurd triumph. Just as one cannot pretend war does not exist, one cannot wish away cosmic meaninglessness. Creation is the lucid refusal to flee reality while transforming cold truth into vital form.

---

### §3 • Original PDF Page 76
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 76):**  
> In the experience that I am attempting to describe and to stress on several modes, it is certain that a new torment arises wherever another dies. The childish chasing after forgetfulness, the appeal of satisfaction are now devoid of echo. But the constant tension that keeps man face to face with the world, the ordered delirium that urges him to be receptive to everything leave him another fever. In this universe the work of art is then the sole chance of keeping his consciousness and of fixing its adventures. Creating is living doubly. The groping, anxious quest of a Proust, his meticulous collecting of flowers, of wallpapers, and of anxieties, signifies nothing else. At the same time, it has no more significance than the continual and imperceptible creation in which the actor, the conqueror, and all absurd men indulge every day of their lives. All try their hands at miming, at repeating, and at re-creating the reality that is theirs. We always end up by having the appearance of our truths. All existence for a man turned away from the eternal is but a vast mime under the mask of the absurd. Creation is the great mime.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
In the lived experience I am exploring across its various forms, whenever one torment subsides, another inevitably takes its place. The naive scramble for distraction and the craving for permanent comfort no longer resonate. Yet the unyielding tension confronting man with the universe—this disciplined delirium compelling him to welcome every sensation—ignites a new fever within him. Within such an existence, the work of art represents the sole vehicle for sustaining consciousness and recording its trials. To create is to live twice over. The tentative, agonizing quest of Marcel Proust—his obsessive cataloging of hawthorn blossoms, vintage wallpapers, and inner anxieties—expresses nothing less. Simultaneously, it possesses no more ultimate cosmic importance than the ongoing, silent self-creation performed daily by the actor, the conqueror, and every lucid individual. All of them strive to mimic, rehearse, and recreate the immediate reality allotted to them. In the end, we inevitably take on the physical shape of the truths we inhabit. For anyone who has turned away from the illusion of eternity, life is merely a vast performance under the mask of the absurd. Artistic creation is the consummate mime.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Creation as 'living doubly' without claiming cosmic superiority. Proust's grand novel has no more eternal merit than an actor's fleeting gesture or a lover's passion. Art is not an eternal monument, but a lucid mime of mortal experience.

---

### §4 • Original PDF Page 77
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 77):**  
> Such men know to begin with, and then their whole effort is to examine, to enlarge, and to enrich the ephemeral island on which they have just landed. But first they must know. For the absurd discovery coincides with a pause in which future passions are prepared and justified. Even men without a gospel have their Mount of Olives. And one must not fall asleep on theirs either. For the absurd man it is not a matter of explaining and solving, but of experiencing and describing. Everything begins with lucid indifference.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Such men begin with awareness, and their subsequent labor is to explore, expand, and cultivate the transient island upon which they have been cast. Yet consciousness must come first. The revelation of the absurd marks a reflective pause during which future passions are forged and validated. Even those who recognize no gospel endure their own Garden of Gethsemane on the Mount of Olives. And they, too, are forbidden to slumber during the watch. For the absurd mind, the task is never to explain or resolve existence, but to experience and describe it. Everything originates in lucid indifference.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The shift from explanation to description. The absurd thinker does not solve the riddle of the universe; like Christ on the Mount of Olives, he stays awake during the vigil of mortal isolation. The goal is descriptive fidelity rather than metaphysical dogma.

---

### §5 • Original PDF Page 77
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 77):**  
> Describing—that is the last ambition of an absurd thought. Science likewise, having reached the end of its paradoxes, ceases to propound and stops to contemplate and sketch the ever virgin landscape of phenomena. The heart learns thus that the emotion delighting us when we see the world’s aspects comes to us not from its depth but from their diversity. Explanation is useless, but the sensation remains and, with it, the constant attractions of a universe inexhaustible in quantity. The place of the work of art can be understood at this point.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Description remains the ultimate ambition of absurd thought. Science itself, once driven to the outer boundary of its paradoxes, abandons metaphysical speculation to observe and map the pristine landscape of physical phenomena. The soul discovers that the joy we derive from contemplating the world springs not from some hidden cosmic depth, but from sheer sensory diversity. While explanations prove futile, sensory experience endures, accompanied by the endless allure of a universe inexhaustible in its sheer volume. It is here that the true role of the work of art becomes evident.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Phenomenology over metaphysics. Camus aligns absurd philosophy with empirical science: both abandon claims of deciphering ultimate essences and instead document surface appearances and quantitative variety.

---

### §6 • Original PDF Page 77
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 77):**  
> It marks both the death of an experience and its multiplication. It is a sort of monotonous and passionate repetition of the themes already orchestrated by the world: the body, inexhaustible image on the pediment of temples, forms or colors, number or grief. It is therefore not indifferent, as a conclusion, to encounter once again the principal themes of this essay in the wonderful and childish world of the creator. It would be wrong to see a symbol in it and to think that the work of art can be considered at last as a refuge for the absurd. It is itself an absurd phenomenon, and we are concerned merely with its description. It does not offer an escape for the intellectual ailment. Rather, it is one of the symptoms of that ailment which reflects it throughout a man’s whole thought. But for the first time it makes the mind get outside of itself and places it in opposition to others, not for it to get lost but to show it clearly the blind path that all have entered upon. In the time of the absurd reasoning, creation follows indifference and discovery. It marks the point from which absurd passions spring and where the reasoning stops. Its place in this essay is justified in this way.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The work of art signals both the culmination of an experience and its vibrant multiplication. It constitutes a passionate, rhythmic repetition of themes already sounded by the world: the human body, endlessly sculpted upon temple pediments, the interplay of forms and hues, mathematical order and human grief. It is entirely fitting, therefore, that the core themes of this inquiry reappear within the marvelous, uncomplicated realm of artistic creation. Yet it would be an error to treat art as a transcendent symbol or to mistake the artistic work for an escape hatch from absurdity. Art is itself an absurd phenomenon, and our responsibility is simply to describe it. It provides no cure for the intellectual malady. Instead, it is a primary symptom of that condition, projecting the crisis across the entire canvas of human thought. Yet for the first time, creation compels the mind to venture beyond its private borders and confront the world, not to lose itself, but to illuminate the dead-end path upon which all mortals tread. Within the trajectory of absurd reasoning, creation succeeds discovery and detached contemplation. It marks the exact threshold where absurd passions erupt and abstract deduction reaches its limit. This alone justifies its presence in this essay.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Art is a symptom of the absurd, not a cure. Camus warns against treating art as secular salvation. The artwork does not redeem our mortality; it merely externalizes and dramatizes our shared confrontation with the void.

---

### §7 • Original PDF Page 78
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 78):**  
> It will suffice to bring to light a few themes common to the creator and the thinker in order to find in the work of art all the contradictions of thought involved in the absurd. Indeed, it is not so much identical conclusions that prove minds to be related as the contradictions that are common to them. So it is with thought and creation. I hardly need to say that the same anguish urges man to these two attitudes. This is where they coincide in the beginning. But among all the thoughts that start from the absurd, I have seen that very few remain within it. And through their deviations or infidelities I have best been able to measure what belonged to the absurd. Similarly I must wonder: is an absurd work of art possible?

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
It will be enough to illuminate several themes shared by the creator and the philosopher to uncover within art every contradiction inherent in absurd thought. Indeed, what binds kindred minds together is not a shared set of doctrines, but the shared paradoxes they wrestle with. Such is the kinship between contemplation and artistic creation. Both stances, it goes without saying, are propelled by the identical existential anguish. At their origin, they are one. Yet among all the philosophies that commence with the absurd, very few maintain their fidelity to it. It was through their evasions and intellectual betrayals that I was able to chart the exact boundaries of the absurd. Now I must pose an identical question: Is an authentically absurd work of art possible?

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The shared origin of philosophy and art, and the core test: Can an artwork remain absurd without betraying its premises through false transcendence or smuggled hope?

---

### §8 • Original PDF Page 78
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 78):**  
> It would be impossible to insist too much on the arbitrary nature of the former opposition between art and philosophy. If you insist on taking it in too limited a sense, it is certainly false. If you mean merely that these two disciplines each have their peculiar climate, that is probably true but remains vague. The only acceptable argument used to lie in the contradiction brought up between the philosopher enclosed within his system and the artist placed before his work. But this was pertinent for a certain form of art and of philosophy which we consider secondary here. The idea of an art detached from its creator is not only outmoded; it is false. In opposition to the artist, it is pointed out that no philosopher ever created several systems. But that is true in so far, indeed, as no artist ever expressed more than one thing under different aspects. The instantaneous perfection of art, the necessity for its renewal—this is true only through a preconceived notion. For the work of art likewise is a construction and everyone knows how monotonous the great creators can be. For the same reason as the thinker, the artist commits himself and becomes himself in his work. That osmosis raises the most important of æsthetic problems. Moreover, to anyone who is convinced of the mind’s singleness of purpose, nothing is more futile than these distinctions based on methods and objects. There are no frontiers between the disciplines that man sets himself for understanding and loving. They interlock, and the same anxiety merges them.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
One cannot emphasize too strongly how artificial the traditional divide between art and philosophy truly is. Understood in a narrow, dogmatic sense, the distinction is entirely unfounded. If one simply means that each discipline possesses its own distinctive atmosphere, that may be conceded, though it remains a vague observation. The only defensible historical argument contrasted the philosopher locked within a theoretical system against the artist standing directly before their canvas. Yet that contrast applied only to certain historical forms of art and philosophy that are irrelevant to our inquiry. The notion of art existing in sterile detachment from its maker is not merely obsolete; it is fundamentally false. Critics often claim, against the artist, that no philosopher ever constructed more than one system. Yet that holds true only to the extent that no genuine artist ever articulates more than a single core reality across diverse forms. The belief that art achieves spontaneous perfection while constantly reinventing itself is an unexamined myth. A work of art is a painstaking construction, and every perceptive reader knows how relentlessly consistent—even monotonous—the greatest creators can be. Like the philosopher, the artist commits their entire existence to their work and discovers their identity through it. This profound synthesis raises the fundamental question of aesthetics. Furthermore, to anyone aware of the mind's essential unity, few exercises are more pointless than scholastic debates over academic boundaries and methodologies. No rigid borders divide the disciplines human beings devise to comprehend and cherish existence. They intertwine, fused together by the identical existential anxiety.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Dismantling the false dichotomy between art and philosophy. Both thinkers and artists express a single central vision through iterative variation. System-building in philosophy and world-building in fiction are driven by the same human impulse to master reality.

---

### §9 • Original PDF Page 79
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 79):**  
> It is necessary to state this to begin with. For an absurd work of art to be possible, thought in its most lucid form must be involved in it. But at the same time thought must not be apparent except as the regulating intelligence. This paradox can be explained according to the absurd. The work of art is born of the intelligence’s refusal to reason the concrete. It marks the triumph of the carnal. It is lucid thought that provokes it, but in that very act that thought repudiates itself. It will not yield to the temptation of adding to what is described a deeper meaning that it knows to be illegitimate. The work of art embodies a drama of the intelligence, but it proves this only indirectly. The absurd work requires an artist conscious of these limitations and an art in which the concrete signifies nothing more than itself. It cannot be the end, the meaning, and the consolation of a life. Creating or not creating changes nothing. The absurd creator does not prize his work. He could repudiate it. He does sometimes repudiate it. An Abyssinia suffices for this, as in the case of Rimbaud.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
This foundational premise must be stated clearly. If an absurd work of art is to be realized, the intellect must be present in its most razor-sharp clarity. Yet at the same time, that intellect must never show itself except as the guiding, restraining hand. This apparent paradox is resolved through the logic of the absurd. The artwork is born when reason deliberately refuses to rationalize the concrete world. It represents the victory of physical, sensory reality. Lucid intellect initiates the creative act, yet in that very gesture, reason abdicates its claim to explain. It resists the seductive urge to impose an artificial, transcendent meaning upon what it depicts—a meaning it knows to be illegitimate. The work of art reflects an intellectual drama, but it testifies to this conflict only indirectly. An absurd work demands an artist acutely aware of these boundaries, producing an art where physical reality signifies nothing beyond itself. It can never serve as the ultimate purpose, the meaning, or the consoling prize of an existence. Whether one creates or abandons creation alters nothing fundamental. The absurd artist refuses to idolize their own output. They remain capable of renouncing it entirely. Indeed, they sometimes do walk away. A flight to Abyssinia is sufficient, as Rimbaud proved.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The aesthetic paradox of the absurd: Art is born when lucid intellect intentionally surrenders its impulse to explain, allowing physical reality to speak without symbolic baggage. The creator maintains absolute detachment, ready to abandon art entirely (like Rimbaud fleeing to Africa).

---

### §10 • Original PDF Page 79
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 79):**  
> At the same time a rule of æsthetics can be seen in this. The true work of art is always on the human scale. It is essentially the one that says “less.” There is a certain relationship between the global experience of the artist and the work that reflects that experience, between Wilhelm Meister and Goethe’s maturity. That relationship is bad when the work aims to give the whole experience in the lace-paper of an explanatory literature. That relationship is good when the work is but a piece cut out of experience, a facet of the diamond in which the inner luster is epitomized without being limited. In the first case there is overloading and pretension to the eternal. In the second, a fecund work because of a whole implied experience, the wealth of which is suspected. The problem for the absurd artist is to acquire this savoir-vivre which transcends savoir-faire. And in the end, the great artist under this climate is, above all, a great living being, it being understood that living in this case is just as much experiencing as reflecting. The work then embodies an intellectual drama. The absurd work illustrates thought’s renouncing of its prestige and its resignation to being no more than the intelligence that works up appearances and covers with images what has no reason. If the world were clear, art would not exist.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Simultaneously, a fundamental aesthetic principle emerges from this stance. The genuine work of art remains strictly on a human scale. Above all, it is the work that practices restraint—the art that says 'less.' A delicate relationship links an artist’s total life experience to the creation reflecting it, such as the bond between Wilhelm Meister and Goethe’s mature years. That relationship falters when an author attempts to package their entire philosophy inside the ornate wrapping of an explanatory novel. The relationship succeeds when the artwork is merely a carved fragment of lived experience—a single facet of a diamond whose inner radiance is concentrated without being confined. In the first instance, one finds intellectual clutter and an arrogant claim to eternal truth. In the second, one discovers a fertile work rich with tacit experience, whose hidden depth is felt rather than preached. The challenge for the absurd artist is to cultivate this wisdom of living that transcends mere technical craft. Ultimately, a great creator in this landscape is first and foremost a fully alive human being—understanding that living demands both raw experience and lucid reflection. The work then embodies a profound drama of the mind. The absurd creation dramatizes thought surrendering its royal pretensions, accepting its modest role as the craftsman that shapes appearances and garments in vivid imagery that which has no cosmic reason. If the world were transparent, art would not exist.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The art of saying 'less' and the aphorism 'If the world were clear, art would not exist.' Great art is restrained, concrete, and unpretentious; it exists solely because the universe is opaque and refuses to yield rational answers.

---

### §11 • Original PDF Page 80
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 80):**  
> I am not speaking here of the arts of form or color in which description alone prevails in its splendid modesty. Expression begins where thought ends. Those adolescents with empty eyesockets who people temples and museums—their philosophy has been expressed in gestures. For an absurd man it is more educative than all libraries. Under another aspect the same is true for music. If any art is devoid of lessons, it is certainly music. It is too closely related to mathematics not to have borrowed their gratuitousness. That game the mind plays with itself according to set and measured laws takes place in the sonorous compass that belongs to us and beyond which the vibrations nevertheless meet in an inhuman universe. There is no purer sensation. These examples are too easy. The absurd man recognizes as his own these harmonies and these forms.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
I am not referring here to painting or sculpture, where pure visual depiction reigns with magnificent modesty. Genuine artistic expression begins precisely where didactic thought ends. The ancient statues of hollow-eyed youths populating temples and museums express their philosophy through posture and gesture alone. For an absurd observer, those mute figures offer deeper instruction than entire libraries of theory. In a different register, the identical truth governs music. If any art form is entirely free of moralizing lectures, it is surely music. It is too intimately bound to mathematics not to have inherited its pure, non-utilitarian freedom. That rhythmic game the mind plays with sound according to strict laws unfolds within a human acoustic boundary, beyond which sound waves dissolve into an indifferent universe. No sensation is purer. Yet these examples are almost too obvious. The absurd individual readily recognizes their own spirit in these harmonies and sculptural forms.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Pure form vs. thesis: Visual art and music achieve absurd purity effortlessly because they convey no doctrinal lessons; their meaning resides entirely in surface rhythm and physical form.

---

### §12 • Original PDF Page 80
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 80):**  
> But I should like to speak here of a work in which the temptation to explain remains greatest, in which illusion offers itself automatically, in which conclusion is almost inevitable. I mean fictional creation. I propose to inquire whether or not the absurd can hold its own there.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Instead, I want to examine an art form where the temptation to explain is overwhelming, where comforting illusions present themselves at every turn, and where moral conclusions seem almost unavoidable. I speak of fiction. My purpose is to determine whether the absurd can preserve its integrity within the novel.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The novel as the supreme acid test for absurd philosophy. Unlike music or sculpture, fiction constantly tempts the writer to moralize, explain, and fabricate artificial resolutions.

---

### §13 • Original PDF Page 80
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 80):**  
> To think is first of all to create a world (or to limit one’s own world, which comes to the same thing). It is starting out from the basic disagreement that separates man from his experience in order to find a common ground according to one’s nostalgia, a universe hedged with reasons or lighted up with analogies but which, in any case, gives an opportunity to rescind the unbearable divorce. The philosopher, even if he is Kant, is a creator. He has his characters, his symbols, and his secret action. He has his plot endings. On the contrary, the lead taken by the novel over poetry and the essay merely represents, despite appearances, a greater intellectualization of the art. Let there be no mistake about it; I am speaking of the greatest. The fecundity and the importance of a literary form are often measured by the trash it contains. The number of bad novels must not make us forget the value of the best. These, indeed, carry with them their universe. The novel has its logic, its reasonings, its intuition, and its postulates. It also has its requirements of clarity.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
To think is, first and foremost, to construct a world—or to delineate the boundaries of one's own, which amounts to the same thing. It means beginning with the fundamental rift dividing man from his experience, striving to establish common ground shaped by our longing—a cosmos fortified with rational explanations or illuminated by analogies, designed above all to annul this unbearable divorce. Every philosopher, even an architect of systems like Immanuel Kant, is fundamentally a novelist of ideas. He invents his dramatis personae, his conceptual symbols, and his hidden dramatic stakes. He arranges his dramatic conclusions. Conversely, the historical dominance of the novel over poetry and the essay reflects, contrary to superficial appearances, a profound intellectualization of storytelling. Make no mistake: I am speaking exclusively of the greatest novelists. The vitality and cultural weight of any literary genre are often judged by the mountains of mediocre pulp produced in its name. Yet the deluge of bad fiction must never blind us to the supreme worth of the masterpiece. Great novels carry an entire, self-contained universe within them. The novel possesses its own rigorous logic, its dialectical arguments, its intuitive leaps, and its foundational axioms. It also imposes its own demanding standards of clarity.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Philosophers are creators of fiction; great novelists are builders of philosophical worlds. Kant's categories are conceptual characters in a drama designed to repair the rift between mind and world.

---

### §14 • Original PDF Page 81
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 81):**  
> The classical opposition of which I was speaking above is even less justified in this particular case. It held in the time when it was easy to separate philosophy from its authors. Today when thought has ceased to lay claim to the universal, when its best history would be that of its repentances, we know that the system, when it is worth while, cannot be separated from its author. The Ethics itself, in one of its aspects, is but a long and reasoned personal confession. Abstract thought at last returns to its prop of flesh. And, likewise, the fictional activities of the body and of the passions are regulated a little more according to the requirements of a vision of the world. The writer has given up telling “stories” and creates his universe. The great novelists are philosophical novelists—that is, the contrary of thesis-writers. For instance, Balzac, Sade, Melville, Stendhal, Dostoevsky, Proust, Malraux, Kafka, to cite but a few.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The historic opposition between philosopher and artist is even less defensible here. It survived only as long as people could naively divorce an abstract philosophy from the living person who conceived it. Today, when thought has abandoned its pretension to universal systems—and when the truest history of philosophy is simply the chronicle of its own retractions—we recognize that no significant system can ever be severed from its creator. Even Spinoza’s Ethics, viewed from this angle, is essentially a long, reasoned personal confession. Abstract thought finally returns to its anchor in human flesh. Similarly, the fictional movements of characters’ bodies and passions are disciplined to serve a coherent vision of reality. The serious writer no longer merely tells entertaining tales; they build a distinct universe. The supreme novelists are philosophical novelists—the exact opposite of didactic thesis-writers. Consider Balzac, Sade, Melville, Stendhal, Dostoevsky, Proust, Malraux, and Kafka, to name only a handful.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Philosophy as flesh-and-blood confession. Abstract systems are personal confessions of living men (even Spinoza). The great philosophical novelists (Dostoevsky, Melville, Kafka) do not write didactic propaganda; they embody metaphysical visions in carnal drama.

---

### §15 • Original PDF Page 81
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 81):**  
> But in fact the preference they have shown for writing in images rather than in reasoned arguments is revelatory of a certain thought that is common to them all, convinced of the uselessness of any principle of explanation and sure of the educative message of perceptible appearance. They consider the work of art both as an end and a beginning. It is the outcome of an often unexpressed philosophy, its illustration and its consummation. But it is complete only through the implications of that philosophy. It justifies at last that variant of an old theme that a little thought estranges from life whereas much thought reconciles to life. Incapable of refining the real, thought pauses to mimic it. The novel in question is the instrument of that simultaneously relative and inexhaustible knowledge, so like that of love. Of love, fictional creation has the initial wonder and the fecund rumination.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Their choice to write in concrete imagery rather than abstract syllogisms reveals a conviction shared by them all: that ultimate explanatory theories are useless, and that physical appearance carries a profound, instructive power. They view the work of art simultaneously as a culmination and a genesis. It is the fruit of an implicit philosophy—its living demonstration and its fulfillment. Yet it attains completeness only through the unspoken weight of that worldview. This art validates an ancient maxim: a little philosophy detaches us from life, whereas profound contemplation returns us directly to it. Powerless to transcend the real, thought pauses simply to recreate it. The novel becomes the instrument of a knowledge that is both relative and inexhaustible, remarkably akin to the knowledge discovered in love. Like love, the creation of fiction begins with initial wonder and deepens through fertile, unending contemplation.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Thinking in images rather than syllogisms. 'A little thought estranges from life; much thought reconciles to life.' Great fiction abandons metaphysical abstractions to recreate the physical real, acting like love: inexhaustible, relative, and anchored in wonder.

---

### §16 • Original PDF Page 82
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 82):**  
> These at least are the charms I see in it at the outset. But I saw them likewise in those princes of humiliated thought whose suicides I was later able to witness. What interests me, indeed, is knowing and describing the force that leads them back toward the common path of illusion. The same method will consequently help me here. The fact of having already utilized it will allow me to shorten my argument and to sum it up without delay in a particular example. I want to know whether, accepting a life without appeal, one can also agree to work and create without appeal and what is the way leading to these liberties. I want to liberate my universe of its phantoms and to people it solely with flesh-and-blood truths whose presence I cannot deny. I can perform absurd work, choose the creative attitude rather than another. But an absurd attitude, if it is to remain so, must remain aware of its gratuitousness. So it is with the work of art. If the commandments of the absurd are not respected, if the work does not illustrate divorce and revolt, if it sacrifices to illusions and arouses hope, it ceases to be gratuitous. I can no longer detach myself from it. My life may find a meaning in it, but that is trifling. It ceases to be that exercise in detachment and passion which crowns the splendor and futility of a man’s life.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Such, at least, are the promises I perceive in fiction from the start. Yet I once recognized identical promises in those brilliant existential thinkers whose philosophical suicides I was forced to witness. What compels my attention is identifying and describing the insidious force that lures them back into the comforting traps of illusion. The identical method of analysis will serve me here. Having already established this critique, I can compress my argument and test it immediately against a concrete literary masterpiece. My question is this: having accepted a mortal existence without cosmic appeal, can one also resolve to labor and create without appeal, and what path leads to such freedom? I want to rid my world of supernatural specters and populate it exclusively with tangible, flesh-and-blood truths whose presence cannot be disputed. I can engage in absurd labor and choose the creative path over any other. Yet an absurd posture, if it is to maintain its integrity, must remain perpetually conscious of its own ultimate pointlessness. The exact same law applies to the work of art. If the core demands of the absurd are violated—if the novel fails to dramatize mortal estrangement and defiance, if it surrenders to illusion and rekindles metaphysical hope—it loses its radical gratuitousness. I can no longer hold myself detached from it. My life might seek shelter in its manufactured meaning, but that is a cheap consolation. It ceases to be that supreme exercise in passion and detachment that crowns both the splendor and the futility of human existence.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Creating 'without appeal.' The central aesthetic thesis: Can an artist produce a masterpiece while consciously refusing to smuggle in hope or eternal meaning? If art becomes an idol or an ideological crutch, it forfeits its absurd purity.

---

### §17 • Original PDF Page 82
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 82):**  
> In the creation in which the temptation to explain is the strongest, can one overcome that temptation? In the fictional world in which awareness of the real world is keenest, can I remain faithful to the absurd without sacrificing to the desire to judge? So many questions to be taken into consideration in a last effort. It must be already clear what they signify. They are the last scruples of an awareness that fears to forsake its initial and difficult lesson in favor of a final illusion. What holds for creation, looked upon as one of the possible attitudes for the man conscious of the absurd, holds for all the styles of life open to him.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
In the creative act, where the temptation to explain is most seductive, can an artist master and suppress that urge? Within the imaginary landscape of fiction, where our grasp of the concrete world is at its sharpest, can I stay loyal to the absurd without yielding to the impulse to pass moral judgment? These are the demanding questions that must be confronted in this final effort. Their significance is already evident. They represent the final misgivings of a consciousness terrified of trading its hard-won clarity for one last comforting illusion. And whatever applies to artistic creation—considered here as one possible posture for the absurd individual—applies equally to every other path of life open to human freedom.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The creator's ultimate ethical test: Refusing the desire to judge. Art must depict the friction of life without moralizing or resolving it. The chapter ends by establishing that the laws of absurd creation govern all authentic ways of living.

---

## 9. Kirilov (pp. 83–89)
*Part III: Absurd Creation*

### §1 • Original PDF Page 82
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 82):**  
> What holds for creation, looked upon as one of the possible attitudes for the man conscious of the absurd, holds for all the styles of life open to him. The conqueror or the actor, the creator or Don Juan may forget that their exercise in living could not do without awareness of its mad character. One becomes accustomed so quickly.
