A man wants to earn money in order to be happy, and his whole effort and the best of a life are devoted to the earning of that money. Happiness is forgotten; the means are taken for the end. Likewise, the whole effort of this conqueror will be diverted to ambition, which was but a way toward a greater life. Don Juan in turn will likewise yield to his fate, be satisfied with that existence whose nobility is of value only through revolt. For one it is awareness and for the other, revolt; in both cases the absurd has disappeared. There is so much stubborn hope in the human heart. The most destitute men often end up by accepting illusion. That approval prompted by the need for peace inwardly parallels the existential consent. There are thus gods of light and idols of mud. But it is essential to find the middle path leading to the faces of man.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Whatever holds true for artistic creation—regarded as one possible posture for the lucid individual—applies equally to every style of life open to him. The conqueror, the actor, the artist, or Don Juan can easily forget that their way of living is meaningless without constant awareness of its absurd character. Habit dulls the mind so rapidly. A man sets out to earn money solely to achieve happiness, yet he pours his entire energy and the finest years of his life into accumulating that wealth. Happiness itself is forgotten; the mere means is mistaken for the ultimate end. In the same manner, the conqueror's entire striving becomes deflected into raw political ambition, which was originally only a stepping-stone toward a broader existence. Don Juan, too, eventually succumbs to his fate, growing complacent with an existence whose true nobility derived exclusively from relentless defiance. One loses lucid awareness, the other surrenders revolt; in both cases, the absurd evaporates. Hope clings with obstinate tenacity to the human heart. Even the most destitute men often end by embracing comforting illusions. That inward surrender, born of the craving for peace of mind, mirrors the existential leap of faith. Hence men erect luminous gods of light or bow before crude idols of clay. Yet our duty is to preserve the middle road that leads directly to the living reality of human faces.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The creeping corruption of habit and hope. Camus warns that even absurd heroes (the conqueror, Don Juan) easily fall prey to mechanical habit, mistaking means for ends and relapsing into comforting illusions.

---

### §2 • Original PDF Page 83
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 83):**  
> So far, the failures of the absurd exigence have best informed us as to what it is. In the same way, if we are to be informed, it will suffice to notice that fictional creation can present the same ambiguity as certain philosophies. Hence I can choose as illustration a work comprising everything that denotes awareness of the absurd, having a clear starting-point and a lucid climate. Its consequences will enlighten us. If the absurd is not respected in it, we shall know by what expedient illusion enters in. A particular example, a theme, a creator’s fidelity will suffice, then. This involves the same analysis that has already been made at greater length.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Until now, the breakdowns and compromises of absurd thought have best revealed its true nature to us. Similarly, to understand absurd art, it is enough to recognize that fiction exhibits the exact same philosophical ambiguity as certain existential doctrines. I can therefore select as a case study a literary work possessing every hallmark of absurd awareness, beginning with crystal clarity and unfolding in an atmosphere of radical lucidity. Tracing its consequences will illuminate the problem. If the requirements of the absurd are betrayed within it, we will see precisely through which backdoor illusion sneaks back in. A single concrete example, a central theme, and an inquiry into the creator's fidelity will be sufficient. This applies the identical critical method we have already practiced on formal philosophy.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The method of diagnosing intellectual defection. Just as Camus mapped the leaps of faith in Kierkegaard and Chestov, he now interrogates literature to detect where the novelist covertly reintroduces metaphysical hope.

---

### §3 • Original PDF Page 83
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 83):**  
> I shall examine a favorite theme of Dostoevsky. I might just as well have studied other works. But in this work the problem is treated directly, in the sense of nobility and emotion, as for the existential philosophies already discussed. This parallelism serves my purpose.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
I will examine a recurrent obsession in the work of Fyodor Dostoevsky. I could just as easily have explored other authors. Yet in Dostoevsky, the problem of existential revolt is confronted head-on, with supreme emotional intensity and tragic nobility, matching the existential philosophies we have examined. This exact parallel suits my inquiry perfectly.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Selecting Dostoevsky as the supreme laboratory of existential fiction. Dostoevsky directly dramatizes the boundary between radical metaphysical revolt and Christian surrender.

---

### §4 • Original PDF Page 84
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 84):**  
> All of Dostoevsky’s heroes question themselves as to the meaning of life. In this they are modern: they do not fear ridicule. What distinguishes modern sensibility from classical sensibility is that the latter thrives on moral problems and the former on metaphysical problems. In Dostoevsky’s novels the question is propounded with such intensity that it can only invite extreme solutions. Existence is illusory or it is eternal. If Dostoevsky were satisfied with this inquiry, he would be a philosopher. But he illustrates the consequences that such intellectual pastimes may have in a man’s life, and in this regard he is an artist. Among those consequences, his attention is arrested particularly by the last one, which he himself calls logical suicide. In his Diary of a Writer he mentions this. It is worth while to consider the reasonings by which the logical suicide, because he is not convinced of the immortality of the soul, comes to the following conclusions:

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Every one of Dostoevsky’s protagonists tortures himself over the meaning of life. In this, they are thoroughly modern: they have zero fear of looking ridiculous. What separates modern consciousness from classical sensibility is that the classical mind wrestles with moral dilemmas, whereas the modern mind is consumed by metaphysical dread. In Dostoevsky’s novels, the question of existence is posed with such agonizing force that it admits only radical, absolute answers. Either existence is a meaningless phantom, or it is eternal. Were Dostoevsky content with mere abstract inquiry, he would be simply an academic philosopher. Instead, he dramatizes the catastrophic consequences such questions unleash within a living person's existence—and in this, he proves himself a true artist. Among those consequences, his focus is seized above all by the most extreme outcome, which he terms 'logical suicide.' He outlines this concept in his Diary of a Writer. It is instructive to follow the exact reasoning by which the logical suicide—unconvinced of the soul's immortality—arrives at his final verdict:

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Metaphysical vs. moral sensibility. Classical literature dealt with ethics; modern literature wrestles with cosmic meaning. Dostoevsky is an artist because he shows the physical, deadly consequences of ideas, particularly 'logical suicide.'

---

### §5 • Original PDF Page 84
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 84):**  
> “Since in reply to my questions touching upon happiness, I am told, through the medium of my consciousness, that I cannot be happy except in harmony with the great all, which I cannot conceive and shall never be in a position to conceive, it is evident …”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
'Since, in reply to my questions regarding happiness, my own consciousness informs me that I cannot find peace except in harmony with the cosmic totality—a totality I cannot conceive and will never be capable of grasping—it is self-evident...'

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The first premise of logical suicide: Human consciousness demands harmony with the universe, yet the universe remains inherently incomprehensible to mortal reason.

---

### §6 • Original PDF Page 84
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 84):**  
> “Since, finally, in this connection, I assume both the role of the plaintiff and that of the defendant, of the accused and of the judge, and since I find this comedy on the part of nature utterly stupid, and since I deem it humiliating on my part to accept it … In my indisputable capacity of plaintiff and defendant, of judge and accused, I condemn that nature which, with such impudent nerve, brought me into being in order to suffer—I condemn it to be annihilated with me.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
'Since, finally, under these conditions, I find myself forced to play simultaneously the role of plaintiff and defendant, of the accused and the judge; and since I consider this cosmic charade on the part of nature utterly senseless and degrading to endure... In my indisputable authority as plaintiff and defendant, judge and accused, I condemn that indifferent nature which, with insolent arrogance, created me solely to suffer—and I sentence it to be destroyed along with myself.'

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The indictment of nature. The suicide acts as both prosecutor and judge, declaring that by ending his own life, he annihilates the universe that insolently generated him to suffer.

---

### §7 • Original PDF Page 84
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 84):**  
> There remains a little humor in that position. This suicide kills himself because, on the metaphysical level, he is vexed. In a certain sense he takes his revenge. This is his way of proving that “he won’t be taken in.” It is known, however, that the same theme is embodied, but with the most wonderful generality, in Kirilov of The Possessed, likewise an advocate of logical suicide. Kirilov the engineer declares somewhere that he wants to take his life because “it is his idea.” It is clear that this is a question of a proper reason. He is preparing for death for an idea, for a thought. It is a superior suicide. Successively, in scenes in which Kirilov’s mask is gradually illuminated, the fatal thought inspiring him is revealed to us. The engineer, in fact, resumes the reasonings of the Diary. He feels that God is necessary and that he must exist. But he knows that he does not exist and cannot exist. “Why don’t you understand,” he exclaims, “that this is reason enough for killing oneself?” That attitude also entails for him some of the absurd consequences. Through indifferent generosity he accepts having his death utilized for the benefit of a cause he despises. Stavrogin: “I began to realize this evening that you might be in earnest after all.” Kirilov: “I am in earnest.” Stavrogin: “Did you think of it today?” Kirilov: “No, long ago. Ever since I can remember, I have thought about it.” He prepares his deed with that mixed feeling of revolt and passion. “I want to shoot myself,” he says, “because this is my great idea, my supreme liberty.” It is no longer a question of revenge, but of revolt. Kirilov is, then, an absurd character—with the essential reservation, however, that he is about to die. He himself explains that contradiction, but in such a way that he reveals at the same time the absurd secret in all its purity. Indeed, he adds to his fatal logic an extraordinary ambition which gives the character its full perspective: he wants to kill himself to become god.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
There is a grim, ironical humor in this stance. This individual takes his life because, on a cosmic level, he feels deeply insulted. In a sense, he is exacting revenge. It is his definitive method of proving that he will not be played for a fool. Yet this same theme achieves its most magnificent, universal expression in Kirillov from Demons, who is likewise a practitioner of logical suicide. Kirillov, the civil engineer, declares plainly that he intends to end his life because 'it is his idea.' Clearly, this represents a deliberate, calculated conviction. He prepares to die for an idea—for a pure principle of thought. This is suicide elevated to intellectual mastery. Step by step, through scenes where Kirillov’s internal visage is progressively unveiled, his governing obsession is laid bare. The engineer essentially adopts the syllogisms from Dostoevsky's Diary. He senses that God is indispensable and ought to exist. Yet he knows with absolute certainty that God does not exist and cannot exist. 'How can you fail to understand,' he cries, 'that this alone is a sufficient reason to kill oneself?' This radical posture produces in him several characteristic absurd behaviors. With chilling indifference, he even allows his impending death to be exploited by a political conspiracy he completely disdains. Stavrogin asks: 'I realized only tonight that you are genuinely serious.' Kirillov replies: 'I am entirely serious.' Stavrogin presses: 'Did you decide this today?' Kirillov answers: 'No, long ago. For as long as I can remember, I have thought of nothing else.' He orchestrates his demise with that unique mixture of defiance and cold passion. 'I will shoot myself,' he insists, 'because it is my great idea—my supreme assertion of freedom.' This is no longer petty personal revenge; it is metaphysical revolt. Kirillov stands as a quintessential absurd hero—bearing, however, the fatal contradiction that he chooses physical death. Yet he explains this apparent contradiction himself, revealing the absurd core in all its terrifying purity. He attaches to his lethal logic a staggering metaphysical ambition that unveils the true scope of his character: he resolves to kill himself in order to become God.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Introducing Kirillov's 'superior suicide.' Unlike the emotional suicide driven by heartbreak or despair, Kirillov kills himself for an intellectual principle: God ought to exist, yet does not exist; therefore man must assert absolute sovereignty by choosing death.

---

### §8 • Original PDF Page 85
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 85):**  
> The reasoning is classic in its clarity. If God does not exist, Kirilov is god. If God does not exist, Kirilov must kill himself. Kirilov must therefore kill himself to become god. That logic is absurd, but it is what is needed. What remains interesting, however, is to give a meaning to that divinity brought down to earth. That amounts to clarifying the premise: “If God does not exist, I am god.” If God exists, all depends on him and we can do nothing against his will. If he does not exist, everything depends on us. For Kirilov, as for Nietzsche, to kill God is to become god oneself; it is to realize on this earth the eternal life of which the Gospel speaks. But if this metaphysical crime is enough for man’s fulfillment, why add suicide? Why kill oneself and leave this world after having won freedom? That is contradictory. Kirilov is well aware of this, for he adds: “If you feel that, you are a tsar and, far from killing yourself, you will live covered with glory.” Yet the divinity in question is completely terrestrial. “For three years,” says Kirilov, “I sought the attribute of my divinity and I’ve found it: the attribute of my divinity is independence.” Now can be seen the meaning of Kirilov’s premise: “If God does not exist, I am god.” To become god is merely to be free on this earth, not to serve an immortal being. Above all, of course, it is drawing all the painful consequences from that painful independence. If God does not exist, we are responsible for everything. It is to realize that our actions no longer have any supernatural guarantee. Consequently, we must take the full weight upon our shoulders. To give words their specific sense would, in this instance, be ridiculous.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The dialectical reasoning is classical in its razor clarity. If God does not exist, Kirillov is God. If God does not exist, Kirillov must kill himself. Therefore, Kirillov must take his own life in order to become God. This logic appears paradoxical, yet it follows its premises to the bitter end. What remains vital is deciphering what divinity means once brought down to mortal earth. It requires unpacking Kirillov’s foundational axiom: 'If God does not exist, then I am God.' If God exists, everything is subject to His sovereign will, and our autonomy is an illusion. If He does not exist, then everything rests entirely upon human shoulders. For Kirillov, exactly as for Friedrich Nietzsche, to dethrone God is to assume divinity oneself—to claim directly upon this earth the eternal sovereignty promised in religious scripture. Yet if this metaphysical liberation suffices to fulfill human destiny, why crown it with self-destruction? Why slaughter oneself and abandon the world the very moment absolute freedom has been conquered? On the surface, this appears completely contradictory. Kirillov is acutely aware of the paradox, adding: 'If you truly feel this, you are a king, and far from killing yourself, you will live cloaked in glory.' Yet the divinity he envisions is strictly terrestrial and mortal. 'For three years,' Kirillov confesses, 'I searched for the true attribute of my divinity, and I found it: the attribute of my divinity is absolute independence.' Here the true meaning of his declaration becomes transparent: 'If God does not exist, I am God.' To become God simply means to live entirely autonomous on this earth, bowing before no celestial master. Above all, it means bearing every agonizing consequence of that terrible independence. If God does not exist, we bear solitary responsibility for the entire universe. It means recognizing that our choices possess zero cosmic guarantees or moral safety nets. Consequently, human beings must shoulder the full existential burden themselves. To interpret Kirillov’s words in a traditional mystical sense would, in this context, be absurd.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The deconstruction of 'If God does not exist, I am God.' Divinity is unmasked not as supernatural omnipotence, but as radical mortal autonomy. Killing God means accepting total responsibility without celestial appeals or safety nets.

---

### §9 • Original PDF Page 86
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 86):**  
> Kirilov himself helps us to understand. In reply to a question from Stavrogin, he specifies that he is not talking about a god-man. One might think that this is aimed at distinguishing himself from Christ. In reality it is a matter of annexing him. Kirilov in fact fancies for a moment that Jesus at his death did not find himself in Paradise. He found out then that his torture had been useless. “The laws of nature,” says the engineer, “made Christ live in the midst of falsehood and die for a falsehood.” Solely in this sense Jesus personifies the whole human drama. He is the complete man, he who realized the most absurd condition. He is not the God-man, but the man-god. And, like him, each of us can be crucified and victimized—and is to a certain degree.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Kirillov provides the key to his own paradox. In response to Stavrogin’s probing, he clarifies that he is not speaking of a 'God-man.' One might assume this distinction is intended to separate him from Jesus of Nazareth. In reality, Kirillov seeks to reclaim Jesus as his brother in absurdity. Kirillov imagines that when Christ died on the cross, he did not awake in Paradise. He discovered at the final moment that his agonizing martyrdom had been in vain. 'The laws of nature,' the engineer asserts, 'forced Christ to live amidst illusion and to die for a lie.' In this precise sense alone, Jesus embodies the total tragedy of the human condition. He represents the archetypal mortal: the man who lived the absurd condition to its ultimate extremity. He was not the divine God made man, but the mortal man striving to become God. And like him, every conscious human being is crucified and betrayed by the indifferent silence of nature.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The tragic secularization of Christ. Kirillov identifies with Jesus not as a divine redeemer, but as the supreme martyr of cosmic absurdity: a mortal who died for a nonexistent heaven, discovering on the cross that nature is indifferent.

---

### §10 • Original PDF Page 86
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 86):**  
> But if this metaphysical crime is enough for man’s fulfillment, why add suicide? Why kill oneself and leave this world after having won freedom? That is contradictory. Kirilov is well aware of this, for he adds: “If you feel that, you are a tsar and, far from killing yourself, you will live covered with glory.” But men in general do not know it. They do not feel “that.” As in the time of Prometheus, they entertain blind hopes. They need to be shown the way and cannot do without preaching. Consequently, Kirilov must kill himself out of love for humanity. He must show his brothers a royal and difficult path on which he will be the first. It is a pedagogical suicide. Kirilov sacrifices himself, then. But if he is crucified, he will not be victimized. He remains the man-god, convinced of a death without future, imbued with evangelical melancholy. “I,” he says, “am unhappy because I am obliged to assert my freedom.” Kirilov’s pistol shot will be the signal for the last revolution. Thus, it is not despair that urges him to death, but love of his neighbor for his own sake. Before terminating in blood an indescribable spiritual adventure, Kirilov makes a remark as old as human suffering: “All is well.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Yet if overthrowing God is sufficient for human dignity, why crown it with a bullet to the brain? Why take one's own life and abandon existence the very instant absolute liberation has been won? The act seems glaringly contradictory. Kirillov recognizes this perfectly, noting: 'If you comprehend this truth, you become a sovereign king, and rather than killing yourself, you will live cloaked in splendor.' Yet ordinary humanity remains blind to this insight. Mankind does not grasp 'that.' Exactly as in the myth of Prometheus, human beings cling desperately to blind, metaphysical hopes. They require a visible prophet to show them the path, unable to awaken without an undeniable demonstration. Therefore, Kirillov resolves to kill himself out of pure, selfless love for mankind. He must demonstrate to his fellow men a sovereign, terrifying path on which he will be the first pioneer. His death is a pedagogical suicide. Kirillov offers himself as a sacrificial vanguard. Yet though he goes to his own cross, he refuses to play the helpless victim. He remains the 'man-god,' lucidly assured that death is total annihilation, yet steeped in tragic, evangelical compassion. 'I am wretched,' he laments, 'because I am bound by duty to proclaim my absolute freedom.' Kirillov’s gunshot is designed as the starting bell for the final human revolution—the liberation from cosmic terror. Thus, it is not despair or misery that drives him to self-destruction, but an overflowing love for his fellow mortals. And before ending this extraordinary spiritual drama in a puddle of blood, Kirillov utters words as ancient as human suffering: 'All is well.'

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Pedagogical suicide: Kirillov kills himself not from despair, but as a secular savior. To liberate humanity from the ancient terror of death and the phantom of God, he must sacrifice himself to prove that man is the sole master of his destiny.

---

### §11 • Original PDF Page 87
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 87):**  
> This theme of suicide in Dostoevsky, then, is indeed an absurd theme. Let us merely note before going on that Kirilov reappears in other characters who themselves set in motion additional absurd themes. Stavrogin and Ivan Karamazov try out the absurd truths in practical life. They are the ones liberated by Kirilov’s death. They try their skill at being tsars. Stavrogin leads an “ironic” life, and it is well known in what regard. He arouses hatred around him. And yet the key to the character is found in his farewell letter: “I have not been able to detest anything.” He is a tsar in indifference. Ivan is likewise by refusing to surrender the royal powers of the mind. To those who, like his brother, prove by their lives that it is essential to humiliate oneself in order to believe, he might reply that the condition is shameful. His key word is: “Everything is permitted,” with the appropriate shade of melancholy. Of course, like Nietzsche, the most famous of God’s assassins, he ends in madness. But this is a risk worth running, and, faced with such tragic ends, the essential impulse of the absurd mind is to ask: “What does that prove?” They establish logic unto death, exaltation, “dreadful” freedom, the glory of the tsars become human. All is well, everything is permitted, and nothing is hateful—these are absurd judgments. But what an amazing creation in which those creatures of fire and ice seem so familiar to us. The passionate world of indifference that rumbles in their hearts does not seem at all monstrous to us. We recognize in it our everyday anxieties.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Suicide in Dostoevsky’s universe is therefore an undeniably absurd theme. Before advancing, we must observe that Kirillov’s metaphysical rebellion reemerges in other characters who embody related absurd postures. Nikolai Stavrogin and Ivan Karamazov attempt to live out these absurd realizations in concrete action. They represent those who have been theoretically liberated by Kirillov’s sacrifice. They test their prowess at reigning as sovereign kings of their own lives. Stavrogin lives an existence of cold, corrosive irony—and its destructive fallout is infamous. He incites intense hatred everywhere he walks. Yet the definitive cipher to his soul appears in his final suicide note: 'I have never been capable of hating anything.' He is a sovereign king of pure indifference. Ivan Karamazov claims identical kingship by adamantly refusing to surrender the intellectual sovereignty of reason. To those who, like his saintly brother Alyosha, argue that one must humiliate human reason to accept faith, Ivan retorts that such spiritual surrender is shameful. His defining motto remains 'Everything is permitted'—tinged with an unmistakable undercurrent of melancholy. Inevitably, like Nietzsche—the most famous assassin of God—Ivan collapses into madness. Yet this is an occupational hazard of radical honesty, and confronted with such tragic ruins, the absurd mind simply asks: 'What does that prove?' Dostoevsky’s novels construct a ruthless logic pursued to the point of death: ecstasy, terrifying freedom, and the tragic majesty of mortals crowned as kings. 'All is well,' 'Everything is permitted,' and 'I can hate nothing'—these are quintessential absurd verdicts. Yet what a breathtaking imaginative triumph it is that these characters of fire and ice feel so intimately recognizable to us. The stormy universe of indifference churning within their breasts does not strike us as alien or monstrous. We recognize within their torment the magnified reflection of our own daily anxieties.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The absurd triumvirate: Kirillov (suicide to prove freedom), Stavrogin (sovereign indifference), and Ivan Karamazov ('Everything is permitted'). Their tragic ends (madness, suicide) do not refute their logic; they confirm the terrifying weight of lucidity.

---

### §12 • Original PDF Page 88
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 88):**  
> Yet what is his conclusion? Two quotations will show the complete metaphysical reversal that leads the writer to other revelations. The argument of the one who commits logical suicide having provoked protests from the critics, Dostoevsky in the following installments of the Diary amplifies his position and concludes thus: “If faith in immortality is so necessary to the human being (that without it he comes to the point of killing himself), it must therefore be the normal state of humanity. Since this is the case, the immortality of the human soul exists without any doubt.” Then again in the last pages of his last novel, at the conclusion of that gigantic combat with God, some children ask Aliocha: “Karamazov, is it true what religion says, that we shall rise from the dead, that we shall see one another again?” And Aliocha answers: “Certainly, we shall see one another again, we shall joyfully tell one another everything that has happened.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Yet where does Dostoevsky himself ultimately land? Two passages reveal the total metaphysical reversal through which the novelist retreats toward transcendent consolation. When his essay on logical suicide provoked furious outrage from contemporary critics, Dostoevsky clarified his stance in subsequent issues of the Diary, deducing: 'If faith in eternal life is so indispensable to human sanity that without it man is driven to destroy himself, then immortality must represent the natural, intended state of humanity.' 'Because this need is universal, the immortality of the human soul must exist beyond any shadow of a doubt.' And again, in the closing chapter of his final masterpiece, at the climax of that monumental wrestling match with God, schoolboys gather around Alyosha asking: 'Karamazov, is it true what faith teaches—that we will rise from the dead, that we will see each other again?' And Alyosha reassures them: 'Without question, we will see each other again, and we will joyfully recount everything that has happened.'

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The author's philosophical retreat. Camus exposes Dostoevsky's intellectual leap of faith: arguing that because man cannot endure mortality without despair, immortality MUST exist (the pragmatic fallacy), culminating in Alyosha's promise of resurrection.

---

### §13 • Original PDF Page 88
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 88):**  
> Thus Kirilov, Stavrogin, and Ivan are defeated. The Brothers Karamazov replies to The Possessed. And it is indeed a conclusion. Aliocha’s case is not ambiguous, as is that of Prince Muichkin. Ill, the latter lives in a perpetual present, tinged with smiles and indifference, and that blissful state might be the eternal life of which the Prince speaks. On the contrary, Aliocha clearly says: “We shall meet again.” There is no longer any question of suicide and of madness. What is the use, for anyone who is sure of immortality and of its joys? Man exchanges his divinity for happiness. “We shall joyfully tell one another everything that has happened.” Thus again Kirilov’s pistol rang out somewhere in Russia, but the world continued to cherish its blind hopes. Men did not understand “that.” Consequently, it is not an absurd novelist addressing us, but an existential novelist. Here, too, the leap is touching and gives its nobility to the art that inspires it. It is a stirring acquiescence, riddled with doubts, uncertain and ardent. Speaking of The Brothers Karamazov, Dostoevsky wrote: “The chief question that will be pursued throughout this book is the very one from which I have suffered consciously or unconsciously all life long: the existence of God.” It is hard to believe that a novel sufficed to transform into joyful certainty the suffering of a lifetime. One commentator correctly pointed out that Dostoevsky is on Ivan’s side and that the affirmative chapters took three months of effort whereas what he called “the blasphemies” were written in three weeks in a state of excitement. There is not one of his characters who does not have that thorn in the flesh, who does not aggravate it or seek a remedy for it in sensation or immortality. In any case, let us remain with this doubt. Here is a work which, in a chiaroscuro more gripping than the light of day, permits us to seize man’s struggle against his hopes. Having reached the end, the creator makes his choice against his characters. That contradiction thus allows us to make a distinction. It is not an absurd work that is involved here, but a work that propounds the absurd problem.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Thus Kirillov, Stavrogin, and Ivan Karamazov are ultimately repudiated by their creator. The Brothers Karamazov is written as an explicit refutation of Demons. And it represents a definitive theological verdict. Alyosha’s spiritual certainty contains none of Prince Myshkin’s holy ambiguity. The epileptic Myshkin inhabits a perpetual present bathed in luminous indifference—a state that closely mirrors the terrestrial eternal life of which the Gospel speaks. Alyosha, by contrast, proclaims without hesitation: 'We will meet again.' Suicide and existential madness are banished from the horizon. For what reason would anyone take their own life if they are guaranteed the everlasting bliss of heaven? Man abdicates his tragic divinity in exchange for celestial security. 'We will joyfully recount everything that has happened.' Once more, Kirillov’s pistol echoed through the Russian night, but the world moved on, nursing its stubborn illusions. Mankind failed to comprehend his sacrifice. Therefore, the writer speaking to us is not an absurd creator, but an existential novelist who executes a leap of faith. Yet here too, the leap is deeply moving, lending tragic nobility to the literature born from it. It is an agonizing surrender, besieged by skepticism, trembling and feverish. Reflecting upon The Brothers Karamazov, Dostoevsky admitted: 'The central question running through every page of this novel is the identical torment that has haunted my conscience all my life: the existence of God.' It is difficult to believe that the pages of a novel could magically transform the lifelong anguish of a titan into serene religious faith. A perceptive critic rightly observed that Dostoevsky's instinctive sympathy lay with Ivan: the pious refutations required three months of agonizing labor, whereas the fiery 'blasphemies' were drafted in three weeks of incandescent inspiration. Every single one of his characters carries that agonizing thorn in the flesh, inflaming the wound or seeking an antidote through sensory excess or dreams of eternity. Regardless, let us honor the unresolved tension of his struggle. We are left with a masterpiece whose chiaroscuro—more haunting than midday sun—captures the desperate combat between man and his own craving for hope. Arriving at the final crossroads, the creator sides against his own greatest creations. That crucial betrayal allows us to establish a vital aesthetic distinction. Dostoevsky's fiction is not an absurd work; it is a work that powerfully dramatizes the absurd problem only to flee from its conclusion.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The creator choosing against his characters. Dostoevsky's emotional genius was on the side of Ivan's blasphemies, but his theological terror forced a Christian resolution. It is a work that poses the absurd question with genius, but resolves it through an existential leap.

---

### §14 • Original PDF Page 89
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 89):**  
> Dostoevsky’s reply is humiliation, “shame” according to Stavrogin. An absurd work, on the contrary, does not provide a reply; that is the whole difference. Let us note this carefully in conclusion: what contradicts the absurd in that work is not its Christian character, but rather its announcing a future life. It is possible to be Christian and absurd. There are examples of Christians who do not believe in a future life. In regard to the work of art, it should therefore be possible to define one of the directions of the absurd analysis that could have been anticipated in the preceding pages. It leads to propounding “the absurdity of the Gospel.” It throws light upon this idea, fertile in repercussions, that convictions do not prevent incredulity. On the contrary, it is easy to see that the author of The Possessed, familiar with these paths, in conclusion took a quite different way. The surprising reply of the creator to his characters, of Dostoevsky to Kirilov, can indeed be summed up thus: existence is illusory and it is eternal.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Dostoevsky’s final answer is holy humiliation—what Stavrogin calls 'shame.' An authentically absurd creation, by contrast, offers zero consoling answers; that is the absolute divide. We must emphasize this carefully: what betrays the absurd in Dostoevsky’s work is not that it is Christian, but that it promises an afterlife. It is entirely possible to be Christian and remain faithful to the absurd. History offers instances of Christians who harbored no belief in personal immortality. With respect to aesthetics, one could explore a radical direction of absurd analysis hinted at in the preceding chapters. It would explore 'the absurdity of the Gospel' itself. It illuminates the profound insight that intense religious longing does not preclude radical skepticism. Yet the author of Demons, though intimately acquainted with these precipices, chose an entirely different escape route in the end. The startling answer the creator offers his characters—the reply Dostoevsky delivers to Kirillov—can be distilled into a single formula: existence is an illusion, and it is eternal.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The decisive aesthetic verdict: Absurd art provides no answers. Dostoevsky's betrayal of the absurd is not his Christianity, but his promise of an afterlife. He reconciles the world by declaring existence both illusory and eternal.

---

## 10. Ephemeral Creation (pp. 90–93)
*Part III: Absurd Creation*

### §1 • Original PDF Page 90
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 90):**  
> At this point I perceive, therefore, that hope cannot be eluded forever and that it can beset even those who wanted to be free of it. This is the interest I find in the works discussed up to this point. I could, at least in the realm of creation, list some truly absurd works. But everything must have a beginning. The object of this quest is a certain fidelity. The Church has been so harsh with heretics only because she deemed that there is no worse enemy than a child who has gone astray. But the record of Gnostic effronteries and the persistence of Manichean currents have contributed more to the construction of orthodox dogma than all the prayers. With due allowance, the same is true of the absurd. One recognizes one’s course by discovering the paths that stray from it. At the very conclusion of the absurd reasoning, in one of the attitudes dictated by its logic, it is not a matter of indifference to find hope coming back in under one of its most touching guises. That shows the difficulty of the absurd ascesis. Above all, it shows the necessity of unfailing alertness and thus confirms the general plan of this essay.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
At this stage, I recognize that metaphysical hope cannot be evaded effortlessly, and that it can seduce even those who sought most fiercely to be free of it. This is precisely what makes the works analyzed so far profoundly illuminating. I could easily assemble, at least within the domain of art, a catalog of authentically absurd works. Yet every rigorous inquiry must begin at the foundation. The purpose of this entire investigation is an unwavering fidelity to truth. The Church dealt ruthlessly with heretics precisely because she understood that no enemy is more dangerous than an errant child of the faith. Yet the daring provocations of the Gnostics and the tenacity of Manichean currents contributed far more to forging orthodox Christian dogma than centuries of pious prayers. Allowing for differences of scale, the identical principle governs our study of the absurd. One defines the true path by charting every detour that wanders away from it. At the very culmination of absurd reasoning, within an artistic stance demanded by its own logic, it is deeply significant to watch hope slip back inside through one of its most touching disguises. This demonstrates the supreme difficulty of the absurd ascesis. Above all, it reveals why relentless mental alertness is indispensable, thereby confirming the architectural design of this essay.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The dialectical function of heretics and deviations. Hope is an insidious gravity. Just as heresies defined orthodox church dogma, tracing how great artists (like Dostoevsky) retreat into hope illuminates the exacting discipline of maintaining absurd fidelity.

---

### §2 • Original PDF Page 90
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 90):**  
> But if it is still too early to list absurd works, at least a conclusion can be reached as to the creative attitude, one of those which can complete absurd existence. Art can never be so well served as by a negative thought. Its dark and humiliated proceedings are as necessary to the understanding of a great work as black is to white. To work and create “for nothing,” to sculpture in clay, to know that one’s creation has no future, to see one’s work destroyed in a day while being aware that fundamentally this has no more importance than building for centuries—this is the difficult wisdom that absurd thought sanctions. Performing these two tasks simultaneously, negating on the one hand and magnifying on the other, is the way open to the absurd creator. He must give the void its colors.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
While it may be premature to compile a definitive canon of absurd art, we can at least reach a clear conclusion regarding the creative stance—one of the vital disciplines that completes an absurd existence. Art has never been so powerfully vitalized as by a philosophy of conscious refusal. Its austere, humbled methods are as indispensable to grasping a genuine masterpiece as darkness is to revealing light. To labor and create 'for nothing,' to sculpt ephemeral figures in clay, to know that one’s masterpiece possesses no eternal future, and to watch it wash away in a single afternoon while knowing that this loss matters no more than building stone cathedrals for millennia—this is the exacting wisdom sanctioned by the absurd. Executing these two demanding tasks at once—relentlessly negating transcendent illusions on one side while fiercely celebrating the sensory real on the other—is the path charted for the absurd creator. He must paint the void in all its dazzling colors.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Creating 'for nothing' and 'giving the void its colors.' The defining manifesto of absurd aesthetics: sculpting in clay with full knowledge of mortal impermanence. The artist negates transcendent meaning while magnifying immediate sensory beauty.

---

### §3 • Original PDF Page 90
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 90):**  
> This leads to a special conception of the work of art. Too often the work of a creator is looked upon as a series of isolated testimonies. Thus, artist and man of letters are confused. A profound thought is in a constant state of becoming; it adopts the experience of a life and assumes its shape. Likewise, a man’s sole creation is strengthened in its successive and multiple aspects: his works. One after another, they complement one another, correct or overtake one another, contradict one another too. If something brings creation to an end, it is not the victorious and illusory cry of the blinded artist: “I have said everything,” but the death of the creator which closes his experience and the book of his genius.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
This insight leads to a distinctive understanding of the work of art. All too frequently, an author's output is treated as a random collection of isolated, disconnected monuments. When this happens, the genuine artist is confused with the superficial man of letters. A profound idea is in a perpetual state of evolution; it absorbs the lived experience of an entire life and assumes its organic contour. In the same way, an artist's single, unified creation gathers strength across its successive manifestations: his individual books. Appearing one after another, these works complement each other, revise or surpass one another, and frequently contradict each other as well. If anything finally closes the creative process, it is never the triumphant and delusional cry of a blinded artist proclaiming 'I have said everything,' but the physical death of the creator, which seals his lived experience and shuts the book of his genius forever.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The artwork as an organic continuum. An artist does not produce self-contained monuments; each book is an approximation of a single evolving vision, terminated not by intellectual completion, but only by biological death.

---

### §4 • Original PDF Page 91
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 91):**  
> That effort, that superhuman consciousness are not necessarily apparent to the reader. There is no mystery in human creation. Will performs this miracle. But at least there is no true creation without a secret. To be sure, a succession of works can be but a series of approximations of the same thought. But it is possible to conceive of another type of creator proceeding by juxtaposition. Their works may seem to be devoid of interrelations. To a certain degree, they are contradictory. But viewed all together, they resume their natural grouping. From death, for instance, they derive their definitive significance. They receive their most obvious light from the very life of their author. At the moment of death, the succession of his works is but a collection of failures. But if those failures all have the same resonance, the creator has managed to repeat the image of his own condition, to make the air echo with the sterile secret he possesses.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
This sustained discipline and heightened lucidity are not always obvious to the reader. There is no supernatural mystery behind human creation. Conscious will alone performs this marvel. Yet genuine creation is never born without a hidden core. Doubtless, a lifelong sequence of works may be merely a chain of approximations circling a single intuition. Yet one can also imagine another kind of creator who builds entirely through juxtaposition. His various books may appear entirely unrelated on the surface. To a certain extent, they may openly contradict one another. Yet when surveyed as a totality, they snap into their natural constellation. They derive their final, definitive coherence from death itself. They draw their clearest illumination directly from the lived biography of their author. At the hour of death, the catalog of an artist’s works may appear as nothing more than a succession of noble failures. Yet if all those failures reverberate with the identical timbre, the creator has succeeded in projecting the true image of our condition—filling the silence of the world with the sterile secret he bore within.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Creation through will and fertile failure. Art requires no divine muse; will and discipline suffice. Even if every individual book is an incomplete failure, their collective resonance testifies to the heroic human condition.

---

### §5 • Original PDF Page 91
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 91):**  
> The effort to dominate is considerable here. But human intelligence is up to much more. It will merely indicate clearly the voluntary aspect of creation. Elsewhere I have brought out the fact that human will had no other purpose than to maintain awareness. But that could not do without discipline. Of all the schools of patience and lucidity, creation is the most effective. It is also the staggering evidence of man’s sole dignity: the dogged revolt against his condition, perseverance in an effort considered sterile. It calls for a daily effort, self-mastery, a precise estimate of the limits of truth, measure, and strength. It constitutes an ascesis. All that “for nothing,” in order to repeat and mark time. But perhaps the great work of art has less importance in itself than in the ordeal it demands of a man and the opportunity it provides him of overcoming his phantoms and approaching a little closer to his naked reality.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The sheer effort required to command one's material here is immense. Yet human intelligence is capable of far greater feats. It serves primarily to highlight the deliberate, disciplined nature of creation. I have argued earlier that the human will possesses no higher purpose than keeping consciousness alert. Yet maintaining lucidity is impossible without rigorous discipline. Among all the schools of patience and radical clarity, artistic creation is the most formidable. It stands as the dazzling proof of human dignity: an unyielding revolt against our condition, a steadfast perseverance in a labor known to be cosmically sterile. It demands daily devotion, ruthless self-mastery, a precise calibration of the boundaries of truth, proportion, and endurance. It constitutes an authentic secular ascesis. And all of this sustained 'for nothing'—merely to mark time and rehearse existence. Yet perhaps the great work of art matters less for its own sake than for the testing ordeal it imposes upon a human being—the opportunity it grants him to conquer his illusions and stand face to face with naked reality.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Art as an athletic, spiritual ascesis. The masterpiece matters less as an enduring object than as an ethical crucible: a rigorous school of patience and lucidity where the artist masters his demons and confronts unvarnished reality.

---

### §6 • Original PDF Page 92
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 92):**  
> Let there be no mistake in æsthetics. It is not patient inquiry, the unceasing, sterile illustration of a thesis that I am calling for here. Quite the contrary, if I have made myself clearly understood. The thesis-novel, the work that proves, the most hateful of all, is the one that most often is inspired by a smug thought. You demonstrate the truth you feel sure of possessing. But those are ideas one launches, and ideas are the contrary of thought. Those creators are philosophers, ashamed of themselves. Those I am speaking of or whom I imagine are, on the contrary, lucid thinkers. At a certain point where thought turns back on itself, they raise up the images of their works like the obvious symbols of a limited, mortal, and rebellious thought.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Let there be no misunderstanding in our aesthetics. I am not advocating pedagogical pedantry or the endless, sterile demonstration of an ideological thesis. Quite the opposite, if my meaning has been clear. The thesis-novel—the didactic work designed to prove a point, the most repulsive of all literary forms—is almost invariably spawned by smug, self-satisfied minds. The author parades a doctrine they are foolishly confident they possess. Yet those are merely slogans launched into the void, and packaged slogans are the exact opposite of genuine thought. Such writers are merely second-rate philosophers embarrassed by their own trade. The creators I speak of, or whom I envision, are by contrast intensely lucid thinkers. At that decisive frontier where intellect acknowledges its own boundaries, they summon the living images of their art as the vivid emblems of a finite, mortal, and defiant consciousness.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Denouncing the thesis-novel. Camus rejects propaganda and didactic literature. Slogans and ideological proofs are the enemies of thought; authentic absurd art presents concrete, finite images of defiance without moralizing.

---

### §7 • Original PDF Page 92
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 92):**  
> They perhaps prove something. But those proofs are ones that the novelists provide for themselves rather than for the world in general. The essential is that the novelists should triumph in the concrete and that this constitute their nobility. This wholly carnal triumph has been prepared for them by a thought in which abstract powers have been humiliated. When they are completely so, at the same time the flesh makes the creation shine forth in all its absurd luster. After all, ironic philosophies produce passionate works.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Perhaps their stories prove something after all. Yet those proofs are demonstrations the novelists conduct for their own internal clarity, rather than moral lessons preached to the public. The indispensable requirement is that the artist triumph in the tangible, concrete realm—and that this physical victory constitute their nobility. This thoroughly carnal victory is engineered by an intellect that has systematically humbled abstract reason. When abstraction is fully dethroned, the physical presence of the world illuminates the artwork in all its absurd brilliance. In the final analysis, ironic philosophies generate the most passionately vibrant art.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The carnal triumph of the concrete. When abstract reason is humbled, physical reality bursts forth in artistic splendor. Intellectual skepticism (irony) does not produce cold detachment; it generates passionate, sensual engagement.

---

### §8 • Original PDF Page 92
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 92):**  
> Any thought that abandons unity glorifies diversity. And diversity is the home of art. The only thought to liberate the mind is that which leaves it alone, certain of its limits and of its impending end. No doctrine tempts it. It awaits the ripening of the work and of life. Detached from it, the work will once more give a barely muffled voice to a soul forever freed from hope. Or it will give voice to nothing if the creator, tired of his activity, intends to turn away. That is equivalent.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Any philosophy that abandons the illusion of cosmic unity ends by glorifying sheer diversity. And sensory diversity is the native homeland of art. The only thought that truly liberates the intellect is that which leaves it standing in solitary independence, conscious of its frontiers and certain of its approaching death. No dogmatic theology tempts it anymore. It patiently observes the natural ripening of both the artwork and of life. Standing detached from its maker, the finished work gives quiet, clear voice to a soul permanently liberated from hope. Or it will give voice to nothing at all, should the creator grow weary of the craft and decide to walk away. Both outcomes are entirely equivalent.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Diversity over unity, and the freedom of silence. Renouncing cosmic oneness opens up the boundless playground of sensory variety. The absurd artist creates without hope, and walks away into silence with equal indifference.

---

### §9 • Original PDF Page 93
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 93):**  
> Thus, I ask of absurd creation what I required from thought— revolt, freedom, and diversity. Later on it will manifest its utter futility. In that daily effort in which intelligence and passion mingle and delight each other, the absurd man discovers a discipline that will make up the greatest of his strengths. The required diligence, the doggedness and lucidity thus resemble the conqueror’s attitude. To create is likewise to give a shape to one’s fate. For all these characters, their work defines them at least as much as it is defined by them. The actor taught us this: there is no frontier between being and appearing.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Thus, I demand of absurd creation precisely what I demanded of thought: defiance, freedom, and multiplicity. Ultimately, it will reveal its complete cosmic futility. Yet in that daily labor where sharp intelligence and raw passion intertwine and enrich one another, the absurd creator discovers a personal discipline that constitutes his greatest power. The exacting diligence, tenacity, and lucidity demanded by art closely mirror the resolute posture of the conqueror. To create is to chisel a tangible form onto one's own fate. For each of these figures, their work defines their identity every bit as much as they author their work. The actor demonstrated this truth long ago: no real boundary separates what one is from what one performs.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Synthesizing the absurd lives. Creation unites the conqueror's willpower, Don Juan's appetite for multiplicity, and the actor's embodiment. In creating, man chisels his own fate, obliterating the distinction between being and acting.

---

### §10 • Original PDF Page 93
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 93):**  
> Let me repeat. None of all this has any real meaning. On the way to that liberty, there is still a progress to be made. The final effort for these related minds, creator or conqueror, is to manage to free themselves also from their undertakings: succeed in granting that the very work, whether it be conquest, love, or creation, may well not be; consummate thus the utter futility of any individual life. Indeed, that gives them more freedom in the realization of that work, just as becoming aware of the absurdity of life authorized them to plunge into it with every excess.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Let this be repeated with absolute clarity. None of this activity possesses any transcendent, eternal significance. On the journey toward authentic freedom, one final threshold remains to be crossed. The ultimate challenge for these kindred spirits—whether artist or conqueror—is learning to liberate themselves even from their own monumental projects: to accept serenely that the work itself, whether conquest, romance, or artistic creation, could just as easily never have existed; and thereby to embrace the absolute cosmic pointlessness of any individual life. Paradoxically, this radical detachment grants them vastly greater freedom in executing their craft, exactly as realizing the absurdity of existence liberated them to plunge into mortal experience with boundless intensity.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Detachment from one's own masterpiece. The final threshold of absurd freedom: recognizing that even one's greatest creation could just as well never have existed. This detachment does not breed cynicism; it unlocks absolute creative audacity.

---

### §11 • Original PDF Page 93
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 93):**  
> All that remains is a fate whose outcome alone is fatal. Outside of that single fatality of death, everything, joy or happiness, is liberty. A world remains of which man is the sole master. What bound him was the illusion of another world. The outcome of his thought, ceasing to be renunciatory, flowers in images. It frolics—in myths, to be sure, but myths with no other depth than that of human suffering and, like it, inexhaustible. Not the divine fable that amuses and blinds, but the terrestrial face, gesture, and drama in which are summed up a difficult wisdom and an ephemeral passion.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
All that remains is a human destiny whose sole inevitability is physical death. Outside that solitary fatality of our mortality, everything—whether joy, agony, or happiness—is pure liberty. A magnificent world endures of which man is the solitary, undisputed master. The only chains that ever bound him were the ghosts and promises of an imaginary afterlife. The culmination of his thought, having ceased its self-denying retreat, blossoms into vivid, living imagery. It delights in shaping myths—myths possessing no celestial depth beyond that of mortal suffering, and like that suffering, inexhaustibly profound. Not the divine fables of religion that distract and blind our eyes, but the terrestrial face, the human gesture, and the earthly drama in which an exacting wisdom and an ephemeral passion are eternally crowned.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The terrestrial kingdom and mortal myths. The climactic conclusion of Part III: Death is the only inescapable boundary; outside death, total human freedom reigns. Art discards celestial fairy tales to celebrate the mortal face, physical gesture, and transient passion of life on earth.

---

## 11. The Myth of Sisyphus (pp. 95–98)
*Part IV: The Myth of Sisyphus*

### §1 • Original PDF Page 95
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 95):**  
> The gods had condemned Sisyphus to ceaselessly rolling a rock to the top of a mountain, whence the stone would fall back of its own weight. They had thought with some reason that there is no more dreadful punishment than futile and hopeless labor.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The gods had condemned Sisyphus to roll a massive boulder ceaselessly to the top of a mountain, only for the stone to roll back down of its own weight. They reasoned, not without cause, that there is no punishment more terrible than labor that is entirely futile and hopeless.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Camus opens the myth with the psychology of divine punishment: the ultimate torment is not physical agony, but endless exertion permanently stripped of purpose, progress, or conclusion.

---

### §2 • Original PDF Page 95
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 95):**  
> If one believes Homer, Sisyphus was the wisest and most prudent of mortals. According to another tradition, however, he was disposed to practice the profession of highwayman. I see no contradiction in this. Opinions differ as to the reasons why he became the futile laborer of the underworld. To begin with, he is accused of a certain levity in regard to the gods. He stole their secrets. Ægina, the daughter of Æsopus, was carried off by Jupiter. The father was shocked by that disappearance and complained to Sisyphus. He, who knew of the abduction, offered to tell about it on condition that Æsopus would give water to the citadel of Corinth. To the celestial thunderbolts he preferred the benediction of water. He was punished for this in the underworld. Homer tells us also that Sisyphus had put Death in chains. Pluto could not endure the sight of his deserted, silent empire. He dispatched the god of war, who liberated Death from the hands of her conqueror.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
If we trust Homer, Sisyphus was the wisest and most prudent of mortals. According to another tradition, however, he was inclined toward banditry. I see no contradiction here. Opinions differ regarding why he was condemned to be the underworld's futile laborer. To begin with, he was accused of treating the gods with insolent disrespect. He betrayed their secrets. Jupiter abducted Aegina, the daughter of the river god Asopus. Devastated by her disappearance, the father complained to Sisyphus. Sisyphus, knowing of the abduction, offered to reveal the culprit on one condition: that Asopus provide a freshwater spring to the citadel of Corinth. He preferred the tangible blessing of water to the fear of celestial thunderbolts. For this act of defiance, he was sentenced to the underworld. Homer also recounts that Sisyphus actually put Death herself in chains. Pluto could not tolerate the sight of his kingdom lying deserted and silent. He dispatched the god of war, who finally wrestled Death free from the hands of her captor.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Sisyphus's rebellion is rooted in terrestrial solidarity and a visceral passion for life: he chooses water for his mortal city over reverence for Zeus, and chains Death to protect humanity from annihilation.

---

### §3 • Original PDF Page 95
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 95):**  
> It is said also that Sisyphus, being near to death, rashly wanted to test his wife’s love. He ordered her to cast his unburied body into the middle of the public square. Sisyphus woke up in the underworld. And there, annoyed by an obedience so contrary to human love, he obtained from Pluto permission to return to earth in order to chastise his wife. But when he had seen again the face of this world, enjoyed water and sun, warm stones and the sea, he no longer wanted to go back to the infernal darkness. Recalls, signs of anger, warnings were of no avail. Many years more he lived facing the curve of the gulf, the sparkling sea, and the smiles of earth. A decree of the gods was necessary. Mercury came and seized the impudent man by the collar and, snatching him from his joys, led him forcibly back to the underworld, where his rock was ready for him.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
It is also said that when Sisyphus lay near death, he rashly resolved to test his wife's devotion. He ordered her to cast his unburied corpse into the middle of the public square. Sisyphus awoke in the underworld. Outraged by an obedience so contrary to genuine human affection, he obtained Pluto's permission to return to earth to punish his wife. Yet once he beheld the radiant face of this world again—tasting water and sunlight, resting against warm stones, and gazing out at the sea—he refused to return to the infernal darkness. Divine summonses, furious outbursts, and solemn warnings proved entirely useless. For many years he lived on, facing the curve of the bay, the glittering water, and the warmth of the earth. In the end, an absolute decree from Olympus became necessary. Mercury arrived, seized the insolent man by the collar, snatched him away from his pleasures, and dragged him forcibly back to the underworld, where his boulder was waiting.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The second defiance: Tasting the sensory world once more, Sisyphus rejects eternity and divine authority. His revolt is purely immanent—he chooses the warmth of the sun and sea over any promised transcendent afterlife.

---

### §4 • Original PDF Page 96
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 96):**  
> You have already grasped that Sisyphus is the absurd hero. He is, as much through his passions as through his torture. His scorn of the gods, his hatred of death, and his passion for life won him that unspeakable penalty in which the whole being is exerted toward accomplishing nothing. This is the price that must be paid for the passions of this earth. Nothing is told us about Sisyphus in the underworld. Myths are made for the imagination to breathe life into them. As for this myth, one sees merely the whole effort of a body straining to raise the huge stone, to roll it and push it up a slope a hundred times over; one sees the face screwed up, the cheek tight against the stone, the shoulder bracing the claycovered mass, the foot wedging it, the fresh start with arms outstretched, the wholly human security of two earth-clotted hands. At the very end of his long effort measured by skyless space and time without depth, the purpose is achieved. Then Sisyphus watches the stone rush down in a few moments toward that lower world whence he will have to push it up again toward the summit. He goes back down to the plain.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
It is already obvious why Sisyphus is the archetype of the absurd hero. He embodies it as much through his earthly passions as through his punishment. His defiance toward the gods, his hatred of death, and his ferocious passion for life earned him that unspeakable penalty: an entire existence spent straining toward absolute futility. This is the price demanded for loving the things of this earth. The ancient myths tell us almost nothing about his daily existence in the underworld. Yet myths exist precisely for the imagination to animate them. In this myth, what we see is the sheer physical effort of a body straining to lift the colossal stone, rolling it and pushing it up a slope hundreds of times: the contorted face, the cheek pressed hard against the rock, the shoulder wedged under the clay-caked mass, the foot bracing against the earth, the repeated thrust with outstretched arms, and the raw, vulnerable humanity of two mud-stained hands. At the conclusion of this long exertion—measured in skyless space and time without depth—the summit is reached. Then Sisyphus watches the boulder tumble down in a matter of moments into the valley below, from where he will have to push it upward all over again. He turns and walks back down to the plain.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Sisyphus as visceral archetype. Camus de-mythologizes the fable by grounding it in muscular, tactile reality: cheek against stone, mud-caked hands, and repetitive physical strain, transforming metaphysical punishment into concrete human labor.

---

### §5 • Original PDF Page 96
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 96):**  
> It is during that return, that pause, that Sisyphus interests me. A face that toils so close to stones is already stone itself! I see that man going back down with a heavy yet measured step toward the torment of which he will never know the end. That hour like a breathing-space which returns as surely as his suffering, that is the hour of consciousness. At each of those moments when he leaves the heights and gradually sinks toward the lairs of the gods, he is superior to his fate. He is stronger than his rock.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
It is precisely during that return, during that brief pause, that Sisyphus commands my attention. A face that labors in such brutal proximity to stone becomes stone itself. I envision that man descending with a heavy yet rhythmic stride toward an agony that will never end. That interval—a breathing space that returns as reliably as his suffering—is the hour of consciousness. At each of those moments when he departs the summit and descends toward the underworld of the gods, he rises superior to his fate. He is stronger than the rock he must roll.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The pause of consciousness. The descent down the mountain is the philosophical center of the essay. In this brief breathing space, free from the physical weight of the stone, lucid awareness turns a slave of fate into its master.

---

### §6 • Original PDF Page 96
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 96):**  
> If this myth is tragic, that is because its hero is conscious. Where would his torture be, indeed, if at every step the hope of succeeding upheld him? The workman of today works every day in his life at the same tasks, and this fate is no less absurd. But it is tragic only at the rare moments when it becomes conscious. Sisyphus, proletarian of the gods, powerless and rebellious, knows the whole extent of his wretched condition: it is what he thinks of during his descent. The lucidity that was to constitute his torture at the same time crowns his victory. There is no fate that cannot be surmounted by scorn.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
If this myth is genuinely tragic, it is solely because its hero is conscious. Where would the torture be, after all, if at every step he were sustained by the illusion of eventual success? The modern worker labors every day of his life at identical tasks, and his condition is no less absurd. Yet that existence becomes tragic only in those rare moments when he awakens to full consciousness. Sisyphus, the proletarian of the gods, powerless yet defiant, grasps the entire expanse of his wretched condition; that is precisely what occupies his mind during the descent. The radical clarity meant to constitute his torment becomes the very instrument of his triumph. There is no destiny that cannot be overcome by defiant contempt.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The proletarian analogy and the power of scorn. Modern routine is identical to Sisyphus's labor; both are absurd. Lucidity is a double-edged sword: it makes suffering tragic, but conscious contempt disarms the gods' victory.

---

### §7 • Original PDF Page 97
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 97):**  
> If the descent is thus sometimes performed in sorrow, it can also take place in joy. This word is not too much. Again I fancy Sisyphus returning toward his rock, and the sorrow was in the beginning. When the images of earth cling too tightly to memory, when the call of happiness becomes too insistent, it happens that melancholy rises in man’s heart: this is the rock’s victory, this is the rock itself. The boundless grief is too heavy to bear. These are our nights of Gethsemane. But crushing truths perish from being acknowledged. Thus, Œdipus at the outset obeys fate without knowing it. But from the moment he knows, his tragedy begins. Yet at the same moment, blind and desperate, he realizes that the only bond linking him to the world is the cool hand of a girl. Then a tremendous remark rings out: “Despite so many ordeals, my advanced age and the nobility of my soul make me conclude that all is well.” Sophocles’ Œdipus, like Dostoevsky’s Kirilov, thus gives the recipe for the absurd victory. Ancient wisdom confirms modern heroism.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
While this descent is sometimes undertaken in sorrow, it can equally occur in joy. That word is by no means an exaggeration. Once more I picture Sisyphus walking down toward his rock; the sorrow belonged only to the beginning. When memories of the earth cling too stubbornly to the mind, when the longing for past happiness becomes overwhelming, melancholy floods the human heart: this is the rock's true victory; this is the rock itself. That boundless grief feels impossible to bear. These are our personal nights of Gethsemane. Yet crushing truths lose their power the moment they are lucidly acknowledged. At the outset, Oedipus blindly obeys a fate he does not recognize. The moment he learns the truth, his tragedy begins. Yet at that exact instant, blind and desperate, he realizes that his sole anchor to existence is the cool hand of a young girl. Then a magnificent declaration rings out: “Despite all my ordeals, my old age and the nobility of my soul compel me to conclude that all is well.” Sophocles' Oedipus, like Dostoevsky's Kirillov, provides the exact formula for the absurd triumph. Ancient tragic wisdom validates modern existential courage.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Confronting crushing truth destroys its dominion. Melancholy stems from clinging to false hopes or nostalgic regrets. Oedipus's heroic affirmation (“all is well”) demonstrates that embracing tragedy without illusion transforms despair into sovereign peace.

---

### §8 • Original PDF Page 97
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 97):**  
> One does not discover the absurd without being tempted to write a manual of happiness. “What! by such narrow ways—?” There is but one world, however. Happiness and the absurd are two sons of the same earth. They are inseparable. It would be a mistake to say that happiness necessarily springs from the absurd discovery. It happens as well that the feeling of the absurd springs from happiness. “I conclude that all is well,” says Œdipus, and that remark is sacred. It echoes in the wild and limited universe of man. It teaches that all is not, has not been, exhausted. It drives out of this world a god who had come into it with dissatisfaction and a preference for futile sufferings. It makes of fate a human matter, which must be settled among men.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
One cannot discover the absurd without feeling the temptation to write a manual of happiness. “What! Along such a narrow and unforgiving path?” Yet there is only one real world. Happiness and the absurd are twin children of the same earth. They are entirely inseparable. It would be an error to claim that happiness arises automatically from realizing the absurd. Just as often, the sudden intuition of the absurd erupts directly from profound happiness. “I conclude that all is well,” Oedipus declares, and that statement is sacred. It reverberates across the wild, bounded horizon of human life. It teaches that reality is not, and never has been, exhausted. That single affirmation expels from this world any god who arrived bearing grievances and an appetite for futile suffering. It restores destiny entirely to human hands, as a matter to be settled strictly among men.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Reclaiming destiny from the divine. The Absurd does not extinguish joy; it births it. By declaring “all is well,” humanity banishes the concept of divine punishment and reclaims existence as an entirely secular, mortal affair.

---

### §9 • Original PDF Page 97
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 97):**  
> All Sisyphus’ silent joy is contained therein. His fate belongs to him. His rock is his thing. Likewise, the absurd man, when he contemplates his torment, silences all the idols. In the universe suddenly restored to its silence, the myriad wondering little voices of the earth rise up. Unconscious, secret calls, invitations from all the faces, they are the necessary reverse and price of victory. There is no sun without shadow, and it is essential to know the night. The absurd man says yes and his effort will henceforth be unceasing. If there is a personal fate, there is no higher destiny, or at least there is but one which he concludes is inevitable and despicable. For the rest, he knows himself to be the master of his days. At that subtle moment when man glances backward over his life, Sisyphus returning toward his rock, in that slight pivoting he contemplates that series of unrelated actions which becomes his fate, created by him, combined under his memory’s eye and soon sealed by his death. Thus, convinced of the wholly human origin of all that is human, a blind man eager to see who knows that the night has no end, he is still on the go. The rock is still rolling.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The entirety of Sisyphus's silent joy is contained right here. His fate belongs strictly to him. His rock is his own domain. Similarly, when the absurd person looks upon his struggle, he silences every metaphysical idol. In a universe suddenly returned to its majestic silence, thousands of quiet, marveling voices rise up from the earth. Unconscious whispers, secret gestures, invitations from countless human faces—these are the necessary counterpart and the price of his triumph. There is no sunlight without shadow, and one must intimately know the night. The absurd person says yes to life, and his commitment becomes relentless. If an individual destiny exists, there is no transcendent Providence governing it—or at most, only a blind mechanism that he recognizes as inevitable and regards with contempt. Beyond that, he knows he is the sole master of his days. In that decisive instant when a person casts a backward glance across life—as Sisyphus pivots toward his rock—he surveys that succession of unconnected actions that has coalesced into his destiny, shaped by his own choices, bound together by memory, and soon to be sealed by his death. Convinced of the purely human origin of everything human—like a blind man who aches to see yet knows the darkness is infinite—he moves forward. The rock is rolling still.

> [!TIP]
