> **The Philosophical Move:** Total existential autonomy. In silencing the idols, man inherits the silent world. Sisyphus's backward glance affirms that life is an unscripted series of human choices, unified only by personal consciousness and mortal finality.

---

### §10 • Original PDF Page 98
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 98):**  
> I leave Sisyphus at the foot of the mountain! One always finds one’s burden again. But Sisyphus teaches the higher fidelity that negates the gods and raises rocks. He too concludes that all is well. This universe henceforth without a master seems to him neither sterile nor futile. Each atom of that stone, each mineral flake of that night-filled mountain, in itself forms a world. The struggle itself toward the heights is enough to fill a man’s heart. One must imagine Sisyphus happy.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
I leave Sisyphus at the foot of the mountain. A person will always return to his burden. Yet Sisyphus teaches that higher fidelity which repudiates the gods and hoists colossal stones. He, too, concludes that all is well. This universe, freed forever from any celestial master, appears to him neither sterile nor meaningless. Every single atom of that stone, every mineral splinter of that dark mountain, constitutes a complete cosmos in itself. The struggle toward the heights is itself enough to fill a human heart. One must imagine Sisyphus happy.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The climactic apotheosis. Life requires no transcendent justification to be profoundly lived; the lucid, relentless exertion against gravity and mortality is its own ultimate reward. Heroic defiance transforms pointless labor into absolute joy.

---

## Hope and the Absurd in the Work of Franz Kafka (pp. 99–109)
*Appendix*

### §1 • Original PDF Page 99
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 99):**  
> The whole art of Kafka consists in forcing the reader to reread. His endings, or his absence of endings, suggest explanations which, however, are not revealed in clear language but, before they seem justified, require that the story be reread from another point of view. Sometimes there is a double possibility of interpretation, whence appears the necessity for two readings. This is what the author wanted. But it would be wrong to try to interpret everything in Kafka in detail. A symbol is always in general and, however precise its translation, an artist can restore to it only its movement: there is no word-for-word rendering. Moreover, nothing is harder to understand than a symbolic work. A symbol always transcends the one who makes use of it and makes him say in reality more than he is aware of expressing. In this regard, the surest means of getting hold of it is not to provoke it, to begin the work without a preconceived attitude and not to look for its hidden currents. For Kafka in particular it is fair to agree to his rules, to approach the drama through its externals and the novel through its form.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
The entire art of Franz Kafka lies in compelling the reader to reread. His ambiguous conclusions—or his deliberate refusal to provide endings at all—hint at explanations that are never stated directly, demanding that we revisit the narrative from an entirely different vantage point before any interpretation feels earned. At times his narratives sustain two contradictory readings at once, making a second encounter essential. This ambiguity was precisely what the author intended. Yet it would be an error to attempt a mechanical, point-by-point deciphering of every detail. A true symbol always operates in the realm of the general, and no matter how precise our commentary, an artist can only convey its dynamic movement: there can be no literal, one-to-one translation. Furthermore, few things are harder to comprehend than a symbolic work of art. A genuine symbol invariably surpasses the writer who deploys it, expressing far more than the author consciously intended. The most reliable way to approach it, therefore, is not to force a reading upon it, but to begin without preconceptions and refrain from hunting for hidden allegories. With Kafka especially, we must accept the terms he sets: entering the drama through its concrete surfaces and engaging the novel strictly through its external form.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Aesthetics of the Open Symbol. Kafka deliberately constructs open-ended narratives that resist rigid, one-to-one allegorical decoding. True symbols transcend their authors' conscious intentions; they must be experienced through concrete narrative surfaces rather than forced into dogmatic systems.

---

### §2 • Original PDF Page 99
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 99):**  
> At first glance and for a casual reader, they are disturbing adventures that carry off quaking and dogged characters into pursuit of problems they never formulate. In The Trial, Joseph K. is accused. But he doesn’t know of what. He is doubtless eager to defend himself, but he doesn’t know why. The lawyers find his case difficult. Meanwhile, he does not neglect to love, to eat, or to read his paper. Then he is judged. But the courtroom is very dark. He doesn’t understand much. He merely assumes that he is condemned, but to what he barely wonders. At times he suspects just the same, and he continues living. Some time later two well-dressed and polite gentlemen come to get him and invite him to follow them. Most courteously they lead him into a wretched suburb, put his head on a stone, and slit his throat. Before dying the condemned man says merely: “Like a dog.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
To the casual reader at first glance, these are unsettling tales that sweep trembling yet persistent protagonists into the pursuit of problems they never clearly define. In The Trial, Joseph K. is arrested and accused. Yet he never learns what he is charged with. He is naturally eager to mount a defense, but he cannot articulate what he must defend himself against. His lawyers consider his case extraordinarily complicated. Meanwhile, he goes on loving, eating his meals, and reading his morning newspaper. Eventually, judgment is rendered. Yet the courtroom remains plunged in gloom. He understands almost nothing of the proceedings. He simply deduces that he has been sentenced, though he scarcely inquires into the nature of his punishment. At times he suspects the worst, yet he continues his daily routine regardless. Sometime later, two well-dressed, polite gentlemen arrive to collect him, courteously inviting him to accompany them. With impeccable manners, they escort him to a squalid quarry on the outskirts of town, lay his head upon a stone, and plunge a knife into his heart. Drawing his final breath, the condemned man whispers only: “Like a dog.”

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Trial and the horror of the mundane. Camus charts the narrative trajectory of Joseph K., highlighting the terrifying juxtaposition between arbitrary existential condemnation and banal daily routines (reading the newspaper, polite executioners).

---

### §3 • Original PDF Page 100
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 100):**  
> You see that it is hard to speak of a symbol in a tale whose most obvious quality just happens to be naturalness. But naturalness is a hard category to understand. There are works in which the event seems natural to the reader. But there are others (rarer, to be sure) in which the character considers natural what happens to him. By an odd but obvious paradox, the more extraordinary the character’s adventures are, the more noticeable will be the naturalness of the story: it is in proportion to the divergence we feel between the strangeness of a man’s life and the simplicity with which that man accepts it. It seems that this naturalness is Kafka’s. And, precisely, one is well aware what The Trial means. People have spoken of an image of the human condition. To be sure. Yet it is both simpler and more complex. I mean that the significance of the novel is more particular and more personal to Kafka. To a certain degree, he is the one who does the talking, even though it is me he confesses. He lives and he is condemned. He learns this on the first pages of the novel he is pursuing in this world, and if he tries to cope with this, he nonetheless does so without surprise. He will never show sufficient astonishment at this lack of astonishment. It is by such contradictions that the first signs of the absurd work are recognized. The mind projects into the concrete its spiritual tragedy. And it can do so solely by means of a perpetual paradox which confers on colors the power to express the void and on daily gestures the strength to translate eternal ambitions.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
One realizes quickly how difficult it is to discuss symbolism in a story whose most striking quality is its sheer naturalness. Yet naturalness is a deceptive and elusive category. There are narratives where bizarre events strike the reader as perfectly natural. There are others—far rarer—where the protagonist himself accepts whatever happens to him as entirely ordinary. Through a peculiar yet undeniable paradox, the more extraordinary the character's ordeals become, the more vivid that naturalness appears: it springs directly from the gulf between the bizarre absurdity of a man's fate and the unruffled simplicity with which he endures it. This very naturalness is the hallmark of Kafka's art. And that is why we immediately sense what The Trial conveys. Critics have called it an allegory for the universal human condition. Undoubtedly it is. Yet the truth is simultaneously simpler and far more intimate. By this I mean that the novel carries a significance intensely personal to Kafka himself. To a large extent, Kafka is speaking of his own plight, even as he lays bare our own private condition. He exists, and he is already condemned. He discovers this truth on the very first page of the life he pursues in this world, and while he attempts to navigate his sentence, he does so without the slightest shock. One can never marvel enough at this total absence of astonishment. It is through precisely such contradictions that we identify the signature of a genuinely absurd work of art. The intellect projects its internal spiritual tragedy into the tangible world. It accomplishes this through a continuous paradox: granting physical textures the power to evoke cosmic emptiness, and charging everyday habits with the weight of eternal yearnings.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Paradox of Unastonished Naturalness. Kafka's genius lies in characters who accept nightmarish existential condemnation without surprise. The Absurd arises from the friction between monstrous circumstances and the character's everyday composure.

---

### §4 • Original PDF Page 100
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 100):**  
> Likewise, The Castle is perhaps a theology in action, but it is first of all the individual adventure of a soul in quest of its grace, of a man who asks of this world’s objects their royal secret and of women the signs of the god that sleeps in them. Metamorphosis, in turn, certainly represents the horrible imagery of an ethic of lucidity. But it is also the product of that incalculable amazement man feels at being conscious of the beast he becomes effortlessly. In this fundamental ambiguity lies Kafka’s secret. These perpetual oscillations between the natural and the extraordinary, the individual and the universal, the tragic and the everyday, the absurd and the logical, are found throughout his work and give it both its resonance and its meaning. These are the paradoxes that must be enumerated, the contradictions that must be strengthened, in order to understand the absurd work.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
In the same way, The Castle may well be a theology in action, but it is above all the private odyssey of a soul seeking its state of grace—of a man demanding from worldly objects their ultimate secret, and searching in women for the dormant traces of the divine. The Metamorphosis, for its part, portrays the terrifying iconography of an ethic of radical lucidity. Yet it is equally the product of that fathomless shock a human being experiences upon awakening to the beast he can so effortlessly become. Kafka's essential secret resides in this profound ambiguity. These ceaseless oscillations—between the mundane and the extraordinary, the individual and the universal, the tragic and the routine, the absurd and the relentlessly logical—pervade his entire body of work, providing both its emotional depth and philosophical weight. To understand the absurd masterpiece, one must catalogue these paradoxes and sharpen these underlying contradictions.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Aesthetic Oscillation: The Castle as a quest for grace, The Metamorphosis as an ethic of radical lucidity. The absurd artwork does not resolve contradictions; it vibrates between the mundane and the metaphysical, the insect and the human.

---

### §5 • Original PDF Page 101
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 101):**  
> A symbol, indeed, assumes two planes, two worlds of ideas and sensations, and a dictionary of correspondences between them. This lexicon is the hardest thing to draw up. But awaking to the two worlds brought face to face is tantamount to getting on the trail of their secret relationships. In Kafka these two worlds are that of everyday life on the one hand and, on the other, that of supernatural anxiety.[1] It seems that we are witnessing here an interminable exploitation of Nietzsche’s remark: “Great problems are in the street.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
A symbol presupposes two distinct realms—two parallel worlds of thought and sensation—and an exact dictionary of correspondences between them. Compiling such a lexicon is the most difficult task of all. Yet becoming conscious of these two realms brought into direct collision is already to glimpse their hidden subterranean links. In Kafka, these two worlds are the routine reality of daily life on the one side, and the dread of supernatural anxiety on the other.[1] Here we witness an endless, brilliant exploration of Nietzsche's aphorism: “Great problems are in the street.”

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Two Planes Colliding: Everyday routine vs. Supernatural Anxiety. Kafka anchors metaphysical dread directly into street-level reality, demonstrating Nietzsche's principle that existential crises take place within ordinary daily life.

---

### §6 • Original PDF Page 101
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 101):**  
> There is in the human condition (and this is a commonplace of all literatures) a basic absurdity as well as an implacable nobility. The two coincide, as is natural. Both of them are represented, let me repeat, in the ridiculous divorce separating our spiritual excesses and the ephemeral joys of the body. The absurd thing is that it should be the soul of this body which it transcends so inordinately. Whoever would like to represent this absurdity must give it life in a series of parallel contrasts. Thus it is that Kafka expresses tragedy by the everyday and the absurd by the logical.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Within the human condition—as world literature has recognized across centuries—there exists a foundational absurdity alongside an unyielding nobility. Naturally, the two dimensions coincide. Both are embodied, let me emphasize, in the grotesque divorce between our boundless spiritual appetites and the fleeting physical joys of the flesh. The fundamental absurdity is that this mortal body houses an intellect that so wildly transcends its biological limits. Whoever seeks to portray this absurdity in art must animate it through an architecture of sustained contrasts. And so it is that Kafka renders tragedy through the mundane, and articulates the absurd through rigorous logic.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Dual Anatomy of Man: Absurdity and Nobility. The existential rift between the mind's infinite reach and the body's mortal decay. Camus shows that Kafka captures this rift by expressing tragedy through banality and absurdity through relentless logic.

---

### §7 • Original PDF Page 101
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 101):**  
> An actor lends more force to a tragic character the more careful he is not to exaggerate it. If he is moderate, the horror he inspires will be immoderate. In this regard Greek tragedy is rich in lessons. In a tragic work fate always makes itself felt better in the guise of logic and naturalness. Œdipus’s fate is announced in advance. It is decided supernaturally that he will commit the murder and the incest. The drama’s whole effort is to show the logical system which, from deduction to deduction, will crown the hero’s misfortune. Merely to announce to us that uncommon fate is scarcely horrible, because it is improbable. But if its necessity is demonstrated to us in the framework of everyday life, society, state, familiar emotion, then the horror is hallowed. In that revolt that shakes man and makes him say: “That is not possible,” there is an element of desperate certainty that “that” can be.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
An actor breathes greater power into a tragic role the more rigorously he avoids theatrical excess. If his restraint is absolute, the horror he evokes will be overwhelming. On this point, ancient Greek tragedy offers profound instruction. In genuine tragedy, destiny always exerts its greatest power when masked as ordinary logic and inescapable naturalness. The doom of Oedipus is declared from the start. A supernatural decree has ordained that he will commit patricide and incest. The entire momentum of the drama lies in demonstrating the step-by-step logical apparatus that, deduction by deduction, inexorably seals the hero's catastrophe. Simply being told of such an extraordinary doom carries little terror, for it strikes us as improbable. Yet when its necessity is demonstrated within the fabric of daily social life, civic institutions, and familiar human emotions, the horror becomes consecrated. In that shudder of revolt that forces a man to cry, “That cannot be,” lies the desperate, terrifying certainty that it can indeed occur.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Tragic Mechanism: Greek Tragedy & Inescapable Logic. Destiny terrifies not by being fantastical, but by unfolding with mathematical, mundane necessity. Revolt against horror carries the awful recognition that the unthinkable is completely real.

---

### §8 • Original PDF Page 102
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 102):**  
> This is the whole secret of Greek tragedy, or at least of one of its aspects. For there is another which, by a reverse method, would help us to understand Kafka better. The human heart has a tiresome tendency to label as fate only what crushes it. But happiness likewise, in its way, is without reason, since it is inevitable. Modern man, however, takes the credit for it himself, when he doesn’t fail to recognize it. Much could be said, on the contrary, about the privileged fates of Greek tragedy and those favored in legend who, like Ulysses, in the midst of the worst adventures are saved from themselves. It was not so easy to return to Ithaca.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
This constitutes the core secret of Greek tragedy—or at least one of its primary facets. There is another aspect, working in reverse, that illuminates Kafka even more clearly. The human heart possesses an exhausting habit of reserving the label of 'destiny' solely for whatever destroys it. Yet joy, too, is ultimately without transcendent reason, arriving with its own unearned inevitability. Modern man, however, eagerly credits himself for his good fortune—whenever he manages to notice it at all. Much might be observed, by contrast, about those favored figures of Greek myth who, like Odysseus, are preserved from disaster and delivered from their own folly amid the direst perils. After all, reaching Ithaca was no effortless triumph.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Asymmetry of Fate. Humanity attributes catastrophes to 'destiny' while congratulating itself for unearned joy. Camus highlights the Greek understanding of fortunate destinies (Odysseus) to prepare the ground for Kafka's inverted universe.

---

### §9 • Original PDF Page 102
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 102):**  
> What must be remembered in any case is that secret complicity that joins the logical and the everyday to the tragic. This is why Samsa, the hero of Metamorphosis, is a traveling salesman. This is why the only thing that disturbs him in the strange adventure that makes a vermin of him is that his boss will be angry at his absence. Legs and feelers grow out on him, his spine arches up, white spots appear on his belly and—I shall not say that this does not astonish him, for the effect would be spoiled—but it causes him a “slight annoyance.” The whole art of Kafka is in that distinction. In his central work, The Castle, the details of everyday life stand out, and yet in that strange novel in which nothing concludes and everything begins over again, it is the essential adventure of a soul in quest of its grace that is represented. That translation of the problem into action, that coincidence of the general and the particular are recognized likewise in the little artifices that belong to every great creator. In The Trial the hero might have been named Schmidt or Franz Kafka. But he is named Joseph K. He is not Kafka and yet he is Kafka. He is an average European. He is like everybody else. But he is also the entity K. who is the x of this flesh-and-blood equation. Likewise, if Kafka wants to express the absurd, he will make use of consistency. You know the story of the crazy man who was fishing in a bathtub. A doctor with ideas as to psychiatric treatments asked him “if they were biting,” to which he received the harsh reply: “Of course not, you fool, since this is a bathtub.” That story belongs to the baroque type. But in it can be grasped quite clearly to what a degree the absurd effect is linked to an excess of logic. Kafka’s world is in truth an indescribable universe in which man allows himself the tormenting luxury of fishing in a bathtub, knowing that nothing will come of it.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
What remains essential is that covert complicity linking rigorous logic and mundane routine directly to the tragic. This is precisely why Gregor Samsa, the protagonist of The Metamorphosis, is cast as an ordinary traveling salesman. It explains why his sole anxiety upon waking up transformed into a monstrous insect is whether his supervisor will reprimand him for missing his train. Jointed legs and antennae sprout from his torso, his back turns into an armored shell, whitish spots cover his belly, and—I will not say he feels no astonishment, for that would ruin the nuance—the horror registers primarily as an 'annoying inconvenience.' Kafka's entire mastery hinges on that subtle distinction. In his major work, The Castle, the trivial details of daily village life dominate the foreground, yet in that strange book where nothing finishes and every quest resets, what is dramatized is the core metaphysical adventure of a soul seeking its state of grace. This translation of abstract dilemmas into physical action, this fusion of universal myth with concrete detail, reflects the craftsmanship shared by all great literary creators. In The Trial, the protagonist might easily have been named Schmidt or Franz Kafka. Instead, he is designated Joseph K. He is not Kafka, yet he unmistakably is. He represents the average modern European. He lives like everyone else. Yet he is also the algebraic variable K., the unknown 'x' in this flesh-and-blood equation. In the same manner, when Kafka seeks to capture the absurd, he relies upon relentless, hyper-rational consistency. Consider the classic parable of the madman sitting by an empty bathtub with a fishing rod. A psychiatrist seeking to test his sanity asks him gently, “Are they biting?” only to receive the scathing retort: “Of course not, you idiot—this is a bathtub.” The anecdote belongs to the tradition of eccentric satire. Yet it reveals with utmost clarity how the effect of absurdity arises from an excess of logic. Kafka's world is an inexplicable cosmos where human beings grant themselves the agonizing luxury of fishing in an empty bathtub, fully aware that nothing will ever be caught.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Excess of Logic: Bathtub Fishing and Gregor Samsa. Absurdity does not arise from nonsense, but from hyper-rational consistency pushed to lunatic extremes. Samsa's bureaucratic worry about his commute while transformed into vermin exemplifies this fatal divergence.

---

### §10 • Original PDF Page 103
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 103):**  
> Consequently, I recognize here a work that is absurd in its principles. As for The Trial, for instance, I can indeed say that it is a complete success. Flesh wins out. Nothing is lacking, neither the unexpressed revolt (but it is what is writing), nor lucid and mute despair (but it is what is creating), nor that amazing freedom of manner which the characters of the novel exemplify until their ultimate death.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Consequently, I recognize in The Trial a work that is authentically absurd in its foundational principles. It stands as a consummate artistic triumph. The physical reality of the flesh prevails over abstract systems. Nothing essential is absent: neither unspoken revolt (which fuels the very act of writing), nor clear-eyed, silent despair (which drives the act of creation), nor that astonishing lightness of comportment that the novel's characters maintain right up to the moment of their execution.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Trial as Pure Absurd Art. The Trial succeeds completely because it maintains the absurd tension without evasion: physical reality triumphs, silent despair fuels creation, and revolt endures without appealing to celestial rescue.

---

### §11 • Original PDF Page 103
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 103):**  
> Yet this world is not so closed as it seems. Into this universe devoid of progress, Kafka is going to introduce hope in a strange form. In this regard The Trial and The Castle do not follow the same direction. They complement each other. The barely perceptible progression from one to the other represents a tremendous conquest in the realm of evasion. The Trial propounds a problem which The Castle, to a certain degree, solves. The first describes according to a quasi-scientific method and without concluding. The second, to a certain degree, explains. The Trial diagnoses, and The Castle imagines a treatment. But the remedy proposed here does not cure. It merely brings the malady back into normal life. It helps to accept it. In a certain sense (let us think of Kierkegaard), it makes people cherish it. The Land Surveyor K. cannot imagine another anxiety than the one that is tormenting him. The very people around him become attached to that void and that nameless pain, as if suffering assumed in this case a privileged aspect. “How I need you,” Frieda says to K. “How forsaken I feel, since knowing you, when you are not with me.” This subtle remedy that makes us love what crushes us and makes hope spring up in a world without issue, this sudden “leap” through which everything is changed, is the secret of the existential revolution and of The Castle itself.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Yet this literary world is not quite as sealed as it first appears. Into an impasse devoid of any earthly exit, Kafka proceeds to reintroduce hope under a bizarre and unsettling disguise. In this respect, The Trial and The Castle move in opposing directions. They serve as mutual counterparts. The subtle, almost imperceptible shift from the former to the latter represents a monumental victory for the strategy of evasion. The Trial formulates a dilemma that The Castle, to a certain extent, attempts to resolve. The former describes reality with clinical, almost scientific detachment, arriving at no final verdict. The latter, to a degree, provides an explanatory framework. The Trial offers the diagnosis, while The Castle devises a cure. Yet the remedy offered here brings no true healing. It merely assimilates the pathology into everyday life. It trains the patient to accommodate his affliction. In a certain sense—reminiscent of Kierkegaard—it actually teaches people to cherish their torment. K., the Land Surveyor, cannot conceive of any reality beyond the specific obsession devouring him. Even the villagers around him grow emotionally attached to this void and this nameless grief, as though their suffering had taken on an aura of sacred privilege. “How desperately I need you,” Frieda confesses to K. “Ever since I met you, I feel completely abandoned whenever you leave my side.” This insidious remedy that teaches us to adore what crushes us—conjuring hope inside an exitless labyrinth—this sudden 'leap of faith' that transfigures reality is the hidden engine of the existentialist revolution and of The Castle itself.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** From Diagnosis to Evasion: The Castle's Leap of Faith. The Trial was pure absurd diagnosis; The Castle attempts a cure. Camus reveals how Kafka introduces existential evasion: teaching the sufferer to love what crushes him, transforming torment into divine grace.

---

### §12 • Original PDF Page 104
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 104):**  
> Few works are more rigorous in their development than The Castle. K. is named Land Surveyor to the Castle and he arrives in the village. But from the village to the Castle it is impossible to communicate. For hundreds of pages K. persists in seeking his way, makes every advance, uses trickery and expedients, never gets angry, and with disconcerting good will tries to assume the duties entrusted to him. Each chapter is a new frustration. And also a new beginning. It is not logic, but consistent method. The scope of that insistence constitutes the work’s tragic quality. When K. telephones to the Castle, he hears confused, mingled voices, vague laughs, distant invitations. That is enough to feed his hope, like those few signs appearing in summer skies or those evening anticipations which make up our reason for living. Here is found the secret of the melancholy peculiar to Kafka. The same, in truth, that is found in Proust’s work or in the landscape of Plotinus: a nostalgia for a lost paradise. “I become very sad,” says Olga, “when Barnabas tells me in the morning that he is going to the Castle: that probably futile trip, that probably wasted day, that probably empty hope.” “Probably”—on this implication Kafka gambles his entire work. But nothing avails; the quest of the eternal here is meticulous. And those inspired automata, Kafka’s characters, provide us with a precise image of what we should be if we were deprived of our distractions[2] and utterly consigned to the humiliations of the divine.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Few narratives unfold with more meticulous rigor than The Castle. Appointed Land Surveyor to the Castle, K. arrives at the village. Yet between the village below and the Castle above, direct communication is impossible. Across hundreds of pages, K. relentlessly searches for a path, pursues every opening, employs petty tricks and improvisations, never loses his temper, and strives with bewildering earnestness to take up the post assigned to him. Every chapter brings another thwarting. Yet every setback marks an identical fresh start. This is not ordinary logic, but an unyielding methodical obsession. The sheer stamina of his persistence produces the tragic texture of the work. When K. picks up the receiver to telephone the Castle, he hears only an indistinct hum of overlapping murmurs, faint laughter, and elusive invitations. That faint static is sufficient to sustain his hope, like those subtle shifts in a summer sky or those evening intuitions that make up our daily reasons for living. Here lies the source of Kafka's unique, piercing melancholy. It is the very same melancholy found across Proust's prose or Plotinus's metaphysics: an incurable nostalgia for an Eden forever lost. “It breaks my heart,” Olga confides, “whenever Barnabas announces in the morning that he is setting out for the Castle: another likely futile journey, another squandered day, another hollow hope.” “Likely”—upon this single sliver of possibility Kafka stakes the fate of his entire book. Yet all efforts prove futile; the pursuit of the transcendent here is maddeningly bureaucratic and exhaustive. And these possessed automata—Kafka's driven characters—present an exact portrait of what human beings would resemble if stripped of our daily Pascalian distractions[2] and surrendered wholly to the humiliations of the divine.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Bureaucracy of Eternity: The Castle and Pascalian Distraction. K.'s telephone to the Castle captures the tragic melancholy of human longing: seizing upon garbled noise as divine revelation. Kafka's characters show man stripped of worldly distractions, consumed by the quest for divine grace.

---

### §13 • Original PDF Page 104
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 104):**  
> In The Castle that surrender to the everyday becomes an ethic. The great hope of K. is to get the Castle to adopt him. Unable to achieve this alone, his whole effort is to deserve this favor by becoming an inhabitant of the village, by losing the status of foreigner that everyone makes him feel. What he wants is an occupation, a home, the life of a healthy, normal man. He can’t stand his madness any longer. He wants to be reasonable. He wants to cast off the peculiar curse that makes him a stranger to the village. The episode of Frieda is significant in this regard. If he takes as his mistress this woman who has known one of the Castle’s officials, this is because of her past. He derives from her something that transcends him—while being aware of what makes her forever unworthy of the Castle. This makes one think of Kierkegaard’s strange love for Regina Olsen. In certain men, the fire of eternity consuming them is great enough for them to burn in it the very heart of those closest to them. The fatal mistake that consists in giving to God what is not God’s is likewise the subject of this episode of The Castle. But for Kafka it seems that this is not a mistake. It is a doctrine and a “leap.” There is nothing that is not God’s.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
In The Castle, this submission to everyday routine hardens into a moral ethic. K.'s supreme ambition is to be formally accepted and adopted by the Castle. Powerless to achieve this on his own, his entire campaign is geared toward earning this privilege by becoming an ordinary villager—erasing the status of outsider that everyone forces upon him. What he craves is regular employment, a domestic hearth, the unexamined life of a healthy, unremarkable man. He can no longer endure the torment of his existential estrangement. He longs to conform to reason. He yearns to throw off the singular curse that marks him as an alien among the villagers. His liaison with Frieda is profoundly revealing in this context. If he takes as his lover a woman who previously belonged to an official of the Castle, he does so specifically on account of that sacred connection. He draws from her an aura of the transcendent, even while remaining acutely aware of the degradation that renders her forever unworthy of the Castle. This dynamic immediately recalls Kierkegaard's tormenting devotion to Regine Olsen. In certain individuals, the hunger for eternity burns with such intensity that they gladly consume the hearts of those closest to them in its flames. The fatal error of rendering unto God what belongs purely to the world forms the true subject of this episode in The Castle. Yet for Kafka, this surrender is treated not as an error, but as an article of faith. It constitutes a deliberate dogma and an existential leap. In his theology, nothing exists that does not belong to God.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Surrender to the Everyday as Spiritual Abdication. K. longs to renounce his lucid estrangement and melt into unremarkable normalcy. Camus diagnoses K.'s exploitation of Frieda (and its Kierkegaardian echo) as an existential leap: consecrating the mundane as divine.

---

### §14 • Original PDF Page 105
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 105):**  
> Even more significant is the fact that the Land Surveyor breaks with Frieda in order to go toward the Barnabas sisters. For the Barnabas family is the only one in the village that is utterly forsaken by the Castle and by the village itself. Amalia, the elder sister, has rejected the shameful propositions made her by one of the Castle’s officials. The immoral curse that followed has forever cast her out from the love of God. Being incapable of losing one’s honor for God amounts to making oneself unworthy of his grace. You recognize a theme familiar to existential philosophy: truth contrary to morality. At this point things are far-reaching. For the path pursued by Kafka’s hero from Frieda to the Barnabas sisters is the very one that leads from trusting love to the deification of the absurd. Here again Kafka’s thought runs parallel to Kierkegaard. It is not surprising that the “Barnabas story” is placed at the end of the book. The Land Surveyor’s last attempt is to recapture God through what negates him, to recognize him, not according to our categories of goodness and beauty, but behind the empty and hideous aspects of his indifference, of his injustice, and of his hatred. That stranger who asks the Castle to adopt him is at the end of his voyage a little more exiled because this time he is unfaithful to himself, forsaking morality, logic, and intellectual truths in order to try to enter, endowed solely with his mad hope, the desert of divine grace.[3]

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Still more decisive is K.'s decision to abandon Frieda in order to align himself with the Barnabas sisters. For the Barnabas household is the solitary family in the village completely ostracized by both the Castle and the local community. Amalia, the older sister, had refused the degrading sexual demands issued to her by a Castle official. The perverse curse that ensued cast her out permanently from divine favor. In this theological economy, refusing to sacrifice one's moral integrity for God renders one unworthy of His grace. One recognizes here the signature doctrine of existentialist philosophy: the teleological suspension of the ethical—truth standing in direct opposition to human morality. The philosophical implications here are immense. For the trajectory traced by Kafka's protagonist from Frieda to the Barnabas sisters is precisely the passage from worldly affection to the deification of the absurd. Once again Kafka's logic mirrors Kierkegaard's Abraham. It is hardly accidental that the narrative of the Barnabas family appears toward the climax of the book. K.'s ultimate gambit is to recover God through whatever denies Him—to worship divinity not through human categories of benevolence or beauty, but hidden behind the hideous masks of divine indifference, arbitrary injustice, and cruelty. That wandering stranger begging the Castle for admission ends his quest more profoundly exiled than ever, because he has committed treason against his own consciousness: abandoning reason, logic, and human ethics to wander, armed solely with irrational hope, into the wasteland of divine grace.[3]

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Deification of the Absurd: Amalia & the Theological Leap. The turning point of Kafka's thought. Amalia retains human moral dignity and is cursed; K. seeks God by worshiping the very injustice and indifference that crushes him. To Camus, this is the ultimate intellectual surrender.

---

### §15 • Original PDF Page 105
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 105):**  
> The word “hope” used here is not ridiculous. On the contrary, the more tragic the condition described by Kafka, the firmer and more aggressive that hope becomes. The more truly absurd The Trial is, the more moving and illegitimate the impassioned “leap” of The Castle seems. But we find here again in a pure state the paradox of existential thought as it is expressed, for instance, by Kierkegaard: “Earthly hope must be killed; only then can one be saved by true hope,”[4] which can be translated: “One has to have written The Trial to undertake The Castle.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
My use of the term 'hope' here is by no means ironic or misplaced. On the contrary: the bleaker and more desperate the condition Kafka portrays, the more stubborn and aggressive that hope grows. The more authentic the absurd rigor of The Trial, the more poignant yet illegitimate appears the impassioned leap of faith in The Castle. Here we confront once again the central paradox of existentialist theology, expressed so memorably by Kierkegaard: “Earthly hope must first be put to death; only then can one be saved by true hope”[4]—an aphorism that translates directly into literary terms: “One had to write The Trial in order to embark upon The Castle.”

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Kierkegaardian Symmetry: Killing Earthly Hope. Camus articulates the existentialist paradox: total despair of worldly solutions is leveraged to justify a supernatural leap. The Trial dismantles mortal illusions so that The Castle can construct a theological sanctuary.

---

### §16 • Original PDF Page 106
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 106):**  
> Most of those who have spoken of Kafka have indeed defined his work as a desperate cry with no recourse left to man. But this calls for review. There is hope and hope. To me the optimistic work of Henri Bordeaux seems peculiarly discouraging. This is because it has nothing for the discriminating. Malraux’s thought, on the other hand, is always bracing. But in these two cases neither the same hope nor the same despair is at issue. I see merely that the absurd work itself may lead to the infidelity I want to avoid. The work which was but an ineffectual repetition of a sterile condition, a lucid glorification of the ephemeral, becomes here a cradle of illusions. It explains, it gives a shape to hope. The creator can no longer divorce himself from it. It is not the tragic game it was to be. It gives a meaning to the author’s life.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Most critics who have interpreted Kafka characterize his oeuvre as a cry of total despair, leaving humanity without any recourse. Yet this assessment demands critical reconsideration. There are different varieties of hope. To my mind, the superficial optimism of a moralist like Henri Bordeaux is profoundly disheartening. It offers nothing of substance to a rigorous, discriminating mind. The heroic literature of André Malraux, by contrast, is always invigorating. Yet between those two writers, we are dealing with fundamentally different definitions of hope and despair. My concern is simply that the absurd artwork itself can slide into the very betrayal I am determined to avoid. A work that ought to have remained the lucid testimony of a sterile human condition—a clear-eyed celebration of the ephemeral—becomes transformed into a nursery of comforting illusions. It begins to explain the inexplicable; it gives tangible contour to metaphysical hope. The creator can no longer maintain an ironic distance from his creation. The artwork ceases to be the heroic, disinterested game it was intended to remain. Instead, it ends by providing an artificial meaning for the author's own life.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Art as Cradle of Illusions: When Absurd Art Betrays Itself. Absurd art must remain a disinterested game and a description of the ephemeral. When the creator uses his art to provide ultimate answers or cosmic meaning, it ceases to be absurd and becomes an instrument of evasion.

---

### §17 • Original PDF Page 106
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 106):**  
> It is strange in any case that works of related inspiration like those of Kafka, Kierkegaard, or Chestov—those, in short, of existential novelists and philosophers completely oriented toward the Absurd and its consequences—should in the long run lead to that tremendous cry of hope.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
In any case, it remains astonishing that works sharing this common lineage—those of Kafka, Kierkegaard, or Shestov, thinkers and novelists entirely focused on the Absurd and its radical consequences—should ultimately culminate in such a resounding proclamation of supernatural hope.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Existentialist Paradox of Hope. The irony of the existentialist tradition: thinkers who begin with the most unsparing analysis of human absurdity end by resurrecting God and finding solace in transcendent hope.

---

### §18 • Original PDF Page 106
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 106):**  
> They embrace the God that consumes them. It is through humility that hope enters in. For the absurd of this existence assures them a little more of supernatural reality. If the course of this life leads to God, there is an outcome after all. And the perseverance, the insistence with which Kierkegaard, Chestov, and Kafka’s heroes repeat their itineraries are a special warrant of the uplifting power of that certainty.[5]

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
They throw themselves into the arms of the very God that consumes them. It is through absolute self-abasement that hope finds its opening. For in their eyes, the sheer absurdity of mortal existence serves as proof of a transcendent realm beyond it. If the agonizing trajectory of this life leads back to the divine, then existence possesses a final purpose after all. And the relentless tenacity with which Kierkegaard, Shestov, and Kafka's protagonists retrace their spiritual paths stands as testimony to the intoxicating power of that supernatural conviction.[5]

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Embracing the God That Consumes: Humility as Evasion. Absurdity turned into theology. Because life makes no earthly sense, existentialists deduce that it must make divine sense. The absurdity of the world is converted into evidence for God.

---

### §19 • Original PDF Page 106
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 106):**  
> Kafka refuses his god moral nobility, evidence, virtue, coherence, but only the better to fall into his arms. The absurd is recognized, accepted, and man is resigned to it, but from then on we know that it has ceased to be the absurd. Within the limits of the human condition, what greater hope than the hope that allows an escape from that condition? As I see once more, existential thought in this regard (and contrary to current opinion) is steeped in a vast hope. The very hope which at the time of early Christianity and the spreading of the good news inflamed the ancient world. But in that leap that characterizes all existential thought, in that insistence, in that surveying of a divinity devoid of surface, how can one fail to see the mark of a lucidity that repudiates itself? It is merely claimed that this is pride abdicating to save itself. Such a repudiation would be fecund. But this does not change that. The moral value of lucidity cannot be diminished in my eyes by calling it sterile like all pride. For a truth also, by its very definition, is sterile. All facts are. In a world where everything is given and nothing is explained, the fecundity of a value or of a metaphysic is a notion devoid of meaning.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Kafka strips his deity of moral nobility, self-evidence, benevolence, and coherence, but he does so only to surrender more abjectly into His embrace. The Absurd is detected, acknowledged, and endured, yet from that very instant we recognize that it has ceased to be the Absurd. Within the claustrophobia of mortal existence, what hope could be more seductive than one promising deliverance from the human condition itself? As becomes clear once more, existentialist philosophy—contrary to popular impression—is drenched in boundless metaphysical hope. It is the selfsame hope that set the ancient Greco-Roman world ablaze during the dawn of Christianity with the proclamation of the Gospel. Yet in that desperate leap defining all existential thought—in that relentless examination of an unfathomable, faceless deity—how can one fail to detect the tragic spectacle of an intellect renouncing itself? Apologists argue that this is merely intellectual pride abdicating in order to achieve salvation. They maintain that such self-renunciation is spiritually fruitful. Yet one cannot escape reality through wordplay. The moral dignity of clear-sighted lucidity cannot be compromised in my eyes by dismissing it as sterile pride. For empirical truth is, by its very nature, fundamentally sterile. All irreducible facts are. In a cosmos where everything is physically given but nothing is cosmically explained, measuring the validity of an idea by its emotional 'fruitfulness' is a concept devoid of meaning.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Sterile Truth vs. Fertile Illusions: The Suicide of Intellect. Camus rejects the pragmatist/existentialist defense of religious faith (that hope is 'fruitful'). Truth does not cater to human comfort; genuine lucidity remains proudly sterile rather than capitulating to fertile delusions.

---

### §20 • Original PDF Page 107
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 107):**  
> In any case, you see here in what tradition of thought Kafka’s work takes its place. It would indeed be intelligent to consider as inevitable the progression leading from The Trial to The Castle. Joseph K. and the Land Surveyor K. are merely two poles that attract Kafka.[6] I shall speak like him and say that his work is probably not absurd. But that should not deter us from seeing its nobility and universality. They come from the fact that he managed to represent so fully the everyday passage from hope to grief and from desperate wisdom to intentional blindness. His work is universal (a really absurd work is not universal) to the extent to which it represents the emotionally moving face of man fleeing humanity, deriving from his contradictions reasons for believing, reasons for hoping from his fecund despairs, and calling life his terrifying apprenticeship in death. It is universal because its inspiration is religious. As in all religions, man is freed of the weight of his own life. But if I know that, if I can even admire it, I also know that I am not seeking what is universal, but what is true. The two may well not coincide.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
In any event, one sees clearly which intellectual lineage claims Kafka's masterpiece. It is entirely plausible to view the trajectory leading from The Trial to The Castle as inevitable. Joseph K. and K. the Land Surveyor represent two magnetic poles that pull at Kafka's sensibility.[6] Adopting his own probabilistic phrasing, I would say that his work is ultimately not absurd. Yet that conclusion in no way diminishes its extraordinary stature or universal power. Its greatness springs from having depicted with such unflinching precision the daily descent from hope to heartbreak, and the regression from clear-eyed insight into willful blindness. His fiction achieves universality—a quality a purely absurd work does not possess—precisely because it captures the moving, tragic spectacle of a human being fleeing his own mortal limits, converting logical contradictions into grounds for faith, harvesting hope from fertile despair, and treating life as a terrifying apprenticeship for eternity. It is universal because its ultimate impulse is religious. As in every religious system, man is relieved of the crushing weight of his sovereign mortal freedom. Yet while I comprehend this impulse and can admire its literary artistry, I also know that my own quest is not for what is comforting and universal, but for what is true. And truth and universality do not necessarily coincide.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Universality vs. Truth: Kafka's Religious Trajectory. Kafka's universality is religious: it captures man's universal temptation to flee mortality into divine solace. Camus respects Kafka's artistry but breaks with him philosophically: truth does not compromise with consolation.

---

### §21 • Original PDF Page 107
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 107):**  
> This particular view will be better understood if I say that truly hopeless thought just happens to be defined by the opposite criteria and that the tragic work might be the work that, after all future hope is exiled, describes the life of a happy man. The more exciting life is, the more absurd is the idea of losing it. This is perhaps the secret of that proud aridity felt in Nietzsche’s work. In this connection, Nietzsche appears to be the only artist to have derived the extreme consequences of an æsthetic of the Absurd, inasmuch as his final message lies in a sterile and conquering lucidity and an obstinate negation of any supernatural consolation.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
This distinction becomes clearer if I state that authentic, uncompromising thought is defined by the inverse criteria: true tragic literature is that which, having banished every future hope, celebrates the life of a happy, mortal human being. The more passionate and radiant existence is, the more absurd appears the certainty of losing it. Herein lies the secret of that proud, unyielding aridity that runs through the philosophy of Nietzsche. In this regard, Nietzsche stands as perhaps the sole thinker to have pursued the aesthetic of the Absurd to its ultimate limits—anchoring his legacy in a sterile, triumphant lucidity and an obstinate refusal of all supernatural solace.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Tragic Joy and Nietzsche's Proud Aridity. The true absurd artist (Nietzsche) rejects supernatural comfort and embraces mortal passion. Tragic art portrays a happy man who loves life precisely because death is absolute.

---

### §22 • Original PDF Page 108
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 108):**  
> The preceding should nevertheless suffice to bring out the capital importance of Kafka in the framework of this essay. Here we are carried to the confines of human thought. In the fullest sense of the word, it can be said that everything in that work is essential. In any case, it propounds the absurd problem altogether. If one wants to compare these conclusions with our initial remarks, the content with the form, the secret meaning of The Castle with the natural art in which it is molded, K.’s passionate, proud quest with the everyday setting against which it takes place, then one will realize what may be its greatness. For if nostalgia is the mark of the human, perhaps no one has given such flesh and volume to these phantoms of regret. But at the same time will be sensed what exceptional nobility the absurd work calls for, which is perhaps not found here. If the nature of art is to bind the general to the particular, ephemeral eternity of a drop of water to the play of its lights, it is even truer to judge the greatness of the absurd writer by the distance he is able to introduce between these two worlds. His secret consists in being able to find the exact point where they meet in their greatest disproportion.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
What has been said should suffice to underscore the paramount significance of Kafka within the inquiry of this book. His fiction carries us to the absolute frontiers of human consciousness. In the truest sense, nothing in his writing is superfluous; every line is essential. He articulates the crisis of the Absurd in its total scope. If we contrast these final insights with our opening observations—weighing content against style, the metaphysical yearning of The Castle against the unadorned realism of its prose, K.'s stubborn spiritual quest against the mundane village against which it unfolds—we begin to measure the true magnitude of his achievement. For if metaphysical nostalgia remains the defining signature of the human spirit, perhaps no author has endowed these phantoms of longing with such tangible flesh and breathing volume. Yet simultaneously, we discern the extraordinary, austere nobility demanded by authentic absurd art—a heroic detachment that is ultimately missing here. If the task of art is to unite the universal with the specific—capturing the ephemeral eternity of a water droplet through the shimmering refraction of light—the greatness of the absurd artist is measured by the immense distance he maintains between human longing and cosmic indifference. His mastery lies in locating the precise knife-edge where these two realms collide in their absolute disproportion.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Measure of Absurd Greatness: Maximum Disproportion. Camus pays tribute to Kafka's unmatched power in giving flesh to metaphysical nostalgia, while re-asserting his aesthetic criterion: absurd art must sustain the violent disproportion between mortal hunger and cosmic silence without reconciling them.

---

### §23 • Original PDF Page 108
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 108):**  
> And, to tell the truth, this geometrical locus of man and the inhuman is seen everywhere by the pure in heart. If Faust and Don Quixote are eminent creations of art, this is because of the immeasurable nobilities they point out to us with their earthly hands. Yet a moment always comes when the mind negates the truths that those hands can touch. A moment comes when the creation ceases to be taken tragically; it is merely taken seriously. Then man is concerned with hope. But that is not his business. His business is to turn away from subterfuge. Yet this is just what I find at the conclusion of the vehement proceedings Kafka institutes against the whole universe. His unbelievable verdict is this hideous and upsetting world in which the very moles dare to hope.[7]

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
Truth be told, this exact intersection between man and the inhuman world is perceived everywhere by the pure in heart. If Faust and Don Quixote stand as monumental triumphs of literature, it is because of the boundless human dignity they gesture toward with mortal, earthly hands. Yet a fatal temptation always arrives when the intellect betrays the tangible realities those hands can touch. A moment comes when the human drama ceases to be lived tragically; it is merely treated with solemn religious earnestness. At that point, humanity becomes obsessed with salvation and hope. Yet hope is not our true calling. Our authentic responsibility is to refuse every comforting subterfuge and look reality in the face. Yet false hope is precisely what emerges at the conclusion of the ferocious trial Kafka brings against the cosmos. His astonishing verdict culminates in this grotesque and disorienting universe where even the blind moles burrowing underground dare to hope.[7]

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** The Final Verdict: Even the Moles Dare to Hope. The climactic summation. True human dignity lies in refusing metaphysical subterfuge. Kafka's cosmic lawsuit ends not in heroic defiance, but in an exhausting world where even the blindest creatures cling to irrational hope.

---

### §24 • Original PDF Page 109
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 109):**  
> 1 It is worth noting that the works of Kafka can quite as legitimately be interpreted in the sense of a social criticism (for instance in The Trial). It is probable, moreover, that there is no need to choose. Both interpretations are good. In absurd terms, as we have seen, revolt against men is also directed against God: great revolutions are always metaphysical.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
1. It is worth emphasizing that Kafka's fiction can just as validly be understood as social and political critique—most visibly in The Trial. Furthermore, one likely does not need to choose between the political and the spiritual readings. Both perspectives are entirely sound. Within the logic of the Absurd, rebellion against human tyrants is simultaneously a revolt against God: all truly profound revolutions are metaphysical at their core.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Footnote 1: Social Critique vs. Metaphysical Revolt. Camus harmonizes political and theological readings of Kafka: confronting bureaucratic tyranny is inseparable from confronting divine silence. Every authentic revolution challenges the cosmic order itself.

---

### §25 • Original PDF Page 109
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 109):**  
> 2 In The Castle it seems that “distractions” in the Pascalian sense are represented by the assistants who “distract” K. from his anxiety. If Frieda eventually becomes the mistress of one of the assistants, this is because she prefers the stage setting to truth, everyday life to shared anguish.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
2. In The Castle, Pascalian 'distractions' appear to be personified by the buffoonish assistants, whose antics divert K. from his existential dread. If Frieda ultimately abandons K. to become the mistress of one of these assistants, it is because she chooses theatrical illusion over reality, preferring comfortable domestic routine to shared metaphysical anguish.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Footnote 2: Pascalian Diversion in the Assistants. The comic assistants function as Pascal's divertissement—clowning buffers that numb existential dread. Frieda's betrayal reflects humanity's retreat into comforting domesticity.

---

### §26 • Original PDF Page 109
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 109):**  
> 3 This is obviously true only of the unfinished version of The Castle that Kafka left us. But it is doubtful that the writer would have destroyed in the last chapters his novel’s unity of tone.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
3. This critique naturally applies strictly to the unfinished manuscript of The Castle as Kafka left it. Yet it is highly improbable that the author would have shattered the tonal and philosophical unity of his masterpiece in its final, unwritten chapters.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Footnote 3: Integrity of the Unfinished Manuscript. Camus defends his reading of The Castle despite its unfinished state: Kafka's tonal and structural unity indicates that the theological reconciliation was fully intentional.

---

### §27 • Original PDF Page 109
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 109):**  
> 4 Purity of heart.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
4. A reference to Søren Kierkegaard's Purity of Heart Is to Will One Thing.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Footnote 4: Kierkegaard's Purity of Heart. Camus links Kafka's single-minded quest to Kierkegaard's spiritual demand for total commitment, which requires sacrificing earthly happiness for divine communion.

---

### §28 • Original PDF Page 109
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 109):**  
> 5 The only character without hope in The Castle is Amalia. She is the one with whom the Land Surveyor is most violently contrasted.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
5. The sole character in The Castle entirely devoid of hope is Amalia. She stands as the absolute, uncompromising foil to the Land Surveyor K.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Footnote 5: Amalia as the True Absurd Heroine. Amalia alone refuses to compromise with divine tyranny or indulge in false hope. Her uncompromising ostracism makes her the heroic absurd counterweight to K.'s servile yearning.

---

### §29 • Original PDF Page 109
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 109):**  
> 6 On the two aspects of Kafka’s thought, compare “In the Penal Colony,” published by the Cahiers du Sud (and in America by Partisan Review—translator’s note): “Guilt [‘of man’ is understood] is never doubtful” and a fragment of The Castle (Momus’s report): “The guilt of the Land Surveyor K. is hard to establish.”

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
6. Regarding the dual facets of Kafka's worldview, compare In the Penal Colony—where the officer declares that human guilt is never in doubt—with the bureaucratic fragment in The Castle where Momus notes that the guilt of Land Surveyor K. is exceedingly difficult to establish.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Footnote 6: Guilt in Penal Colony vs. The Castle. Tracing Kafka's dialectic: in the early work, universal guilt is absolute and self-evident; in The Castle, guilt becomes an elusive, bureaucratic enigma that can never be verified.

---

### §30 • Original PDF Page 109
> **Original 1955 Translation (Page 109):**  
> 7 What is offered above is obviously an interpretation of Kafka’s work. But it is only fair to add that nothing prevents its being considered, aside from any interpretation, from a purely æsthetic point of view. For instance, B. Groethuysen in his remarkable preface to The Trial limits himself, more wisely than we, to following merely the painful fancies of what he calls, most strikingly, a daydreamer. It is the fate and perhaps the greatness of that work that it offers everything and confirms nothing.

**Dignified Modern Translation:**  
7. What has been presented above is admittedly an existential interpretation of Kafka's literature. Yet it is only fair to recognize that nothing prevents his art from being appreciated, free of any philosophical reading, from a purely aesthetic perspective. For example, Bernard Groethuysen, in his insightful preface to The Trial, wisely restricts himself to tracing the agonizing reveries of what he memorably describes as an awakened daydreamer. It is the enduring destiny—and perhaps the supreme greatness—of Kafka's work that it offers everything to the human imagination while confirming absolutely nothing.

> [!TIP]
> **The Philosophical Move:** Footnote 7: Aesthetic Pluralism and Open Art. Camus concludes with critical modesty: Kafka's art defies final dogmatic capture. Its supreme triumph is that it evokes every question of human destiny while refusing to confirm any metaphysical answer.
